- **Alternatives rejected:** opening the picker even when `traceStart` exists (adds a keystroke to the demo's lead moment); fuzzy matching (M4b.7's palette brings it; whole-word containment is predictable for paths); a separate picker component with its own styles (two dialogs that would drift); a dwell of one pulse (900ms is too short to read a step's title and code); a bigger particle as the pulse (particles already flow on every path edge, so a third kind of dot wouldn't read as "here").
- **Consequences:** the website's diagram page gets a working trace from T with no selection. `CommandPalette` focuses its field when `autoFocus` is set and keeps Tab in it, which M4b.7 builds on. One new story (Explorer/Trace picker) and two new interface icons (play, pause) change the Controls baseline.

## ADR-0119: The command palette ranks matches by tiers, searches every node, and a pick zooms in to read it
- **Context:** M4b.7. UX.md "Search: command palette": Ctrl+K or ⌘K, a fuzzy match over services, modules, files, symbols, routes, pages, models, and externals, results with kind and path, Enter "flies to the result, drilling as needed, and selects it". The M4a review asked for focus moved into and kept in the dialog.
- **Decision:**
  - **Matching** (`explorer/search.tsx`, `searchNodes`): every word of the query must match the name or, failing that, the path (a service's root, a folder's or file's path, an outside API's host, else the source file). In the name a word scores by tier: the whole name (100), its start (80), a word start (after a non-letter-or-digit or a camelCase step: 60), anywhere (40), or its letters in order with gaps (20 minus the letters skipped, at least 1; two letters or more). A path-only word scores 10. Ties go by kind (services, pages, routes, models, folders, files, symbols, then infrastructure and unresolved items), then the shorter name, then `compareStrings` on name and ID, so the list is the same in any input order. At most 50 results; an empty query lists nothing and says what to type. Every node but the system is searchable, including datastores, queues, and unresolved calls (fail visibly: they're findable too).
  - **Keys and focus:** Ctrl+K or ⌘K (no Shift or Alt) toggles it from anywhere in the explorer, and from the page itself when nothing has focus (a window listener that ignores keys aimed at other fields); the top bar's search field opens it too. Tab stays in the field, and a press anywhere in the dialog but the field doesn't take focus (`mousedown` default prevented), so arrows, Enter, and Esc keep working. Esc and a scrim click close it with focus back where it was. A pick closes it, drills (`drill.reveal`), selects, focuses the picked box, and flies there with `zoomToBox`: centered at 100% (less if the box wouldn't fit), never zooming out, and only panning when already that close. The inspector's connections keep `ensureVisible`, since the reader is already looking at that level.
  - **Esc from a dialog's field** is handled once: the explorer ignores a key whose default the dialog prevented. Before this, the trace picker's Esc ran its close twice and the second blurred the control focus had just returned to.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a fuzzy library (fzf-style scoring is a dependency and harder to keep deterministic and explainable); a typo-tolerant edit distance (noisy on short code names); listing everything on an empty query (thousands of rows on a real repo); keeping the fitted camera on a pick (at 40% a function's name isn't drawn, so "flies to" landed on an unreadable box); ending a trace on a pick (drilling keeps the trace, UX.md; the pick is marked on the canvas).
- **Consequences:** the website gets search as soon as it shows the explorer (Ctrl+K works without the explorer's top bar). Not handled: typos, and a word matched across the name and the path. One new story (Explorer/Search).

## ADR-0120: Filters hide nodes with what's inside them, drop (not re-route) their connections, and find tests and config by name
- **Context:** M4b.8a. SPEC §3 asks for filters "by layer, kind, and confidence, plus a toggle to hide tests and config"; UX.md adds a folder include/exclude, with tests and config hidden by default. The graph has no "test" or "config" flag (the analyzer parses every source file, test files included), and a hidden node's connections need a rule: the view model otherwise lifts an edge to the nearest visible ancestor.
- **Decision:** `model/filters.ts`, applied by `buildView`'s new `filters` option.
  - **Rules:** a node is hidden when its own layer or kind is off, when its repo path (a service's root, a folder's or file's path, an unresolved call's source file) is a test or config path, in an excluded folder, or, with folders included, neither in one nor (for a service or folder) above one. Everything inside a hidden node is hidden; so is a folder whose contents all are. Files stay even when emptied (hiding symbols keeps the file boxes). Datastores, queues, and outside APIs have no path, so folders don't touch them. The kind filter offers routes, pages, symbols, models, datastores, queues, outside APIs, and unresolved calls; services, folders, and files are structure and go by layer or folder. Edge kinds stay with `hiddenKinds` (imports off by default).
  - **Connections:** an edge with a hidden end, or of a hidden confidence, is dropped before lifting. Redrawing it from the hidden node's visible parent would say the parent does it, which the filter's reader asked not to see.
  - **Tests and config by name** (`isTestOrConfig`): `*.test.*`, `*.spec.*`, `*.e2e.*`, `*.config.{js,ts,…}`, `.*rc.js`, Jest/Vitest/Playwright setup files, `setupTests.*`, and folders named `__tests__`, `__mocks__`, `__fixtures__`, `test`, `tests`, `e2e`, `cypress` (so a test workspace goes too). Not stories, `fixtures/`, `.d.ts`, or framework files like `middleware.ts`.
  - **Cache key:** `filtersKey` is the canonical JSON of the sorted, deduplicated filters ("" when none apply), stored on the view as `filtered` and added to `layoutKey`, so equal filters set in any order share a layout. `buildView` without `filters` is unchanged (`NO_FILTERS`), so the README image, which shows everything, keeps its bytes; the explorer will start from `DEFAULT_FILTERS` (tests and config hidden).
- **Alternatives rejected:** an analyzer flag for test files (a schema change and eval re-record for what is a naming convention the viewer can read from paths); lifting a hidden node's edges to its parent (misattributes them); dimming instead of hiding (the canvas's dim state already means "off the trace"); glob patterns for folders (a prefix by whole segments is what "include this folder" means, and needs no glob engine).
- **Consequences:** the explorer can show "N filters" and a chip from `activeFilterCount`. The popover, chip, and explorer wiring (selection, trace, and search over hidden nodes) are M4b.8b. Not handled: test files named by other conventions (`*_test.ts`, `testUtils.ts`); they stay visible.

## ADR-0121: The filters popover offers only what the graph has; the chip shows only when something is hidden; going to a hidden node takes off just the filters hiding it
- **Context:** M4b.8b. UX.md "Filters": a popover from the top bar with toggles by layer, kind, and confidence, a folder include/exclude, and "Hide tests and config" on by default; active filters as a chip with "Clear". The explorer starts from `DEFAULT_FILTERS` (ADR-0120), the website hides the explorer's top bar, and search, the trace picker, the inspector's connections, and a trace's steps can all lead to a node the filters hide.
- **Decision:**
  - **Popover** (`chrome/FiltersPopover.tsx`, non-modal): the tests switch first (it takes focus on open), then pill choices for layers, node kinds, connection kinds (imports included, off by default), and confidence, each pressed while what it names is shown and counted from the graph (`filterOptions`); a choice the graph has none of isn't offered. Off pills are muted with a dashed edge, so "hidden" doesn't rest on color. The folder field suggests the graph's service roots and folders and refuses any other path with a message ("No folder x in this repo") instead of a filter that hides nothing; Enter hides, "Show only" includes; folders in use are listed, each removable. Esc or the close button closes it with focus back on its opener; a press outside closes it and leaves focus where the press put it. From the top bar it opens under the zoom controls; from the canvas under the chip.
  - **Count and chip:** the Filters button counts the settings that are on (`activeFilterCount`, plus connection kinds hidden beyond the default), so the shop demo reads "Filters 1" (tests hidden). The canvas chip ("2 filters · 7 hidden", or "· N connections hidden" when only lines go) shows only when the filters actually hide something in this graph, so a repo without tests isn't told about them on its hero canvas. Its text opens the popover; its × is Clear. Clear sets `NO_FILTERS` and keeps the connection kinds hidden by default as they are: imports are a level of detail, not a filter, and a user who turned them on keeps them. Without the top bar (the website), a quiet Filters button takes the chip's place when nothing is hidden.
  - **Going to a hidden node:** a search pick, a trace-picker pick, a connection in the inspector, "Trace from here", and a step's **Show** call `filtersShowing`, which removes only the rules that hit the node or what holds it (their layers and kinds, the tests switch if one of their paths is a test or config one, the excluded folders holding them; with folders included, the node's nearest path is added), so the other filters stay. A folder still hidden because everything inside it is gets the first single whole rule whose removal shows it, else the rules go in a fixed order. The change is announced and the chip updates. The palette and the picker label hidden results "Hidden by filters".
  - **Hidden, not drawn elsewhere:** a hidden selection or trace hop has no box; it isn't marked on its parent (that would say the parent is it, the same reason ADR-0120 drops its edges). The inspector says "Hidden by filters" with **Show on canvas**; a trace step with a hidden end says "Checkout is hidden by filters." with **Show Checkout**. The steps list is computed on the whole graph, so it never skips a hop.
- **Alternatives rejected:** offering every kind and layer whatever the graph has (a long popover of choices that do nothing); a chip whenever a setting is on (every demo and test-free repo would carry "1 filter · nothing hidden"); clearing all filters when going to a hidden node (loses the reader's other choices); refusing to go to a hidden node (search would find what it can't show); computing the trace on the filtered graph (a hidden middle hop would silently end the path); a modal dialog (filters are adjusted while watching the canvas change).
- **Consequences:** every explorer story's top bar now shows "Filters 1", so their baselines change by that count. New story Explorer/Filters. Not handled: the filters aren't in the URL yet (M4b.9 deep links); the folder field matches whole service roots and folders only, not files.

## ADR-0122: Deep links are a readable query of what differs from the default, checked against the graph, and reported by the explorer as it changes
- **Context:** M4b.9a. SPEC §3 and UX.md "Deep links": the URL encodes repo, commit, the drill-down path, the selection, the trace's start and step, and the filters (the diff base comes with M6). The website's path already carries the repo and commit (`/<owner>/<repo>/<sha>`). A link may be old (a node renamed at a later commit), hand-edited, or hostile, and the explorer is uncontrolled: it takes `initial*` props.
- **Decision:**
  - **Codec** (`explorer/deepLink.ts`): `encodeState` writes only what differs from `DEFAULT_STATE`, in a fixed parameter order: `open` (the open nodes, in opening order so Esc still closes the last), `sel`, `trace`, `step` (1-based like the player, left out on the first step), then `hideLayers`, `hideKinds`, `hideConfidence`, `hideLines` (the hidden connection kinds, written only when not just imports; empty means all shown), `only`, `hideFolders`, and `tests=shown`. Filters are canonical (`normalizeFilters`), so equal states give equal strings. Lists are comma-separated; values are percent-encoded with `/`, `:`, and `@` kept, so node IDs read as themselves (`sel=route:api/POST%20/api/orders`) and a comma inside an ID can't split a list.
  - **Nothing in a link is trusted:** `decodeState(query, index)` keeps a node only if the graph has it and it can do what's asked (break down, be selected, start a trace), a step only within that trace's steps, filter values only from the known lists, and folders only the repo has (the popover's rule). Everything else is left out and returned in `ignored`, so the page can say the link doesn't fully match this commit (fail visibly) instead of guessing. Unknown parameters (`utm_source`) are skipped silently; a malformed escape is noted. `+` reads as a space, as browsers send forms.
  - **Explorer:** `initialTrace` starts a trace from any traceable page or route (before, only `traceStart` could be opened tracing) and `initialHiddenKinds` sets the connection kinds. `onStateChange` gets the state after each change a link keeps, compared by its encoding, so a re-render, a camera move, or filters set to equal values report nothing, and the state it opened with isn't reported. Only effectively open nodes go in (a node remembered inside a closed one isn't drawn, so it isn't linked), and a trace that ended isn't linked though its start is kept for focus.
- **Alternatives rejected:** `URLSearchParams` alone (encodes `/` and `:`, so links become `%2F` soup); repeated parameters (`open=a&open=b`; longer, and UX.md's example uses commas); JSON or base64 state (unreadable and not hand-editable); writing every field (long links for the common case); the camera in the link (screen-size dependent; the drill-down path is what SPEC asks for); a controlled explorer (every host would have to own a dozen pieces of state to get one URL).
- **Consequences:** the website can read `location.search` into the `initial*` props and write `onStateChange` back with `history.replaceState` (M4b.9b, with Copy link in the share menu). Not handled yet: the diff base (M6); back/forward between states (each change replaces the URL rather than adding history entries).

## ADR-0123: The website keeps its URL in step with `replaceState`, says what a link left out, and shares the commit's permalink from a share menu
- **Context:** M4b.9b, on top of ADR-0122's codec. The repo page (`/<owner>/<repo>` for the latest commit, `/<owner>/<repo>/<sha>` pinned) hides the explorer's top bar, so its share menu isn't there; "Add to README" already sits on the repo bar (ADR-0073). Node IDs can change between commits, and `/<owner>/<repo>` moves to newer commits as they're analyzed.
- **Decision:**
  - **In:** once the graph has loaded, `RepoDiagram` decodes `location.search` against it (once per load) and passes the state to the explorer's `initial*` props. What `decodeState` left out is named in a quiet note above the canvas ("Part of this link doesn't apply to commit a1b2c3d, so it was left out: selected route:api/GET /gone."), up to three parts and a count, with Dismiss. The rest of the link applies as usual.
  - **Out:** each `onStateChange` replaces the query with `history.replaceState`, keeping the path and hash. No new history entries: Back leaves the page, as it did before, instead of stepping through every click. Left-out parts drop out of the URL at the first change, since they never applied.
  - **Share menu** (`ShareMenu` in the viewer): a Share button opening a small disclosure panel (not a dialog) with the link in a read-only field and **Copy link**; Esc, the button, or a press outside closes it, and Esc doesn't reach the explorer (it would go up a level). A refused copy selects the field for the reader. The explorer's top bar draws it (`TopBar`'s `share` slot, `linkTo` for the URL, by default this page with the query); the website puts one on the repo bar next to "Add to README", its panel hanging from the bar's right edge like that one's.
  - **The shared link is the commit's permalink** (`/<owner>/<repo>/<sha>?…`), even from `/<owner>/<repo>`: the IDs in it are this commit's, and the page at the repo's path can move to a commit that lacks them.
- **Alternatives rejected:** `pushState` per change (Back would undo one click at a time and the history list would fill with drill-downs); Next's `router.replace` (a server round trip per click for a client-only state); rewriting the URL at once to drop what didn't apply (the reader would lose the evidence of what they were sent before reading the note); sharing the page's own URL (a `/<owner>/<repo>` link would stop matching after the next analysis).
- **Consequences:** a reload or a pasted link reopens the same view at the same commit. Export (M4b.10) joins the share menu. Not done: the diff base in the link (M6); undo with Back.

## ADR-0124: Exports are pure text renderers of the view on screen: SVG from its ELK layout with the README image's markup, Mermaid with the diff's escaping
- **Context:** M4b.10a (UX.md "Export": SVG and PNG of the current view, both themes; Mermaid of the current level). The canvas is HTML boxes over an SVG of lines, styled by CSS with `color-mix`, so serializing the DOM wouldn't give a standalone file: the CSS, fonts, and React state stay behind, and the selection, trace, and zoom detail would leak in. The README image (ADR-0071) already draws nodes, lines, and pills as escaped SVG text from the tokens, and the diff's Mermaid summary (M6.6) already escapes repo text for Mermaid.
- **Decision:**
  - **`renderViewSvg(index, view, layout, { theme })`:** a pure function of the view and its ELK layout (the one the canvas is drawing). Closed frames, icon tiles, lines, pills, and the footer reuse `image/diagram.ts`'s markup (now exported, with the service's layer passed in for nested nodes); new here are open frames (translucent layer tint at 6%, so nested frames deepen, with their name row as the header) and the card shapes the README image never needed (capsule routes, window pages, a model's fields row, dotted unresolved calls). Stacked as the canvas stacks: open frames, lines, closed boxes, pills. One theme per file (dark or light); the raw layer colors go in a second `.gi` rule, so the README image's bytes don't change.
  - **`viewMermaid(index, view)`:** a `flowchart LR` of the visible nodes in drawing order (`n<i>` IDs), open ones as nested `subgraph`s; shapes per kind and label escaping are the diff's (`mermaidShape`, newly exported, and `escapeMermaidLabel`), so both Mermaid outputs read the same and share one escaping rule (SECURITY.md T10). Exact and pattern lines solid, heuristic and unresolved dotted.
  - The viewer now depends on `@gitinside/diff` (browser-safe, no Node APIs); M6.9's diff mode needs it anyway.
- **Alternatives rejected:** serializing the canvas's DOM (needs inlined CSS and `color-mix`, carries the selection and trace, and isn't deterministic); a canvas-only PNG path (no SVG, and text measured per browser); copying the escaper into the viewer (two escaping rules to keep in step).
- **Consequences:** the same view and layout give the same bytes, testable without a browser. Fonts aren't embedded: an exported SVG uses Geist where installed, else the system's sans and mono (text is sized from estimates and cut with "…", so it never overflows). The selection, trace, and zoom-dependent detail aren't exported. PNG (the SVG rasterized) and the share menu's Export section are M4b.10b.

## ADR-0125: Export lives in the share menu, is made in the browser from the drawn view, and the PNG is the SVG rasterized at 2×
- **Context:** M4b.10b wires ADR-0124's renderers to the reader. The explorer's top bar has a share menu, but the website hides that bar and draws its own share menu on the repo bar, outside the explorer, so the page needs the explorer's current view and layout without owning them. A PNG needs pixels, and the site's CSP allows `img-src 'self' blob: data:` and nothing that uploads.
- **Decision:**
  - **What's exported is what's drawn:** `viewExport(index, shown)` wraps the view the canvas last drew and the layout it drew it at (`Shown`), not the view being laid out, so an export never mixes a new view with an old layout. Each file is rendered only when asked for.
  - **Host access by a ref:** `Explorer`'s `exportRef` is kept pointing at that export (`undefined` before the first layout); `ShareMenu`'s `exports` reads it each time it draws. A ref, not state, so the page doesn't re-render on every layout. Without a layout yet, the buttons say "still being laid out" rather than disabling (a disabled focused button loses its keys, see Lessons).
  - **The menu:** an "Export this view" section under the link: SVG and PNG with a Dark/Light choice beside them (starting from the screen's `data-theme` on each open), and Mermaid (no theme) on its own row, saved as `.mmd` or copied. A live region says what was saved, by file name.
  - **PNG:** the SVG loaded as an image from a `blob:` URL, drawn on a canvas at 2× and encoded with `toBlob`. When 2× passes a browser's canvas limits (16,384 px a side, or Safari's 16.7 M px area), it's drawn as large as fits and the message gives the scale. If the browser can't (no 2D canvas, no blob), the message points to the SVG.
  - **Saving:** a hidden `download` link to a `blob:` URL, revoked a minute later (revoking at once cancels the download in some browsers). File names are `owner-name-sha7[-theme].ext`, with anything but `[A-Za-z0-9._-]` replaced, so a repo name can't add a folder or an extension.
- **Alternatives rejected:** lifting the explorer's view and layout state into the page (the page would re-render on every layout, and the explorer's internals would become its API); an `onExportChange` callback into page state (same re-render); rendering the PNG on the server (it would cost compute and upload nothing the browser can't make); disabling the buttons until a layout (focus trap risk, and they'd flash disabled on open).
- **Consequences:** nothing leaves the reader's machine. An exported PNG has no Geist (an SVG image can't load fonts), so text uses the system's sans and mono. Checked in Chromium (both themes: SVG 2108×408, PNG 4216×816, Mermaid saved and copied); Firefox and Safari weren't run here, and the website's export wasn't run under the real CSP in a browser (it uses only `blob:` images, which the CSP allows).

## ADR-0126: The canvas's boxes are a WAI-ARIA tree with every box a Tab stop, arrows move spatially, and the camera follows keyboard focus
- **Context:** M4b.11 (UX.md "Keyboard and accessibility", M4a review finding 4). Boxes were focusable `role="group"` elements whose selection and broken-down state were only words in their `aria-label`, there were no arrow keys, and the live region said only "db, datastore. Selected". Boxes are absolutely placed siblings (one flat list so a node keeps its element and focus when it opens), not nested in the DOM. The canvas is `overflow: hidden` under a CSS-transform camera, so a browser scrolling it to show a focused box would put the drawing and the camera out of step.
- **Decision:**
  - **A tree, not groups or buttons:** the boxes sit in a `role="tree"` ("Architecture"), each a `treeitem` with `aria-level` (frames around it + 1), `aria-setsize`/`aria-posinset` among its visible siblings (these give a flat list a tree's shape), `aria-expanded` only when it can break down (true when broken down), and `aria-selected` on the box holding the selection. The name no longer repeats "Selected" when the role carries it (`DiagramNode` drops it whenever `aria-selected` is given); trace and diff states stay in the name, since no ARIA state fits them.
  - **Every box stays a Tab stop, in drawing order** (a frame, then its inside), as UX.md's table says, rather than APG's single roving Tab stop: a screen-reader user's way through a diagram is reading order, and the arrows are spatial, which only helps sighted keyboard users. (Revisit with M4b.13's culling at 2,000 nodes, where Tab past the canvas gets long.)
  - **Arrow keys move to the nearest box that way** (`nearestBox`): among boxes wholly past the focused one's edge on that side (1px tolerance), the least gap with each pixel off to the side counting double; ties go to the center nearest the line through the focused box, then to drawing order. "Wholly past" excludes the frame a box sits in and a frame's own children, so arrows move between neighbors and Tab goes in and out of frames. Arrows with a modifier are left to the browser and screen reader; plain ones are always kept from scrolling the page, even at an edge.
  - **The camera follows keyboard focus:** a box focused by Tab, an arrow, or focus put back after a change (`:focus-visible`) glides into view with `ensureVisible`, once the explorer has the layout it's in (focus restored after breaking down comes before it). A click's focus doesn't move the camera, so a box half off the edge doesn't jump from under the pointer. A box bigger than the canvas counts as its top-left part that fits (where its name is), for keyboard focus and inspector fly-to alike; before, a big frame was centered and its name could be off-screen. The canvas is `overflow: clip`, which isn't a scroll container, so focus can't scroll it.
  - **The live region says what a selection connects to** (`selectionAnnouncement`): "placeOrder, function. Calls POST /api/orders. Called by Checkout.", the inspector's connections (hidden kinds left out), each end named once, five per list then "and N more"; clearing the selection says "Selection cleared".
- **Alternatives rejected:** `role="button"` with `aria-pressed` (Enter and Space would have to do the same thing, and "pressed" isn't selection); `role="grid"` (the layout isn't rows and columns); nesting boxes in the DOM for an implicit tree (a box opening would remount its children and lose focus, M4b.3a); `aria-owns` to build the hierarchy (poorly supported, and levels already give it); APG's tree arrows (Up/Down through reading order, Right/Left to open and close) instead of spatial ones (UX.md asks for direction, and Enter and Esc already open and close); `scrollIntoView` (scrolls the clipped canvas, not the camera).
- **Consequences:** a box's role and states now reach assistive technology as states, not words (Chromium's accessibility tree: `treeitem "@shop/api, service" [expanded] [level=1] [selected]`). Checked in Chromium (both themes): Tab, arrows, Enter, Space, Esc, the camera bringing 12 tabbed boxes into view at 99% (a too-big frame by its corner), a click on a half-hidden box leaving the camera still, nothing scrolled, and Playwright's ARIA snapshot of the tree. Not checked with a real screen reader here, nor in Firefox or Safari (`overflow: clip` needs Safari 16).

## ADR-0127: First-visit hints are one card at the canvas's top, moved on by doing what they say, remembered per browser through a store the host passes in
- **Context:** M4b.12a (UX.md "Onboarding": three dismissible hints in sequence, zoom in → select → trace; "Don't show again" stored per browser). The explorer is a library: Storybook, its tests, and the README image's pages draw it too, and every Explorer story has a Linux baseline. The canvas's four corners already hold overlays (status chips, zoom, breadcrumbs, minimap), and the inspector's overview has a "Start here" paragraph.
- **Decision:**
  - **Opt-in through a store:** `Explorer`'s `onboarding` prop takes an `OnboardingStore` (`done()`, `remember()`); without one there are no hints, so existing stories, tests, and baselines don't change. `browserOnboarding()` keeps `gitinside.onboarding = "done"` in `localStorage`, reading storage on each call and swallowing its errors (blocked storage shows the hints every visit rather than breaking the page); `memoryOnboarding()` is for stories and hosts with their own settings. The website passes `browserOnboarding()`.
  - **Chosen once, after mount:** the hints are picked in an effect, so a server render (no storage) matches the first client one. A hint already done when the explorer opens (a deep link with something broken down, selected, or tracing) or impossible in this repo (nothing to break down, no page or route with a request path) is left out, so "Tip 1 of 2" can happen and no tip asks for the impossible.
  - **Doing it moves on:** breaking a node down (any way), selecting one, or starting a trace advances the hint that asks for it; Next does too. Only the hint on screen counts, so the click before a double-click doesn't skip "Select a box". Finishing the last hint, by Done or by doing it, is remembered like "Don't show again"; the close button and Esc (with focus in the hint) hide them for this visit only.
  - **One small popover at the top center** (elevation 2, 304px), not a tooltip pointing at a box (which would follow the camera and cover what it points at), nor the inspector (already the overview's "Start here"). It's a `section` named by its title, first in the canvas region so Tab reaches it before the boxes; it rises in and fades out like the other overlays, and between hints only its words swap, so Next keeps focus as it becomes Done. Closing it with focus inside moves focus to the selection's box or the first box, so the keys keep working (focus dropped to `<body>` breaks them, M4b.6b).
- **Alternatives rejected:** hints on by default with a Storybook decorator marking them done (every host would have to know to turn them off, and jsdom tests would all grow a hint); a cookie (sent to the server for nothing); counting visits instead of a done flag (UX.md asks for a first visit and a "Don't show again"); spotlighting the element a hint is about with a scrim (heavy, and DESIGN_SYSTEM's anti-patterns list leans away from such generated-UI flourishes); announcing each hint in the live region (it would talk over the selection announcement; the card is reachable by Tab and labelled).
- **Consequences:** the website shows the hints once per browser; clearing site data shows them again. A host that wants them per account (M8 sign-in) can pass its own store. Not checked with a real screen reader; checked in Chromium (both themes, reduced motion).

## ADR-0128: A repo with no app gets one card instead of a diagram; the analysis's warnings live in the repo overview, reached from a chip on the canvas
- **Context:** M4b.12b1. UX.md "States" gives the copy for a repo with no TS/JS app; ADR-0070 says the analysis's warnings (what wasn't analyzed and why) must be visible, and "fail visibly" forbids hiding them. Until now the explorer drew such a repo as one empty service box with search, filters, and trace that had nothing to act on, and showed no warning anywhere. Warning codes are the analyzer's (`SYNTAX_ERROR`, `SKIPPED_LARGE`, …), and a graph made by a newer analyzer may carry codes this viewer doesn't know.
- **Decision:**
  - **"No app" means no `file` node.** The analyzer makes services from workspace config and datastores from compose files without any source, so counting services would draw empties. Then the explorer renders one `EmptyState` card (UX.md's words, the mark, then "What the analysis skipped" with the warnings) in the canvas's place, a top bar with only the mark and repo (`TopBar actions={false}`), and no inspector, search, filters, share, or trace: there's nothing they could act on.
  - **Warnings are grouped by code under a plain-English title**, each with its file and the analyzer's own message, unchanged (`groupWarnings`, `WARNING_TITLES`). A code without a title is shown under the code itself, never dropped. A test reads the analyzer's source for every code it emits and fails if one has no title. A group over five shows five and "Show all N".
  - **They live in the repo overview** (the inspector with nothing selected), under "Start here", as a focusable section. A chip on the canvas ("9 warnings", above the breadcrumbs, hidden while tracing) clears the selection and moves focus to that section, so a keyboard or screen-reader user lands on its heading.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a banner over the canvas (covers the diagram, the hero, and most warnings are about files nobody will miss); a popover from the chip (a second place listing the same thing, and long lists don't fit a popover); rewording the analyzer's messages into friendlier text (the analysis didn't say that; titles group them instead); a separate warnings tab in the inspector (one more control for a list that's usually short).
- **Consequences:** a repo that has config but no source reads as a clear "not supported yet" instead of a broken diagram. New analyzer warning codes must get a title in `packages/viewer/src/model/warnings.ts` or the viewer's test fails. The no-app top bar drops the search column, so it fits a 390px screen; the full explorer still has no narrow layout.
- **Addendum (M4b.12b2):** the website's graph that fails to load or parse uses the same card in the error tone (`EmptyState tone="error"`) in the diagram's place, instead of a bare red line. `loadGraph`'s failure now carries a `title` (the card's heading: "The diagram didn't load", or "The saved diagram couldn't be read" for one that fails the schema) as well as the `message` under it (the server's own words when it sent some), and a test keeps a message from repeating its heading. The card is the alert and takes focus (`tabIndex={-1}`), so a keyboard or screen-reader user lands on it and Tab reaches Try again; Try again shows only when another attempt could help (not for a 404 or a graph that fails the schema). A failed retry moves focus to the new card.

## ADR-0129: The drill-down canvas culls to a tile-snapped window around the camera's view on big levels, keeps what focus may land on, and memoizes every box, line, and pill
- **Context:** M4b.13a. SPEC asks for 60fps pan and zoom with 2,000 visible nodes and names viewport culling (D5.2, ADR-0017's React Flow plan; the canvas is our own `DrillCanvas`, M4b.3). Two costs grow with a level's size: every camera move re-rendered every box, line, and pill (the zoom prop sets the detail level, so `DrillCanvas` renders on each wheel tick), and every element stays in the page however far the camera is zoomed in. Several features find a box by its element: focus put back after a trace or a filter change, a palette pick, the arrow keys (`nearestBox` reads the layout, then focuses the element).
- **Decision:**
  - **Memoized boxes, lines, and pills** (`CanvasBox`, `CanvasLine`, `CanvasPill`) taking only stable or primitive props (the view's own node and edge objects, the layout's rects, booleans and strings for the look); their handlers read a ref when they fire. A camera move then re-renders the canvas's lists but none of their items unless their look or the culling changed.
  - **Culling only from 400 boxes and lines together** (`CULL_FROM`). Below that, drawing everything costs less than recomputing, and every box stays a Tab stop (ADR-0126), so small and medium repos, every story, and every baseline are unchanged.
  - **The window is the visible rect grown by half its size per side, snapped outward to 512px tiles** (`cullWindow`): panning within a tile changes nothing, and a fast pan or a zoom-out step doesn't uncover empty canvas before React catches up. A box is drawn if it overlaps the window (an open frame holding the window counts); a line if its route's bounds or its pill's reach do.
  - **Always drawn:** the host's `keep` (the selection's box and the trace start's box, where the explorer puts focus back), the focused box (tracked with focus events, so panning away never drops focus to the page), an arrow key's culled target (drawn, then focused in a layout effect), boxes leaving in a fold, and boxes whose glide starts in the window (so they slide out rather than vanish).
  - **While the camera glides, the window spans where it glides from and to** (M4b.15c, `useGlideSpan`, from the M4b review: a palette pick or a far Tab used to cull to the destination only, so the start emptied out for the 240–600 ms of the glide). Translate and scale ease along one curve, so each edge of the visible rect moves one way: the span of start and end holds every frame. An interrupted glide keeps the first one's start; the span narrows to the destination after the move's duration token plus 100 ms. Pointer moves (at once), the first fit, and reduced motion don't glide, so they cull to the view alone. A far jump draws more for that moment, at most what an unculled level draws.
- **Alternatives rejected:** CSS `content-visibility: auto` on each box (keeps every element in the page and still re-renders them all on a zoom tick; its skipping depends on the browser's own viewport math under our transformed stage); per-frame culling without tiles (a reconcile of the whole list on every pan frame); culling every level (changes Tab order and the ARIA tree for small repos, which gain nothing); a canvas/WebGL renderer (SPEC: only if benchmarks demand it, which M4b.13b measures).
- **Consequences:** on a big level, Tab reaches only drawn boxes, so getting past the canvas takes at most a screenful of presses (ADR-0126's open question); the arrow keys and the palette still reach every box. A long camera glide (fit, fly-to) draws its start and destination windows together, more boxes for that moment (M4b.15c; before that, boxes near its start disappeared during the glide). `aria-setsize`/`aria-posinset` still count the whole level, so a screen reader hears the real size. Whether 2,000 visible nodes reach 60fps is M4b.13b's measurement, not assumed here.

## ADR-0130: The render benchmark drives the production explorer in headless Chromium on an exact synthetic graph and on cal.com opened to each size, and "60fps" means the median rounds to 60

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31)
- **Context:** M4b.13b1. EVALUATION.md §2 asks for fps at 500 / 1,000 / 2,000 visible nodes on a synthetic and a real graph, Playwright panning and zooming for 10 s, median and p5 from `requestAnimationFrame` deltas, target ≥ 60fps median at 2,000. It leaves open what "visible" counts, how a real graph gets to a size, what page is measured, and how a 60Hz display's 16.6–16.7 ms frames are read against "60".
- **Decision:**
  - **Visible nodes are the level's boxes** (`buildView(...).nodes` under the explorer's default filters): what the level holds, whether or not culling (ADR-0129) draws it. The report also lists the fewest and most boxes actually in the DOM during the run, so culling's effect shows.
  - **Synthetic: exact sizes.** 10 services, each broken down into its routes or pages plus one closed folder, so a level has exactly 10 + 10 × (entries + 1) boxes, with 1.5 lines per entry (handles, renders, and frontend-to-backend HTTP lines in all three confidence styles).
  - **Real: the analysis benchmark's repo (cal.com, 113 services), opened as a reader would.** Shallowest first, in drawing order, each node opened if the level still fits, until nothing more fits (`openToReach`). Opening never shrinks a level, so a node that didn't fit is never retried. The case is the largest level not over the target, reported with its real count.
  - **The page is the explorer in a Vite production build** (`bench/render/`, React production mode, ELK in a Web Worker like the website, dark theme, no hints), served locally, measured in Playwright's headless Chromium at 1440×900. 2 s idle are recorded first: the display's own rate, the ceiling `requestAnimationFrame` can reach.
  - **The run starts at the busiest spot at the lowest zoom.** The explorer fits a big level at no less than 40%, centered, and on a sparse layout that can be empty canvas (cal.com at 1,000 nodes drew 0 boxes there), so fps would measure drawing nothing. The runner zooms out to 25%, scans the level a canvas at a time from its top-left, parks the camera where the most leaf boxes are drawn, and reports that count.
  - **The gestures are real input events through Playwright**, one every 8 ms (about a trackpad's rate) for 10 s in five phases: wheel pan, Ctrl+wheel zoom in, a circular drag, Ctrl+wheel zoom out, wheel pan back.
  - **p5 fps is 1000 / the 95th-percentile frame time** (nearest rank): a frame rate's slow tail is its long frames. Frames over 50 ms are counted too.
  - **"Meets 60fps" means the median rounds to 60 or more.** At 60Hz the median frame time sits at 16.6–16.7 ms, so a strict 60.0 would fail or pass on timer rounding alone; the report prints the unrounded numbers next to the verdict.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Storybook as the page (development-only wrappers, and a story would join the visual baselines); dispatching synthetic events from inside the page (not Playwright, and they skip the browser's input path); counting only DOM boxes as "visible" (would let culling define the target away); a headed browser (needs a display on the autopilot machine and varies with what else is on screen).
- **Consequences:** results depend on the machine and on headless Chromium's frame scheduling, so each report names both; numbers from different machines aren't compared. A real case can land under its target when its folders come in big steps (cal.com reached each target exactly). The first-layout time is reported too, though it isn't the fps target: a case whose first layout isn't drawn within 10 minutes is reported as not measured (nothing to pan), never dropped or given made-up frames.

## ADR-0131: Big views lay out without ELK's model order, and the biggest with one crossing-minimization pass

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31)
- **Context:** M4b.13b2. The first render report (ADR-0130) found pan and zoom at 59.9 fps, but cal.com's first layout took 18 s at 500 visible nodes, 56 s at 1,000, and didn't finish in 10 minutes at 2,000, so 2,000 couldn't be measured. ELK's own per-step timer (`elk.layout(graph, { measureExecutionTime: true })`, in Node on the dumped layout inputs) put 18 of 24 s at 500 nodes in one step, "Sort By Input Model": the model-order sort behind `considerModelOrder` (ADR-0106's stable ordering). Every strategy cost the same (`NODES_AND_EDGES`, `PREFER_NODES`, `PREFER_EDGES`: 24 s), and `forceNodeModelOrder` instead made crossing minimization take 191 s. Without model order: 1.7 s at 500, 2.7 s at 1,000, 40 s at 2,000, where crossing minimization (24 s) led. The sort's cost grows steeply with the long edges' dummy nodes, not with boxes alone: on the first k boxes of cal.com's top level it took 0.2 s at 164 nodes plus edges, 0.9 s at 297, 13.6 s at 438; cal.com's top level alone (149 boxes, 364 lines, no frames) took 10.3 s, 0.8 s without it.
- **Decision:**
  - **Up to 200 nodes plus edges (`MODEL_ORDER_LIMIT`), the options are unchanged.** Every story and the shop demo's states are under it (a test checks), so no layout, screenshot, or baseline moves, and the ordering test still runs with model order on.
  - **Above it, `considerModelOrder.strategy: NONE`.** Layouts stay deterministic (fixed seed); what's lost is siblings keeping their order between related views where edges leave ELK a choice. Routes and pages stay in their service's first column (`layerConstraint: FIRST`, independent of model order).
  - **Above 3,000 nodes plus edges (`THOROUGHNESS_LIMIT`), `thoroughness: 1`** (ELK's default is 7 crossing-minimization passes). cal.com at 2,000 boxes (4,328 with lines): 40 s → 15 s (3 passes: 21 s). Brandes–Köpf placement and orthogonal routing (about 4 s each) are what's left.
  - Counted as nodes plus edges, like culling's threshold (ADR-0129): known from the input before ELK runs.
- **Alternatives rejected:** model order off everywhere (every baseline moves for no gain on small views, and small views are where order across steps is followed by eye); a time budget with a fallback rerun (the slow run would still be paid first, and the result would depend on the machine); `SEPARATE_CHILDREN` hierarchy handling (edges between services, most of a big level's lines, would no longer be routed in one run); a cheaper count such as boxes only (cal.com's top level has 149 boxes and was still 10 s).
- **Consequences:** a level crossing 200 nodes plus edges may reorder its siblings as it grows. The re-run render benchmark (`bench/results/2026-10-31-render.md`, in Chromium's layout worker) measured cal.com's first layout at 2.0 s (500 boxes), 2.5 s (1,000), and 9.2 s (2,000), and 2,000 boxes then panned at 59.9 fps median. A 2,000-box first layout still takes seconds; the explorer shows its loading state meanwhile, in the worker. The numbers come from one machine, Node's V8, like the worker's.

## ADR-0132: The explorer's E2E runs the production site against PGlite served over the wire protocol, and the browser's outside reads are answered by the test

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31)
- **Context:** M4b.14a. The website's E2E (`apps/web/e2e`) ran only against a server with no database, so the repo page said "temporarily unavailable" and the explorer was never driven end to end. `viewRepo` needs Postgres for a permalink (no GitHub call), and the browser then fetches the graph from `/api/graph` (object storage) and code from `raw.githubusercontent.com`. Docker isn't available on the development machine, and CI's `web-e2e` job runs in Playwright's container without a Postgres service.
- **Decision:**
  - **A second server of the same build, `seeded`,** whose `DATABASE_URL` points at PGlite (in memory) served by `@electric-sql/pglite-socket` on a local port (`e2e/database.ts`, a Playwright web server that's ready once it prints "seeded"). It's migrated with the real migrations through `runMigrations` and seeded with one finished analysis of `fixtures/shop-demo` through the db package's own `upsertRepo` / `startAnalysis` / `completeAnalysis`, so the page reads it with production code and the production driver.
  - **The test answers the two outside reads** with `page.route`: the graph with the analyzer's golden `expected.json` (stamped with the commit, as the worker's graphs are), and the fixture's files with `access-control-allow-origin: *`. Routing happens after the browser's CSP check, so a blocked origin still fails (checked by mutating `connect-src`: two tests turned red).
  - **Two Playwright projects:** `unconfigured` (site.e2e.ts, port 3107, unchanged) and `seeded` (explorer.e2e.ts, port 3108). Any console error or warning fails a test, as in site.e2e.ts.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a Postgres service in the `web-e2e` job (works on CI only, not on a machine without Docker); a fake object store behind `/api/graph` (covered by `graphRoute`'s tests; it'd add a third server for no explorer behaviour); a test-only route or flag in the app to inject a graph (code that ships but only tests use).
- **Consequences:** the explorer is exercised in Chromium under the real CSP, the layout Web Worker, ELK, Shiki, and deep links (load, drill down, select, trace, code from GitHub, Open on GitHub). `/api/graph`'s bucket read isn't part of this E2E. PGlite runs one query at a time, which is fine at E2E load. Website visual baselines follow as M4b.14b.

## ADR-0133: The website's key screens are compared as screenshots on the seeded E2E server, in CI's `web-e2e` container

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31)
- **Context:** M4b.14b. SPEC's M4b needs visual tests green, and TESTING.md lists "stories + key screens". Every viewer story already has Linux baselines (ADR-0053), but the website's own CSS (landing, the repo bar, the page around the explorer, the cookie-driven theme) was only screenshot by hand.
- **Decision:**
  - **A third Playwright project, `visual`,** in `apps/web/e2e/playwright.config.ts`, running `screens.visual.ts` against the `seeded` server (ADR-0132) with the viewer's screenshot settings (1280×800, scale 1, animations disabled, caret hidden). Four screens, each in dark and light: `landing`, `diagram` (the permalink's system level), `selection` (`/checkout` selected inside `@shop/web`, code loaded and colored), `trace` (step 3 of `/checkout`'s trace). Light comes from the site's `gi-theme` cookie, set before the first request, as a returning visitor's would be.
  - **Baselines in `apps/web/e2e/baselines/<screen>--<theme>.png`,** compared by the existing `web-e2e` job (same container, `--project` not narrowed) and captured by a `capture-web` job in the "Visual baselines" workflow (artifact `web-visual-baselines`). A test fails if a baseline has no screen. Off Linux the file skips itself.
  - **Each shot also checks** the theme on `<html>`, Geist loaded, and no console error or warning; the pointer is moved off the canvas first so no box is hovered.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a separate CI job for web visuals (a second production build per push for the same container); screenshots on the `unconfigured` server (the repo page there is only "temporarily unavailable"); more screens now (search, filters, share, and the inspector's states are viewer stories with baselines already).
- **Consequences:** a website CSS change shows up as a failed `web-e2e` job with a diff in its report artifact. Baselines must be re-captured when the analyzer's golden for `shop-demo` changes what the diagram shows.

## ADR-0134: The explorer frames what was just broken down at a readable size, each trace step frames its hop, and the step's header stays put

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31); amends ADR-0112
- **Context:** M4b.15f, review finding 6. On real repos (taxonomy at 1440×900, shop-demo at 1280×800) a broken-down level sat at the fit's 40% floor: names about 5px tall, most of the service off-screen, and a route a user wanted to click out of reach. A trace step centered nothing, so its other end could be off to the side, and the inspector could scroll the step's title out of view.
- **Decision:**
  - **A readable zoom, 80%** (`READABLE_ZOOM`, `explorer/camera.ts`): names about 11px, secondary text about 10px, a step above full detail (65%, `DETAIL_ZOOM`).
  - **The camera the explorer picks** (`autoCamera`, `explorer/framing.ts`, while nothing is hand-set) is the fit when the whole level fits at 80% or more. Otherwise it frames the node broken down last (the last of the expansion list the drawn view still has open): its whole box at its fit when that's readable, else at the zoom that fits its name and entries (the routes and pages leading a service's first level, `entriesRect`), never below 80%, with its top-left corner at the fit's inset (`placeRect`). It's derived on every render, so a canvas resize, a deep link, and Esc back to a level that reads whole all follow without effects.
  - **0 and the fit button show the whole level** (an `overview` flag), at its fit even below 80%, until the next drill-down or close.
  - **Each trace step frames its hop** (`frameHop`), once per step, after the first fit and on the drawn layout: the zoom stays, is raised to 80% from below, and is lowered as far as it takes to show both ends (the closed boxes lit as current) clear of the canvas's corner overlays (`HOP_MARGIN`: 48px at the sides, 160px above and below, past the chips and the minimap, each at most a quarter of the canvas so a short canvas (≤ 320px tall) keeps room for the hop instead of dropping to 25%, M4b.15h; the first browser run had an end "on the canvas" under the minimap); the camera doesn't move when that's the zoom already and both ends are wholly on the canvas. This hands the camera to the reader, like flying to a connection.
  - **The trace step's header is sticky** at the inspector's top (`.gi-explorer__hop-header`), on the inspector's own background, so stepping from the list or the buttons below, or focus put back on the current step, never scrolls the title away.
- **Alternatives rejected:** raising the fit's floor to 80% (a big level would open on its middle, not on what it leads with); framing the whole frame from its top-left at 80% even when it fits at more (wastes the canvas on small services); centering the entries column (the frame's name and folders then sit off to the left); scrolling the inspector to the top on each step (a step clicked low in the list would jump away from the pointer).
- **Consequences:** a fitted camera on a small level is unchanged, so most stories keep their baselines; levels too big for 80% open closer in, and a user sees the rest of the level by panning or with 0. Trace steps move a hand-set camera.

## ADR-0135: The progress page shows what the analyzer found, from the nodes its stages produced, capped and checked at every hop

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31); extends ADR-0064 (progress events)
- **Context:** M5.15b, issue #8 part 2: while an analysis runs, show the repo's facts as they're found ("Found 3 services: web, api, worker", "12 routes", "Postgres, Redis, Stripe"), only what the analyzer produced, calm rather than a log. The names come from the repo (package names, compose engines, hosts), so they are untrusted text travelling child → worker → Redis → web → browser.
- **Decision:**
  - **Findings come from nodes, not new detection.** `analyze({ onFound })` (`packages/analyzer/src/findings.ts`) reports, after each stage that makes such nodes, everything found so far: service labels (`workspace`), route and page counts (`express`, `next`), datastore and queue labels (`infra` after the Prisma relink, then `queues`), external labels (`http`, `sdk`). Nodes are counted once per ID, first wins, in assembly's part order, so the last report equals `findingsOf(graph.nodes)` of the returned graph; the golden test checks that on every fixture. A key appears once its stage has run, so `routes: 0` is "looked, none" and a missing key "not yet". Findings never enter the graph.
  - **A separate channel, cumulative.** The child prints `{"found":{…}}` lines beside its progress lines; the runner hands them to `onFound`; the processor keeps the latest with every later BullMQ progress update of the attempt, the upload stage's too, and a retry starts empty. Cumulative snapshots (not deltas) make any one stored progress complete on its own.
  - **Caps and checks** (`apps/worker/src/progress.ts`): the child stores the first 8 names per list (`FOUND_NAMES_MAX`) with the true count, each at most 64 characters (`FOUND_NAME_LENGTH`, then `…`), control and format characters (bidi overrides, zero-width) replaced with U+FFFD. `parseFoundFacts` accepts exactly that shape and is applied to the child's line, to the stored progress, and to the website's copy of the status. The child's stdout cap rises from 64 KiB to 128 KiB to fit seven found lines at their caps.
  - **Words on the server** (`describeFound`, ADR-0067's rule): fixed order (services, "Entry points" as "2 routes, 3 pages", "Data", "Outside services"), anything empty left out, "and N more" past the kept names. The page shows a "Found so far" definition list under the stages once there's a line; each new row eases in (240ms, no fill mode, none under reduced motion), and the term column has a fixed width so names don't shift as longer terms arrive.
- **Alternatives rejected:** counting from the final graph only (nothing until the end, which defeats the point); new per-stage detection for the page (two sources of truth that could disagree with the diagram); deltas per stage (a reader of one stored progress would miss earlier facts); dropping names with hidden characters (the kept names would no longer be the first 8, and the count would mislead); announcing each fact in a live region (the stage text is already announced; facts would make it chatty).
- **Consequences:** a detector that fails (`DETECTOR_FAILED`) reports its key with nothing found, which the page shows as nothing; the warning is in the diagram. M5.17a's move of job state to Neon carries `found` as one more column of the job row.
- **Correction (M5.15c):** the enqueue API's `publicStatus` copied the documented status fields one by one and `found` was never added to it, so `GET /jobs/:id` dropped it and the website would never have shown a finding. The web side's tests fed it a status directly, and the API's test had no `found`. The API test now sends `found` (and `leftMs`) through and failed before the fix.

## ADR-0136: The progress page's time left is the worker's estimate from per-file step rates measured on past jobs (the cal.com benchmark until there are five), counted down from each step's start

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31); extends ADR-0064 (progress events) and ADR-0135
- **Context:** M5.15c, issue #8 part 1: show the time left once it's known ("About 40 s left"), from real data (the repo's file count or tarball size, measured times of past analyses: benchmark results, then real job durations), updated as stages finish, "Taking longer than usual" instead of a frozen or negative number, and nothing until it can be computed: never a fake countdown.
- **Decision:**
  - **Predictor: source files, from `programs` on.** The analyzer already reports the source-file count when `programs` starts, and nearly all the time is from there on (cal.com's step medians: 53.2 of 56.5 s). Before that (queue, download, unpack, listing files) there's no estimate. The tarball size isn't used: it's known only after the download, counts docs and assets, and the file count arrives a few seconds later anyway.
  - **Rates: median milliseconds per source file, per analyzer step** (`apps/worker/src/estimate.ts`), from the 50 most recently finished analyses' `stats` (`files`, `stageMs`; `recentAnalysisStats` in `@gitinside/db`), plus the median `jobMs.upload` for saving. Medians, so one odd repo doesn't swing it. With fewer than 5 usable analyses, the committed cal.com benchmark (`BENCH_SAMPLE`, each step's median and its 5,041 file nodes; a test keeps it equal to `bench/results/2026-10-26-analysis-calcom.json`). The benchmark measured no upload, so with it "Saving the diagram" shows no estimate and the analysis's estimate leaves saving out.
  - **The worker estimates; the web words it.** At each step's start the processor stores, with the BullMQ progress, `since` (that moment, the worker's clock) and `expectMs` (this step and every later one, plus saving). `GET /jobs/:id` answers `leftMs = max(0, expectMs − (now − since))` from the same machine's clock, so there's no clock skew between hosts. The rates are read once per attempt and never delay the job: until the read finishes (or if it fails, logged `estimate_samples_failed`) the benchmark is used.
  - **Coarse words** (`describeTimeLeft`): 0 is "Taking longer than usual"; under 10 s "A few seconds left"; under a minute rounded up to 10 s; then whole minutes. The SSE stream sends a view only when it changes, so the line changes every few seconds at most and never ticks like a stopwatch. It sits under the progress bar's end, on a line kept even while empty (the stages don't move when it arrives), and is added to the bar's `aria-valuetext`, not to a live region (the stage text is already announced).
- **Alternatives rejected:** a client-side countdown (a reopened page doesn't know when a step started, and it would tick between real updates); computing on the website (no Postgres on the job routes today, and the worker's start times are on another clock); one rate for the whole job (per-step rates make the estimate move as each step finishes, as #8 asks); a regression on file count with an intercept (more parameters than five samples support; revisit with production data); a fixed guess for the upload while the benchmark is used (a number not measured).
- **Consequences:** the first production jobs are estimated from a laptop's benchmark (20 cores; Cloud Run gives 1 vCPU), so they'll likely run over and say "Taking longer than usual" until five finish. Each new step starts from its own expectation, so a slow step's overrun isn't carried forward: the line can go from "Taking longer than usual" back to a time. Per-file rates fit big repos best; a tiny repo's fixed costs make its rate high, which overestimates big ones a little while the samples are mostly small. M5.17a moves job state to Neon: `since` and `expectMs` become job-row columns, and `estimate.ts` has no dependencies so the website can compute `leftMs` itself then.

## ADR-0137: A repo with over 50 MB of source fails at the inventory (`LIMIT_SOURCE_SIZE`), before any parsing

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31); extends the T3 limits (docs/SECURITY.md)
- **Context:** M5.16b, issue #10: VS Code (`11ba54a`) passes every check before parsing (328 of 500 MiB unpacked, 25,233 of 100,000 entries, 15,184 of 20,000 source files; M5.16a). Through the worker's own runner at production's caps (`pnpm bench:capped`, `bench/results/2026-10-31-capped-vscode.md`) it then died with `LIMIT_MEMORY` 33 s into the analysis, while building its one TypeScript program (an earlier, unrecorded session saw 44 s and 77 s, same step). The progress page sat on "Parsing 15,184 files", then said "The analysis needed more memory than one repo may use." after the download, the unpacking, and that half minute. SPEC §11 asks that it fail fast and say so, without raising the caps.
- **Decision:**
  - **Predictor: the bytes of the kept source files together.** The file count doesn't separate the cases: VS Code's 15,184 files are under the 20,000 limit, and its `src/vs/workbench` (4,946 files) ran out of memory while cal.com (5,073) and `src/vs/workbench/contrib` (3,523) finished. Bytes do: VS Code has 201 MiB of source against cal.com's 18. The inventory already `lstat`s every file it keeps, so the sum costs nothing and no file is read.
  - **Limit: 50 MB (MiB).** Under the caps on the dev laptop, VS Code folders of 10, 33, 45, and 59 MiB finished in 10 to 51 s, 80 MiB (`src/vs/workbench`) ran out of memory at 52 s, and cal.com (18 MiB, 113 services) took 38 s (one run each). 50 is under the largest size that finished: the margin is for what wasn't measured (how close 59 MiB came to 3 GB; Cloud Run's 1 vCPU against a 20-core laptop).
  - **Checked after `LIMIT_FILES`, in the analyzer's inventory** (`maxSourceBytes`, a default limit like the file count, so the CLI meets it too), with its own code so the website can say what happened: "This repo has over 50 MB of source code, the limit for one analysis. Try a smaller repo." Not retriable (failure.ts retries only listed codes).
- **Alternatives rejected:** lowering the file limit (it would refuse small-file repos that fit and still let a few huge files through); the tarball's size (it counts docs, assets, and vendored folders the inventory skips; GitHub's repo `size` is M5.18b's earlier, coarser check); counting in the extractor while streaming (seconds earlier, but it would have to repeat the inventory's ignore and generated-file rules); a memory model per service (two calibration points per shape aren't enough; cal.com took about 2.4× the time per MB of VS Code's `workbench/contrib`, across its 113 programs).
- **Consequences:** VS Code-sized repos now fail seconds after unpacking instead of minutes into parsing. The limit is a ceiling, not a promise: a repo of many services can still run out of memory or time under 50 MB (cal.com took 38 s at 18 MB on a 20-core laptop; Cloud Run has 1 vCPU), and `LIMIT_MEMORY`/`LIMIT_TIME` stay the backstop. Recalibrate on Cloud Run's hardware in M5.13 with `pnpm bench:capped`. What would let the analyzer take a VS Code: see the write-up's last section (one program per extension or folder, not holding every program at once, the M6.7 fact cache); none of it raises the caps.

## ADR-0138: The supported size is said as both limits, "up to 20,000 source files and 50 MB of source code"

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31); M5.16c, issue #10
- **Context:** after ADR-0137 a repo can be refused on two size limits, and the landing page said neither: a visitor found out only after the download and unpacking. The task asked for "repos up to about N files / M MB" from the measurements (M5.16a, `bench/results/2026-10-31-capped-vscode.md`).
- **Decision:**
  - **The statement is the two inventory limits, not a measured typical size:** "up to 20,000 source files and 50 MB of source code". Neither number alone predicts the other: cal.com has 3.6 KB per source file (5,073 files, 18 MiB) and VS Code 13.6 KB (15,184 files, 201 MiB), so 50 MB is about 14,000 files at cal.com's density and 3,800 at VS Code's. A rounded "about 5,000 files" would understate the file limit and still not be a promise. What was measured goes in the docs next to it (README, EVALUATION.md §2): the largest runs that finished under the caps were 5,073 files / 18 MiB (cal.com, 38 s) and 3,523 files / 59 MiB (51 s), on a 20-core laptop.
  - **Where it's said:** the landing page's hint under the field; the `LIMIT_FILES` and `LIMIT_SOURCE_SIZE` copy (on the progress page and as the repo page's reason) names both limits, so a repo over one knows about the other; `LIMIT_MEMORY` and `LIMIT_TIME` (checked only once both size limits passed) say that a repo with many services can still be too much under 50 MB, as cal.com's 2.4× time per MB suggests. `LIMIT_SIZE` and `LIMIT_ENTRIES` count the whole tarball, not source, so they keep their own numbers.
  - **One copy of the numbers in the web app** (`apps/web/lib/limits.ts`), pinned to the analyzer's `DEFAULT_LIMITS` by a test rather than imported, so the landing page's client bundle doesn't pull in the analyzer.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a single "about N files" (true for neither density); importing `DEFAULT_LIMITS` into the client component (the analyzer and TypeScript in the landing bundle); repeating the limits in UX.md's "Over the limit" example with a subfolder input (`?path=` isn't built; it stays a later task).
- **Consequences:** when ADR-0137's limit is recalibrated on Cloud Run (M5.13), `MAX_SOURCE_MB` and `limits.ts` change together or the test fails. SPEC §3's limits line names only 20k files; PROPOSALS P-3 asks to add the 50 MB.

## ADR-0139: The job is the `analyses` row: job ID, attempts, queue entry time, and progress as columns

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31); M5.17a1, issue #9, follows ADR-0114
- **Context:** ADR-0114 moves job state and progress into Neon, so the progress page stops asking the worker (`GET /jobs/:id`, BullMQ's memory) and Cloud Tasks (M5.17b) can replace the queue. The page needs, by job ID: the status, a failure's code, the running attempt's stage, step, counts, findings (#8) and time estimate (ADR-0136), and while queued, how many jobs are ahead.
- **Decision:**
  - **No separate `jobs` table.** The `analyses` row already has the job's lifecycle (`queued` → `running` → `done` | `failed`, ADR-0063) and its dedupe key (repo, commit, analyzer version). It gains four columns (migration `0002_analysis_jobs`):
    - `job_id`: the derived `analyze-<32 hex>` ID (`jobIdFor`), stored by the web app when it queues the row and by the worker when an attempt starts. Indexed, **not unique**: a repo deleted and created again under the same name with the same commits has the same ID, and the old row keeps it; `findJob` takes the newest row.
    - `attempts`: +1 at each start, 0 when the web app queues a failed row again.
    - `queued_at`: when the row last entered the queue (insert, a retry's requeue, a failed row queued again); existing rows take their `created_at`.
    - `progress` (`jsonb`): the worker's `StoredProgress` as BullMQ stores it (stage, progress, step, count, found, and the estimate's `since` + `expectMs`). The database allows it only on a `running` row (`analyses_progress_check`), and every move out of `running` (or a new start) clears it, so a retry never shows the last attempt's findings.
  - **Queue position = `queued` rows ahead by (`queued_at`, `id`)**, one subquery in `findJob` over a partial index on queued rows. A retry goes to the back, as BullMQ's delayed retries did (they had no position at all).
  - **The worker writes progress through the recorder** (`recordJobProgress`, only on a `running` row with that job ID): each update the processor stores in BullMQ is written too, one at a time in order (a slow write can't be overtaken), never awaited by the job, and a failure only logged (`progress_record_failed`). About 20–30 small writes per job.
  - **`since` is the worker's clock** (epoch ms), so the website must not subtract it from its own "now": that adds the two hosts' skew to the answer (the reason `timeLeft` runs on the worker today). M5.17a2 keeps elapsed time on one clock, e.g. the write records the database's `now()` with the time left at that moment, and `findJob` subtracts on the database's clock.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a `jobs` table keyed by job ID (a second row per analysis to keep in step, and the dedupe key twice); a unique `job_id` (the recreated-repo case would fail the new row's insert); progress in its own columns (the shape is the worker's and already checked field by field on read; columns would add a migration per new field); writing progress only at stage boundaries (the page's "Parsing 1,204 files" and findings come from steps).
- **Consequences:** M5.17a2 switches `/api/jobs/:id` and its SSE to `findJob` and checks `progress` with the worker's parsers before showing it; private jobs (`visibility`) stay hidden until M8's access check. A `queued` row whose job is lost (Redis wiped now; a task that failed to be created in M5.17b) would count in everyone's position until something fails it: M5.17b/c must fail such rows (a task that can't be created, or a sweep of `queued` rows older than the queue's retry window). The download stage's first update arrives before the attempt's row is `running`, so it isn't written; the page shows "Downloading" for a running row without progress anyway (`jobView`).

## ADR-0140: The progress page reads the job's row; the time left is aged on the database's clock

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31); M5.17a2, issue #9, follows ADR-0139
- **Context:** `/api/jobs/:id` and its SSE asked the worker (`GET /jobs/:id`), which goes away with BullMQ (ADR-0114). The row now has everything the page needs (ADR-0139) except one thing: the worker's estimate was `since` (a moment on the worker's clock) plus `expectMs`, and subtracting `since` from Vercel's `Date.now()` would add the two hosts' clock skew to "About 40 s left" (LEARNING.md, "Elapsed time on one clock").
- **Decision:**
  - **Each elapsed time is measured on one clock.** The worker's recorder turns `since` + `expectMs` into `leftMs`, the time left at the write, on its own clock (`recordedProgress`). `recordJobProgress` stamps the write with the database's `now()` in a new `progress_at` column (migration `0003_analysis_progress_at`; null exactly when `progress` is, a check constraint; every move out of `running` clears both). `findJob` returns `progressAgeMs = now() - progress_at`, on the database's clock, and the website shows `max(0, leftMs - progressAgeMs)`. No reader compares two machines' clocks.
  - **The row's `progress` is `RecordedProgress`**, checked with the worker's own parser (`parseRecordedProgress` in `@gitinside/worker/progress`, sharing its field checks with `parseStoredProgress`, which BullMQ's status now uses too). Only documented fields are copied; anything malformed shows as no progress ("Downloading"), as the worker's status did. A stored error code that isn't one of ours shows as `WORKER_ERROR`.
  - **Private jobs read as missing** (404), whatever their state, until M8's access check.
  - **`jobDepsFromEnv` needs only `DATABASE_URL`**; the web app's worker client keeps only `enqueue`. The missing message no longer says "over a day ago" (BullMQ's 24 h); a row is missing only if it never existed or retention removed it.
- **Alternatives rejected:** `progress_at` inside the `jsonb` (an untyped field written by SQL beside the worker's shape); computing the time left in SQL (the estimate's words and checks live in TypeScript, where the worker's parser is); keeping `since` and asking the database for the worker's offset (there is no shared reference).
- **Consequences:** each open progress page reads one row a second (the SSE interval), which keeps Neon's compute awake while someone watches; a stream lives 25 s and closes with the tab, so idle time still suspends it. The worker's `GET /jobs/:id` is unused by the website and goes with BullMQ in M5.17c. Rows written before this change hold `since`/`expectMs`; they show no time left.

## ADR-0141: Cloud Tasks from Vercel without an SDK: keyless token in two calls, a task per queue entry, a deadline past Cloud Run's

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31); M5.17b1, issue #9, follows ADR-0114
- **Context:** ADR-0114 has Vercel create one Cloud Tasks task per analysis, through the REST API, authenticated keylessly. It left two facts to check: the HTTP-target dispatch deadline's range and how long a used task name stays reserved. Checked against Google's reference on 2026-10-31: the deadline must be **15 s to 30 min** (default 10 min); a task ID used by a task that was deleted or **executed** is refused with `ALREADY_EXISTS` for **up to 24 hours** (9 days for `queue.yaml` queues), named tasks cost an extra lookup, and Google asks for hashed, not sequential (timestamp) prefixes.
- **Decision:**
  - **The token, in two plain requests** (`apps/web/lib/googleAuth.ts`): STS exchanges the platform's OIDC JWT (`subjectToken`, Vercel's in M5.17b2) for a federated token at the pool provider's `audience`, then IAM Credentials' `generateAccessToken` turns it into a 1 h token of an **enqueue-only service account** (`cloud-platform` scope; the account's roles are the limit). Cached until 5 min before expiry, one refresh shared by concurrent callers, retried after a failure. No `google-auth-library`: two `fetch` calls don't justify a dependency, and the SigV4 store set the precedent.
  - **One task per queue entry, named `<job ID>-<queued_at ms>`** (`taskIdFor`). The job ID alone would break retries: a failed job queued again within a day would get `ALREADY_EXISTS`, read as "already queued", and never run. The row's `queued_at` changes exactly when the row re-enters the queue, and two racing requests for one entry read the same row, so a race still makes one task. The hashed job ID comes first, as Google asks. The row (repo, commit, analyzer version) stays the dedupe; the name only guards the race.
  - **Only `409` with status `ALREADY_EXISTS` means "already queued".** `ABORTED` is also 409.
  - **The task is the signed job:** body `{ kind, owner, repo, sha }` (base64), `content-type` and the two HMAC headers in the task's headers, signed for `POST /run`; `oidcToken` for the invoker service account with the Cloud Run service's origin as audience. The signature binds the body, so it can't be moved to another job, but it's dated at creation: the worker (M5.17c) must accept it for as long as a task can wait and retry, not the API's 5 min skew.
  - **Dispatch deadline 330 s**, 30 s past Cloud Run's 300 s request timeout. With a shorter deadline Cloud Tasks would give up on a running job and push it again beside the first; this way Cloud Run ends an overlong request (answering 504, retried as the queue allows) before Cloud Tasks would.
  - **Every failure is a `WorkerUnavailableError`** (no token, network, a refusal), so `/api/analyze` keeps one unavailable path; the reason names the step and status, never a token.
  - **Tests run against a fake Google** (`google.testkit.ts`) that makes Google's checks: the provider audience and subject token, the one account it may impersonate, the access token's expiry, the queue's existence, the name under the queue, the deadline range, https, a POST, base64, an OIDC token, and names never reused. The worker's own `verifyRequest` checks the signature.
- **Alternatives rejected:** naming the task after the job ID alone (the 24 h reservation strands a re-queued failure); unnamed tasks (no guard on the race between two requests for one commit); a Google SDK (`@google-cloud/tasks` pulls gRPC; `google-auth-library` for two calls); a service-account key in Vercel (a long-lived secret the keyless setup exists to avoid).
- **Consequences:** M5.17b2 switches `/api/analyze` and check-on-view to the queue: the web app writes the `queued` row first, then creates the task from its `queued_at`, and fails the row when the task can't be created (ADR-0139's stranded-row case). M5.17c's `/run` must check the signature with a window of the queue's whole retry span. M5.17e's queue settings must keep the defaults' room for 3 attempts inside that window. One live call to Google is unverified until #3's project exists: the fake follows the reference, not a recording.

## ADR-0142: The website enqueues on Cloud Tasks: row first, `QUEUE_UNAVAILABLE` for a task never created, the OIDC token from each request

- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31); M5.17b2, issue #9, follows ADR-0139 and ADR-0141
- **Context:** `/api/analyze` and check-on-view called the worker's `POST /enqueue`, which goes with BullMQ (M5.17c). ADR-0141 built the Cloud Tasks client; this wires it in. Three things had to be settled: what to do with a row whose task can't be created, how a Vercel function gets its OIDC token, and where the job ID comes from now that the worker doesn't answer with one.
- **Decision:**
  - **`enqueueAnalysis` (`apps/web/lib/enqueue.ts`), shared by both callers:** upsert the repo, `queueAnalysis` (which now returns the row's `queued_at` and `entered`: whether this call inserted the row or moved it from `failed`), then, only for a `queued` row, `createTask` named `taskIdFor(jobId, queued_at)`. A `running` or `done` row gets no task. Only an `entered` row keeps the caller's rate-limit slot; anything else is refunded.
  - **A task that can't be created fails the row this call queued** with `QUEUE_UNAVAILABLE`, on any error (a bug too, then rethrown), so no `queued` row sits in everyone's queue position with nothing to run it (ADR-0139). The code isn't permanent, so the job page offers "Analyze again", and `/api/analyze` re-queues it as a new analysis. A row another request queued is left alone: that request owns its task. If a racing request did create the task, the worker's `startAnalysis` moves the failed row to `running` (it updates any row but `done`), so nothing is lost.
  - **Check-on-view retries `QUEUE_UNAVAILABLE`** instead of showing "behind, failed": it means the queue was down, not that the commit can't be analyzed, and before this change a down worker left no row, so the next view tried again. Other failures still aren't re-run on view.
  - **The job ID is the worker's `jobIdFor`**, from a new light entry `@gitinside/worker/request` (only `node:crypto`; `parseJobRequest` moved with it for M5.17c's `/run`), so the website and the worker can't disagree on a job's ID. Tests compare against the worker's main entry.
  - **The OIDC token:** Vercel puts the deployment's token in each function request's `x-vercel-oidc-token` header (`VERCEL_OIDC_HEADER`); the route or page passes it to `analyzeDepsFromEnv` / `repoViewDepsFromEnv` / `imageDepsFromEnv`. The Google token source is kept per identity for the process (so the access token is cached across requests), and reads the newest request's token at each refresh, else `VERCEL_OIDC_TOKEN` (local development). A forged header gets nothing: Google's STS checks the JWT's signature, issuer, and audience; at worst it fails that refresh, and the next request's token is used.
