  - **Configuration:** `WORKER_URL` (now the Cloud Run origin, https only), `ENQUEUE_HMAC_SECRET`, `GCP_PROJECT_ID`, `GCP_REGION`, `GCP_TASKS_QUEUE`, `GCP_WORKLOAD_IDENTITY_PROVIDER` (checked for the provider's full-name shape), `GCP_ENQUEUE_SERVICE_ACCOUNT`, `GCP_INVOKER_SERVICE_ACCOUNT`. Any missing or malformed ⇒ "temporarily unavailable" for `/api/analyze`, and no re-analysis on view.
  - The web app's enqueue client (`createWorkerClient`) and its tests are removed; `worker.ts` keeps `AnalyzeJob` and `WorkerUnavailableError`.
- **Alternatives rejected:** deleting the row instead of failing it (a failure the job page can't explain; the failed row says what happened and is re-queued by the next request anyway); a sweep of old `queued` rows instead of failing at once (rows would mislead everyone's position until it ran; still possible later for rows a crashed function left); `@vercel/functions`' `getVercelOidcToken` (a dependency reading Vercel's internal request context for one header we already have); computing the job ID in the web app (a second copy of the hash to keep in step).
- **Consequences:** until M5.17c ships `POST /run`, tasks have nothing to push to, and nothing is deployed yet (M5.13). Local development needs `vercel env pull` and the Google project (#3), or M5.17c's fake Cloud Tasks server. A Vercel function that dies between the row and the task still strands a `queued` row; M5.17c should sweep `queued` rows older than the queue's retry window.

## ADR-0143: `POST /run`: one attempt per push under a 280 s budget, the answer decides the retry, signatures valid for a day
- **Context:** M5.17c1. Cloud Tasks pushes each job to the worker on Cloud Run (ADR-0114), which gives a request at most 300 s (ADR-0111). The BullMQ processor held the attempt logic (progress, estimates, recording, failure mapping); the deadline, the attempt count, and how to say "retry" were all BullMQ's. The website signs the task's body when it creates the task (ADR-0141), so the signature can be old by the time a push arrives.
- **Decision:**
  - **One attempt, two deliverers:** the processor's body moved to `runAttempt` (`apps/worker/src/attempt.ts`), which never throws and returns the outcome or a `JobFailure`. BullMQ's processor and `POST /run` (`run.ts`) both call it, so progress, estimates, the Neon row writes, and logging behave the same until BullMQ goes (M5.17c3).
  - **One deadline:** the attempt has `JOB_BUDGET_MS` = 280 s from the request's arrival (300 s less 20 s to record a failure, delete the job folder, and answer). `processJob` takes a `timeLeft()`; the download gets its own 2 min or what's left less `PUBLISH_RESERVE_MS` (20 s), whichever is smaller, and the child the same against its 5 min, rounded down to whole seconds. Nothing left for the download is a retriable `DOWNLOAD_TIMEOUT`; nothing left for the child is `LIMIT_TIME` without spawning it. A monotonic clock (`performance.now`) measures it.
  - **The answer is the retry decision:** `200 { state: "done" }`; `200 { state: "failed", errorCode }` for a permanent failure or any failure on the last attempt (the row already says `failed`); `503 { error }` for a retriable failure with attempts left (the row is `queued` again). The attempt number is `X-CloudTasks-TaskRetryCount` + 1 (missing or malformed counts as the first), and the last is `MAX_ATTEMPTS` = 3, which the queue's `maxAttempts` must equal (M5.17e). Answering 200 on the last attempt means a queue set to more attempts still can't re-run a row that's already failed.
  - **Signatures are accepted up to a day old** (`MAX_TASK_AGE_SECONDS`; `verifyRequest`'s new `maxAgeSeconds`, still at most 5 min in the future): a task waits for one of 2 workers and between retries, and a day is ~290 five-minute jobs per worker. Cloud Run's IAM (only the invoker account's OIDC token) is the first gate; the HMAC is the second, and replaying a captured push could only re-run an analysis of a commit already asked for.
  - **One job at a time:** a push that arrives while a job runs gets `429 BUSY` (Cloud Tasks retries it). Cloud Run's concurrency 1 should make this unreachable; the guard keeps two jobs from sharing one 4 GiB instance if the setting is ever wrong.
  - **Cancelled:** if the caller hangs up before the answer, or the process's shutdown signal fires, the job is killed and fails with the retriable `CANCELLED`.
  - **Refusals before any work** (`400`, `401`, `413`, `415`, `404`, `405`) are bugs or forgeries; Cloud Tasks retries them too, which costs a few operations and no compute.
  - **A fake Cloud Tasks** (`tasks.testkit.ts`) builds tasks as the website does and pushes them as Cloud Tasks does (retry count header, again on any non-2xx, up to `maxAttempts`); the tests run real HTTP against the handler.
- **Alternatives rejected:** 4xx for permanent failures (Cloud Tasks retries every non-2xx, so only 2xx stops it); one deadline for the child only (a slow download plus a full 5 min analysis overruns Cloud Run's timeout, which kills the instance mid-publish and retries the whole job); re-signing on each push (Cloud Tasks can't sign; a Cloud Function in front would be another service to run); keeping the 5 min signature window (any job that waited in the queue for 5 minutes would be refused).
- **Consequences:** `POST /run` exists beside the BullMQ worker; the entry point serves it in M5.17c2. Still open there: a duplicate push for a commit that's already `done` re-runs it (Cloud Tasks delivers at least once; `startAnalysis` returns `done` rows unchanged but the job doesn't check), `queued` rows a crashed function or an expired task left behind, the heap cap and `/tmp` in memory against 4 GiB, and where maintenance runs without an always-on process.

## ADR-0144: The worker process serves `POST /run`: no Redis in its config, the startup sweep by age, shutdown cancels the job
- **Status:** accepted (2026-10-31); M5.17c2a, issue #9, follows ADR-0143
- **Context:** M5.17c1 built `POST /run`, but the process still started the BullMQ worker and the enqueue API, needed `REDIS_URL`, and its startup sweep failed every `running` row that started before the process did. On Cloud Run, with up to two instances, a cold start beside a busy instance would mark that instance's live job `JOB_INTERRUPTED`.
- **Decision:**
  - **The entry point (`main.ts`) serves only `createRunServer`** on `HOST`:`PORT` (Cloud Run sets `PORT`; a container sets `HOST=0.0.0.0`), with the recorder, the counted store, time estimates, and the maintenance schedule as before. BullMQ's worker and the enqueue server are no longer started; their code goes in M5.17c3.
  - **No Redis:** `workerConfigFromEnv` no longer reads `REDIS_URL` or `REDIS_PASSWORD` (still set by `infra/oracle/compose.yml`, ignored), and `redisConnection` is gone.
  - **The sweep by age:** `failInterruptedAnalyses(db, runningForSeconds)` fails rows `running` for at least that long by the database's clock; the worker passes `INTERRUPTED_AFTER_SECONDS` = 360 (Cloud Run's 300 s request timeout ends every attempt sooner, plus a minute). A younger row may be the other instance's live job and is left alone. Job folders are still all deleted: `/tmp` is per instance.
  - **Unauthenticated `GET /health`** on the run server (`200 {"status":"ok"}`, nothing else), so the VM's container check and a Cloud Run startup probe work. Everything else still needs the HMAC first.
  - **Shutdown:** abort the server's shutdown signal (the job is killed and its push answered `503 CANCELLED`, so Cloud Tasks delivers it again), wait for that answer while the server closes, stop maintenance, close the database. An answer given while shutting down carries `connection: close`: otherwise `server.close()` waited ~3 s for the caller to drop its idle keep-alive socket (measured: 3,017 ms ⇒ 7 ms). `SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS` drops from 20 s to 8 s, under Cloud Run's 10 s between SIGTERM and SIGKILL.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a heartbeat column per instance (a write every few seconds for what the request timeout already bounds); keeping the sweep by process start time with one instance (ADR-0111 allows two); answering `/health` only with a signature (probes can't sign).
- **Consequences:** the whole process test (`main.test.ts`) now runs without Redis, locally too. A row whose instance died stays `running` until the next cold start or its task's retry restarts it; M5.17c2d's maintenance should fail such rows (and stranded `queued` ones) without waiting for a start. Still open in M5.17c2b–d: a duplicate push for a `done` row, sizing for 1 vCPU / 4 GiB, and maintenance without an always-on process.

## ADR-0145: A push for a job that's already `done` is answered `200` before any work
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.17c2b)
- **Context:** Cloud Tasks delivers at least once, and the website names each task after a queue entry, so the same commit can reach `POST /run` again after it finished. `startAnalysis` leaves a `done` row alone, but the push still called GitHub, downloaded the tarball, and ran the child: a whole job's share of Cloud Run's free allowance for nothing.
- **Decision:**
  - **`JobRecorder.done(jobId)`** (record.ts: `findJob(db, jobId)?.status === "done"`). The job ID is derived from the body (`jobIdFor`), the same one the website stored on the row, so no new column or index is needed.
  - **`POST /run` asks once a job may start** (signature, body, and the busy check passed), and answers `200 {"id","state":"done"}` with no GitHub call, download, or child; it logs `job_already_done`. A busy worker still answers `429` first, so the read never runs beside a job.
  - **A failed lookup runs the job as usual** (logged `done_check_failed`): the check only saves work, and `startAnalysis` still protects the `done` row.
- **Alternatives rejected:** skipping in `processJob` when `startAnalysis` returns `done` (it runs after GitHub's repo lookup and needs a second outcome type; a race that slips past the check costs one wasted run and nothing else); refusing such tasks at the website (it can't see Cloud Tasks' redeliveries).
- **Consequences:** a contract test (`apps/web/lib/cloudTasks.test.ts`) pushes a task built by the website's `createTask` to the worker's real handler: it's accepted under the website's job ID, and answered done when that row is. Two pushes racing on two instances can both run (neither row is `done` yet); concurrency 1 per instance and task names keep that rare.

## ADR-0146: One job fits a 4 GiB Cloud Run instance: 2,560 MB heap, 400 MiB unpacked, 60,000 entries
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.17c2c)
- **Context:** the Oracle VM gave the worker 12 GB and a 1,200 MB tmpfs for two jobs, so each analysis got a 3,072 MB heap and each tarball 500 MiB / 100,000 entries (ADR-0058, ADR-0059). A Cloud Run instance (ADR-0111) has 4 GiB for everything, and its `/tmp` is an in-memory filesystem that counts against the same 4 GiB. One job runs per instance (ADR-0143). At the old limits one job could need 3,072 + 500 + 100,000 pages of 4 KiB + the processes: about 4.9 GiB, and an instance over its memory is killed, with no `LIMIT_MEMORY` and no clear message.
- **Measured** (`pnpm bench:capped`, `bench/results/2026-11-01-capped-memory.md`; the child now reports its peak RSS and final heap): under the 3,072 MB cap the largest peak RSS was 2,523 MiB (VS Code's `src/vs/platform`, 44.8 MiB of source, the largest folder inside the 50 MB source limit), but its heap after serializing was 1,113 MiB, so most of the peak was garbage V8 had no reason to collect. Under a 2,048 MB cap cal.com and `src/vs/platform` both finished (peak RSS 1,993 and 2,139 MiB: about 90 MiB sits outside the old-space cap). The worker process at rest is 97 MiB; the largest graph was 53.5 MiB. cal.com's tree is 203 MiB as a tar stream (228 MiB in 4 KiB pages), VS Code's 325 MiB (374).
- **Decision:**
  - **Heap cap 2,560 MB:** 25% above the 2,048 MB under which both finished.
  - **Extraction 400 MiB and 60,000 entries.** VS Code (328 MiB, 25,233 entries) and cal.com still unpack and meet the inventory's limits as before. 60,000 entries is 3× a 20,000-source-file repo, while cal.com has 2.0 entries per source file and VS Code 1.7.
  - **The budget is code:** `apps/worker/src/budget.ts` adds the heap cap, 256 MiB for the child outside its heap, 256 MiB for the worker, 128 MiB for the graph file, the tree at the extraction limits (`maxBytes` + a 4 KiB page per entry: 634 MiB), and 256 MiB spare: 4,090 of 4,096 MiB. `budget.test.ts` fails if a limit grows past the instance, and checks the VM's limits don't fit.
  - **Recorded in production:** the child's `peakRssBytes` and `heapBytes` go into `analyses.stats`, so M5.13 can recalibrate on Cloud Run's 1 vCPU from real jobs.
- **Alternatives rejected:** keeping 3,072 MB and shrinking the tree to fit (128 MiB with its page rounding, less than cal.com's tree); writing only the files the analyzer reads (smaller tree, but extraction would have to know the analyzer's rules, and the skip warnings would differ from a checkout); a 2,048 MB cap (no margin over what was measured); 8 GiB instances (twice the GiB-seconds against the free allowance).
- **Consequences:** a repo between 400 and 500 MiB unpacked, or with 60,000–100,000 entries, now fails with `LIMIT_SIZE` / `LIMIT_ENTRIES` (the website's copy reads the new numbers from `apps/web/lib/limits.ts`, pinned to the worker's by a test). Repos that needed 2.5–3 GB of heap now fail with `LIMIT_MEMORY`; none measured did. Nothing caps the graph file: one over 128 MiB (about 2.4× the largest measured, relative to source) would eat into the spare. The measurements are from a loaded Windows laptop; Cloud Run's numbers come with M5.13.

## ADR-0147: Maintenance runs at the end of a job, claimed hourly in Postgres; stale `running` and `queued` rows fail there
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.17c2d); follows ADR-0078, ADR-0142, ADR-0144
- **Context:** the hourly maintenance (stale data, retention, rate-limit pruning, quota check; ADR-0078) ran on a `setTimeout` chain in the one always-on worker process. On Cloud Run (ADR-0111) there's no such process: instances scale to zero, there can be two, and with request-based billing an instance gets CPU only while a request is open, so a timer either never fires or fires throttled. ADR-0142 and ADR-0144 also left two kinds of stuck rows for it: `running` rows whose instance died (failed today only by the next cold start's sweep) and `queued` rows no task will run (a Vercel function died between the row and the task).
- **Decision:**
  - **Run at the end of a job, inside its request.** `POST /run` calls `afterJob` with what's left of the job's 280 s budget once the outcome is known and before answering (done, failed, retriable, or already done; not after a cancel). The entry point's trigger (`createMaintenanceTrigger`) starts a run only with at least `MAINTENANCE_MIN_TIME_LEFT_MS` (60 s) left, none already in progress on that instance, and the claim won; it waits at most the time left (or until shutdown or a hang-up) and answers then, leaving a longer run to finish unawaited (`maintenance_unawaited`). A push arriving meanwhile still gets `429`.
  - **The claim is one statement on the database's clock** (`claimMaintenance`, table `maintenance_runs`: name, started_at): an upsert that only updates when the last start is an hour old or more. Two instances racing reach the row lock; the second re-checks against the first's new time and gets nothing. A claim marks the start, so a run that dies midway waits out the hour like a finished one (each step is safe to abandon, ADR-0077).
  - **A first step fails stale job rows:** `running` for `INTERRUPTED_AFTER_SECONDS` (360 s, now in maintenance.ts; the startup sweep still uses it) ⇒ `JOB_INTERRUPTED`; `queued` since entering the queue for `STALE_QUEUED_AFTER_SECONDS` (a day, the signature window `MAX_TASK_AGE_SECONDS`, plus 10 min) ⇒ `QUEUE_UNAVAILABLE` (`failStaleQueuedAnalyses`). A task's signature dates from its creation, which follows the row's entry within seconds, and a retry's re-queue only moves `queued_at` later than the signature, so past that age no task made for the entry can be accepted. The code is the one the website gives a row whose task was never created (now `QUEUE_UNAVAILABLE_CODE` in `@gitinside/db`): retryable, and check-on-view queues it again.
  - **No run at startup or on a timer;** the schedule (`scheduleMaintenance`) is removed. With no jobs nothing runs: snapshots only grow with jobs, and what accumulates meanwhile (rate-limit windows, a stranded row) waits for the next job.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Cloud Scheduler calling an endpoint hourly (free for 3 jobs, but another service, IAM binding, and 720 cold starts a month for mostly nothing); always-allocated CPU or a minimum instance (billed around the clock, against the free allowance); running after the answer (CPU is throttled once the response is sent); before the job (delays the user's analysis); a shorter window for `queued` rows (a row with a live task would show "failed" while it waits; `startAnalysis` would revive it, but the page would mislead meanwhile); a lease with a heartbeat (one statement's claim needs no second write, and the run is idempotent).
- **Consequences:** maintenance can lag: after an idle spell the first job's end runs it, and an hour's quota alarm may come a job late. A stranded `queued` row inflates queue positions by one for up to a day. A run that outlasts its job's budget carries on with throttled CPU and may be killed with the instance; the next hour's run picks up. The quota command (`pnpm --filter @gitinside/worker quota`) is unchanged.

## ADR-0148: BullMQ, Redis, and the enqueue API removed; supersedes ADR-0014, ADR-0061's queue, and ADR-0082
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.17c3a); completes ADR-0114
- **Context:** since M5.17b–c2 nothing used the BullMQ side: the website creates Cloud Tasks tasks (ADR-0141) and reads job state from Neon (ADR-0139), and the worker process serves only `POST /run` (ADR-0144). What was left was dead weight with a cost: `queue.ts` and `api.ts` with their tests (16 of them skipped everywhere but CI), a Redis service in CI and in the Oracle compose stack, `bullmq` and `ioredis` in the image, and retention removing completed BullMQ jobs (ADR-0082) through an interface nothing supplied.
- **Decision:**
  - **Deleted:** `apps/worker/src/queue.ts` (the BullMQ queue, processor, and worker), `api.ts` (`POST /enqueue`, `GET /jobs/:id`, its `/health`), their tests, `bullmq`, `ioredis`, `supertest` (only those tests used it), CI's Redis service and `TEST_REDIS_URL`, and the Redis service, password file, and volume in `infra/oracle` (compose, `provision.sh`, `check.mjs`). Caddy now forwards `/health` and `/run`, with a write timeout past a job's 300 s.
  - **Kept, moved:** the processor's tests of what an attempt does (progress order, estimates, what was found, Postgres progress writes, failure codes) now test `runAttempt` directly (`attempt.test.ts`); `JobRequest` and `jobIdFor` come from `request.ts`; `WorkerLog` from `attempt.ts`.
  - **Retention** no longer removes jobs (`jobs` dependency and `removedJobs`/`failedJobRemovals` gone): a job is its `analyses` row, so deleting the row is the whole cleanup, and a later request for the commit queues a new row under a new task name (ADR-0141).
  - **The container check** asserts the stack's services are exactly `caddy`, `egress`, `worker` (plus CI's `postgres`), that an unsigned `POST /run` through Caddy is refused, and that the old `/enqueue` and `/jobs/*` paths are 404.
- **Alternatives rejected:** keeping BullMQ as a self-hosted fallback (it needs an always-on worker and a Redis, exactly what ADR-0111 gave up); removing `infra/oracle` now (M5.17d replaces it with `infra/gcp` and a Redis-free container check in one step; until then CI keeps checking the image through it).
- **Consequences:** a worker can only be fed by pushes, so local development needs something that pushes like Cloud Tasks (M5.17c3b). The analyzer's BullMQ *detector* (`packages/analyzer/src/queues.ts`) is unrelated and stays.

## ADR-0149: Local development pushes jobs straight to a loopback worker, like Cloud Tasks, behind an explicit opt-in
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.17c3b); completes ADR-0148's local-development consequence
- **Context:** since ADR-0148 the worker only takes pushes on `POST /run`, and the website only creates Cloud Tasks tasks, which needs a Google project, Workload Identity Federation, and a Vercel OIDC token (ADR-0141). Without one, a developer could run the website and the worker but never get a job from one to the other.
- **Decision:**
  - **`createLocalTaskQueue`** (`apps/web/lib/localTasks.ts`) implements the same `TaskQueue` as the Google client: the job JSON signed once at creation for `POST /run`, delivered with `X-CloudTasks-TaskRetryCount`, again after a backoff (1 s, 2 s) on any non-2xx answer or no answer, up to 3 deliveries (the queue's and the worker's `MAX_ATTEMPTS`), then dropped with a log line. `createTask` returns once the task is recorded, as Cloud Tasks does, so a worker that's down is a logged push failure, not a failed request.
  - **Task names dedupe in memory** for 24 hours (Google's used-name window); the database row stays the real dedupe, and the worker answers a `done` job at once (ADR-0145).
  - **One delivery at a time, in order:** a local worker runs one job and answers `429` to a second; the real queue allows 2 because there are up to 2 instances.
  - **Only on purpose, only locally:** `serverDeps` uses it only when no `GCP_*` variable is set at all (a half-configured Google queue stays "no queue", as before), `LOCAL_TASKS=push`, `WORKER_URL` is an http(s) origin on `localhost`, `127.0.0.1`, or `[::1]` (no credentials, no path), the HMAC secret is long enough, and `VERCEL` is unset. A full Google configuration always wins.
  - **Tests:** a fake worker for retries, order, the name window, and a stopped worker; a contract test pushing to the worker's real `createRunServer` (the same job ID, 503 until the last attempt, then 200); `serverDeps` tests for each condition.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a separate fake Cloud Tasks server process (one more thing to start, and it would still need the website to talk to a non-Google endpoint, i.e. the same switch); calling the worker synchronously from `/api/analyze` (the request would last the whole job, unlike production); accepting any `WORKER_URL` with the opt-in (a mistyped production setting could then push signed jobs to an arbitrary host); turning it on by `NODE_ENV` (`next start` is production locally too, and an implicit switch is easy to miss).
- **Consequences:** a dev server restart forgets task names and drops undelivered tasks; their rows stay `queued` until maintenance fails them (ADR-0147) or the next view queues them again after that. Next.js may load server modules once per bundle layer, so the queue is kept on `globalThis` (one per process, keyed by worker URL and secret), not in a module variable. The worker still needs a real bucket's `SNAPSHOTS_*` locally (its endpoint is built from them, ADR-0095); a development bucket, never production's.

## ADR-0150: The worker image carries its own hardening, and CI checks it as Cloud Run runs it
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.17d1); follows ADR-0074 and ADR-0111
- **Context:** on the Oracle VM, most of the worker's hardening was in `infra/oracle/compose.yml`: `user: 1000:1000`, a read-only root, a `noexec` tmpfs for job folders, all capabilities dropped, `no-new-privileges`, an init process, and the egress gateway. Cloud Run takes none of those flags. It starts the image's own `USER` and `CMD`, sets `PORT`, gives the container a memory limit and a writable in-memory filesystem, and sends SIGTERM to PID 1 with 10 s until SIGKILL. CI's container check (`infra/oracle/check.mjs`) still proved the image through the compose stack, including Redis-era and gateway checks that no longer describe production.
- **Decision:**
  - **What must hold on Cloud Run lives in the image.** `USER 1000:1000` by number (a platform can tell it isn't root without reading `/etc/passwd`); `HOST=0.0.0.0` set in the image (the worker's own default is loopback); and **nothing writable by that user outside `/tmp`**: after the app is copied in, everything uid or gid 1000 owns (the base image's `/home/node`) is handed to root, and every other world-writable path (`/var/tmp`, `/run/lock`) loses that bit. No package manager, as before. A read-only root can't be asked for on Cloud Run, so ownership is the control.
  - **The check runs the image as Cloud Run does** (`infra/gcp/check.mjs`): plain `docker run`, no hardening flags, no init, `PORT=9090` (so ignoring `PORT` fails), the `K_*` variables, 1 vCPU / 4 GiB without swap, `/tmp` as tmpfs (memory, as on Cloud Run), a throwaway Postgres on its own network, no Redis and no gateway. It checks the image config, health on `PORT`, that unsigned requests to any path are `401 MISSING_SIGNATURE` and signed ones reach routing (bad job 400, unknown path 404, `GET /run` 405), that the worker is PID 1 with uid/gid 1000 and no capabilities, a walk of the whole filesystem (but `/proc`, `/sys`, `/dev`, `/tmp`) as the worker finding nothing writable, a golden analysis by the real sandboxed child in the image, and a SIGTERM stop with exit 0 inside the 10 s grace with JSON logs.
  - CI's `worker-image` job runs only this check. The Oracle stack is no longer checked; M5.17d2 deletes it with `infra/gcp`'s service, queue, and IAM config.
- **Alternatives rejected:** keeping the compose check beside the new one until M5.17d2 (CI time on a stack nothing will deploy, and it would keep passing with hardening Cloud Run doesn't apply); `gcloud`'s local emulation or running the Cloud Run gen2 sandbox (neither exists for CI without an account); a read-only root through a distroless base (still not enforceable on Cloud Run, and the analysis child needs Node's full image layout under `/app`).
- **Consequences:** the image no longer depends on a compose file to be safe, so any host that runs it as built gets the same filesystem rules. What the platform adds or removes (seccomp, capabilities' bounding set, egress) isn't checked here; M5.17f reviews SECURITY.md §3 and T1–T9 for Cloud Run. Docker's tmpfs only approximates Cloud Run's in-memory filesystem: on Cloud Run every write counts against memory, not just `/tmp`, which ownership also limits to `/tmp`.

## ADR-0151: The worker's Cloud Run service, Cloud Tasks queue, and IAM are reviewed JSON in `infra/gcp`, tested against the code
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.17d2); follows ADR-0111, ADR-0114, ADR-0141, ADR-0143, ADR-0146, ADR-0150
- **Context:** the worker's Google pieces were spread across ADRs as numbers (max 2 instances, concurrency 1, 300 s, 1 vCPU / 4 GiB, 2 dispatches, 3 attempts, a 330 s dispatch deadline, a day's signature window) with nothing that would notice one drifting from another. `infra/oracle` (VM provisioning, compose, egress gateway, Caddy) described a host nothing will deploy to. Project `gitinside-513223` exists (#3); nothing is deployed yet, and the deploy itself is M5.17e.
- **Decision:**
  - **Three files, one per Google API's own shape:** `service.json`, a Knative `Service` (`gcloud run services replace` reads it, since JSON is YAML, and so does Cloud Run's v1 API); `queue.json`, Cloud Tasks' REST `Queue`; `iam.json`, the accounts, secrets, and a flat list of `{resource, role, member}` bindings. JSON rather than YAML so the test reads them without a parser dependency (the lockfile and every image stay unchanged); the README carries the comments.
  - **The service:** region `us-east4` (beside Vercel's `iad1` and Neon); `maxScale` 2, `minScale` 0, concurrency 1, 300 s, 1 vCPU / 4 GiB; CPU only during requests and no startup boost (both bill vCPU-seconds); gen2 (the Linux the image check reproduces, and faster CPU for a CPU-bound job); **ingress `internal`**, which Google documents as admitting Cloud Tasks from the same project on the `run.app` URL, so the internet can't reach it even before IAM; a startup probe on `/health` (≤ 60 s) and no liveness probe; its own runtime account. Plain variables (`SNAPSHOTS_*` but the secret key, the image) are `{{NAME}}` placeholders the deploy fills from GitHub Actions variables, so no tenancy name is committed.
  - **Secrets in Secret Manager:** `DATABASE_URL`, `SNAPSHOTS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY`, `ENQUEUE_HMAC_SECRET`, `GITHUB_TOKEN` (now effectively required: Cloud Run's outbound addresses are shared, so GitHub's anonymous 60 an hour would run out). Four fit the free tier's 6 active versions with one spare for an HMAC rotation; read at instance start, 4 of the free 10,000 accesses per cold start.
  - **The queue:** 2 concurrent dispatches (`maxScale` × concurrency), 1 per second, `maxAttempts` 3 with no `maxRetryDuration`, backoff 30 s then 60 s. Three 330 s deadlines plus both waits are 18 minutes, under 1/24 of the day the signature stays valid, so the rest is left for waiting behind other jobs. Every operation logged.
  - **IAM, each binding on one resource, none on the project:** `gitinside-worker` reads its four secrets; `gitinside-invoker` may invoke the service; `gitinside-enqueue` may enqueue on `analyses` and act as the invoker (needed to name it in a task's OIDC token); only Vercel's production principal set (pool `vercel`, `attribute.environment`) may become `gitinside-enqueue`. No basic roles, no `allUsers`. The pool, its provider, the project number, and the deploy account are M5.17e's.
  - **`apps/web/lib/gcpConfig.test.ts`** (web sees both sides: it imports the worker's entry and owns the dispatch deadline) checks the guardrails, `MAX_ATTEMPTS`, `JOB_BUDGET_MS` + 20 s ≤ the timeout < `DISPATCH_DEADLINE_SECONDS`, memory = `INSTANCE_MEMORY_MIB`, the retry span against `MAX_TASK_AGE_SECONDS`, the probe on `HEALTH_PATH`, that the service's variables satisfy `workerConfigFromEnv` with secrets only from Secret Manager, that the runtime account reads exactly the mounted secrets, and the IAM rules above. Raising `maxScale` to 20 and `maxAttempts` to 100 failed 4 of its 17 tests.
  - **`infra/oracle` deleted**, with SECURITY.md's references to its controls (T4's egress allowlist, §3's gateway, Caddy, and VM firewall, §4's env file) replaced by what holds now.
- **Alternatives rejected:** Terraform (state to store and a provider to pin for about a dozen resources, and its state bucket would be one more thing on a meter); YAML with comments (needs `yaml` in web's dependencies for the test); `gcloud` scripts as the source of truth (flags can't be tested against the code without parsing shell); ingress `all` (IAM would still refuse, but the service would answer the internet); gen1 (gVisor's syscall overhead on a job that writes and reads tens of thousands of small files under a fixed budget; both environments isolate instances); keeping `infra/oracle` as a fallback host (ADR-0111 dropped it; it would rot unchecked).
- **Consequences:** the worker process now reaches the internet with Cloud Run's ordinary egress; the analysis child still can't (`netguard.ts`), and M5.17f decides what, if anything, replaces the allowlist. The README's `gcloud` lines are for orientation; M5.17e's deploy applies these files and a `verify-guardrails` script reads the live settings back against them. Not verified until then: that gen2 and internal ingress behave as documented for this project.

## ADR-0152: On Cloud Run the analysis child's own guards are the network boundary; the worker process keeps ordinary egress
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.17f); follows ADR-0059, ADR-0075, ADR-0111, ADR-0150, ADR-0151
- **Context:** on the Oracle VM, two layers stood between untrusted input and the network: the analysis child's `netguard.ts` (in-process, "defense in depth") and the container's DNS and iptables egress allowlist (ADR-0075, "the boundary"). ADR-0111 said the platform's sandbox would replace the allowlist and asked for a fresh review of SECURITY.md §3 and T1–T9. Cloud Run changes three things: the worker's secrets are now its own environment variables (Secret Manager, ADR-0151) rather than a root-owned env file; a metadata server at `169.254.169.254` hands any process in the instance the runtime account's token; and nothing restricts the container's outbound network.
- **What was checked:** whether the child's in-process guard can be sidestepped through Node itself, under the same flags the runner uses (Node 22). `process.binding` (every binding: `tcp_wrap`, `udp_wrap`, `cares_wrap`, `fs`, `spawn_sync`, …), `inspector.open`, and `new WASI()` throw `ERR_ACCESS_DENIED` under `--permission`; `http2.connect` and a Unix-socket connect throw `ERR_NETWORK_DISABLED` (they go through the patched `net`/`tls`); reading `/proc/self/environ` or the parent's `/proc/<pid>/environ` is refused, since `/proc` is outside the read roots. No route was found short of a bug in Node's permission model or V8.
- **Decision:**
  - **The child's guards are the boundary for untrusted input:** the permission model (reads only the app and `src`, writes only the graph, no processes, threads, addons, bindings, inspector, or WASI), the empty environment, and `netguard.ts`. New probes in `runner.test.ts` pin the routes above, the metadata server's token URL, and the parent's environment under `/proc`; with the permission flags removed, all five internals probes fail (`ALLOWED`, or `ENOENT` instead of `ERR_ACCESS_DENIED` on Windows).
  - **The worker process keeps Cloud Run's ordinary egress.** It runs only our code and calls four fixed hosts. No outside allowlist is free on Cloud Run: Direct VPC egress sends traffic into a VPC that reaches the internet only through Cloud NAT, and firewall rules by hostname (FQDN objects) need Cloud NGFW; both are billed and ADR-0111 rules out NAT.
  - **Blast radius written down instead:** code running in the worker process could send its four secrets anywhere; the metadata token adds nothing (the runtime account only reads those secrets). So the worker's GitHub token must be a fine-grained one with read-only access to public repositories only, and no Google service-account key exists anywhere.
  - SECURITY.md: assets (the worker's token, no Google keys, the billing account), T1 (secrets in env, `/proc`, Google's per-instance sandbox), T3 (1 vCPU per instance, one job each, an out-of-memory instance retried and its row failed), T4 (rewritten), T8 (internal ingress), T9 (Cloud Run can bill after the trial; bounded, final numbers in M5.18f), §3 (no read-only root on Cloud Run, an outbound bullet), §4 (the token's scope). Stale comments in `netguard.ts`, `runner.ts`, `log.ts`, and `check.mjs`; SPEC §4's isolation line.
- **Alternatives rejected:** an in-process hostname allowlist in the worker process (it would guard our own code against itself; code that has taken over the process can undo a hook in it, and dependencies are T12's); Direct VPC egress plus Cloud NAT and IP-range firewall rules (billed, and Neon's and GitHub's ranges are broad and change); routing outbound traffic through a free proxy elsewhere (a new always-on host, the problem ADR-0111 left); running the analysis in a second, network-less Cloud Run service (Cloud Run has no "no egress" switch without a VPC either, and it doubles cold starts and the Tasks wiring).
- **Consequences:** one layer fewer than on the VM: a child escape is no longer stopped at the network. What remains is pinned by tests that fail if the permission flags go, and the worst case is a rotation of four secrets, none of which can read private data. Revisit if a free egress control appears on Cloud Run, or if private repos (M8) put installation tokens in the worker: those are short-lived, but they read private code.

## ADR-0153: "Too big" is remembered per repo for 7 days, ended early when GitHub's size halves
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.18a); follows ADR-0066, ADR-0146, issue #11
- **Context:** results are cached per commit, and a `LIMIT_*` failure (files, source size, tarball size or entries, time, memory) was only remembered for that commit. A repo that's too big and busy gets a new commit every few minutes, so anyone (or a README image's check-on-view) could make the worker download and parse it again each time: up to 5 minutes of a 1 vCPU / 4 GiB instance per ask, from a free allowance. The rate limits (5/h per IP, 6/h per repo on view) bound it, but not to zero.
- **Decision:**
  - **`findLimitFailure(db, providerRepoId, days)`** (`packages/db`): the newest `failed` analysis of the repo (by GitHub's ID, so a rename doesn't escape it; any commit or analyzer version) with an error code starting `LIMIT_` that finished less than `days` ago by the database's clock, unless an analysis of the repo finished `done` after it (the repo fits again). `until` = its `finished_at` + `days`.
  - **`findSizeHold`** (`apps/web/lib/sizeHold.ts`, `SIZE_HOLD_DAYS` = 7): that failure holds the repo unless GitHub's `size` now is at most half the size stored with the failed row. `analyses.repo_size_kb` (migration `0005`, `>= 0`) is GitHub's `size` when the website queued the row; null if GitHub didn't give a whole, in-range number or the worker inserted the row. Either size unknown ⇒ the hold stays (nothing guessed).
  - **Where it applies:** after the cache and dedupe checks, before any rate-limit slot or task. `/api/analyze` answers `422 REPO_TOO_BIG` with `retryAt` and `Retry-After`; check-on-view shows the last diagram with `behind`/`too-big`. A finished commit is still served and a job in flight keeps going: only new work stops. The message names the limit's reason (the same words as the failed job's page) and the date in UTC, so server and browser agree.
- **Alternatives rejected:** remembering on the `repos` row (a second write path from the worker, and it would need clearing when an analysis passes; reading `analyses` gives both from rows we already keep); any size drop rather than half (a repo that removed a few files would fail again at the same limit); GitHub's size alone as a pre-check (that's M5.18b, and `size` counts git history, so it can't tell a repo with a long history from one with a big tree); a longer hold (a repo that really did shrink but whose size GitHub didn't report would wait longer for no reason).
- **Consequences:** a too-big repo costs one GitHub lookup and one indexed query per ask for a week. `size` includes history, so a repo that halves its tree but not its history stays held until the week ends; that's the conservative side. Rows queued before this migration have no size, so their holds can't end early.

## ADR-0154: Huge repos refused before download by GitHub's language count, not its repo `size`
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.18b); follows ADR-0137, ADR-0153, issue #11
- **Context:** #11 asked for a check from GitHub's repo `size` that refuses obviously huge repos before any download or compute. Measured on 2026-11-01 (`gh api repos/<owner>/<repo>`): cal.com is 1,160,419 KiB and analyzes in 38 s (18.0 MiB of source); VS Code is 1,662,818 KiB and fails (201 MiB of source, `LIMIT_SOURCE_SIZE`, bench/results/2026-10-31-capped-vscode.md). `size` is mostly packed git history, so no threshold on it separates the two: one low enough to stop VS Code would stop cal.com too. GitHub's languages endpoint (`GET /repos/{owner}/{repo}/languages`, Linguist's bytes per language on the default branch, vendored and generated files left out) is close to what the analyzer limits: TypeScript + JavaScript is 221,753,543 bytes (211.5 MiB) for VS Code against the analyzer's 201 MiB, and 18,098,544 bytes (17.3 MiB) for cal.com against 18.0 MiB.
- **Decision:**
  - **`fetchCodeBytes`** (`apps/web/lib/github.ts`): the sum of `JavaScript`, `JSX`, `TSX`, and `TypeScript` in the languages answer; `undefined` when GitHub can't be asked or answers anything malformed (a non-integer, negative, or over-1 MB body).
  - **`findTooMuchCode`** (`apps/web/lib/codeSize.ts`): refused when that count is over `MAX_GITHUB_CODE_BYTES` = 2 × `SUPPORTED_SOURCE_MB` (100 MiB). The 2× margin is for Linguist's exclusions not being the analyzer's (`IGNORED_DIRS`, files over 2 MB, generated markers), so a repo refused here would fail `LIMIT_SOURCE_SIZE` anyway; a repo between 50 and 100 MiB by GitHub's count is still sent, and the worker's limits plus ADR-0153's hold stop it there in seconds (VS Code failed at inventory, 8 s, ADR-0137). Unknown ⇒ not refused (the worker's limits remain; nothing refused on a guess).
  - **Where:** right after ADR-0153's hold, only for new work (not a cached commit or one in flight), before any rate-limit slot or task. `/api/analyze` answers `422 REPO_TOO_BIG` without `retryAt` (nothing to wait for) and a message with GitHub's count, the supported size, and "Try a smaller repo."; check-on-view shows the last diagram with `behind`/`too-much-code`.
  - **Cost:** one more GitHub request per new analysis. Known counts are remembered per process by repo and commit (`createCodeSizeCache`, 1,000 entries), so a refused repo's page costs one count per new commit, not one per view.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a threshold on `size` (above); `size` as a ceiling far above cal.com (says nothing about the tree, which is what's downloaded and parsed, and would refuse a small app with a long history); refusing at the analyzer's own 50 MiB by GitHub's count (Linguist may count files the analyzer skips, so some repos that fit would be refused); fetching languages in `resolveRepo` for every view (a GitHub request per page view, even for repos never re-analyzed); remembering the refusal in the database (a commit's count doesn't change, and a cleanup in the next commit should be let in at once).
- **Consequences:** the VS Code case from #11 never reaches a download. GitHub recounts languages a little after a push, so a fresh commit can be judged by the previous commit's count; a repo that just shrank under the limit may be refused for a few minutes, and one that just grew past it reaches the worker's limits. A repo of mostly non-TS/JS (or over-2 MB) files can still be big to download; the extraction limits (ADR-0146) cover it.

## ADR-0155: One new analysis per repo per 30 minutes on `/api/analyze`, as a claim on the repo's row
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.18c); follows ADR-0066, ADR-0153, ADR-0154, issue #11
- **Context:** the per-address limit (5/h signed out) doesn't stop requests for one busy repo spread over many addresses: each finds a new commit, misses the cache, and starts a job. #11 asked for at most one new analysis per repo every 30–60 minutes on `/api/analyze`, whoever asks. Check-on-view already has its own fixed-window limit (6/h, `repo:<GitHub ID>` in `rate_limits`).
- **Decision:**
  - **A sliding claim, not a fixed window.** `repos.analysis_claim_sha` + `analysis_claimed_at` (migration `0006`, both null or both set, the SHA checked like every other). `claimRepoAnalysis` (`packages/db/src/repoClaims.ts`) sets them in one `update` on the database's clock when no claim stands (none, or older than the period); two racing requests for new commits meet at the row's lock, and one wins. A fixed 30-minute window in `rate_limits` would let two through a minute apart across a window boundary.
  - **The claiming commit isn't blocked by its own claim** (`same-commit`): a second request for it is deduped onto the same job, or retries its failed run, and is still counted against the asker's hourly limit. Otherwise two people pasting the same new commit at once would get one job and one refusal, though only one analysis runs.
  - **Order in `requestAnalysis`:** after the cache, dedupe, and both size checks (a refused repo claims nothing), then the claim, then the caller's hourly slot. A refusal is `429 REPO_RATE_LIMITED` with `retryAt` = claim + 30 min, `Retry-After`, and "Someone asked for a new analysis of this repo in the past 30 minutes, and each repo gets one every 30 minutes. Try again in N minutes." No slot, row, or task.
  - **Released** (`releaseRepoAnalysis`, only the claim it made, matched by its exact millisecond) when the caller is over its hourly limit or the queue was down. Kept when the commit was queued by a racing request or finished meanwhile: an analysis of the claimed commit did start.
  - **30 minutes** (`REPO_ANALYSIS_MINUTES`), the low end of #11's range: an active repo's diagram can catch up twice an hour on request, and check-on-view keeps viewers current between.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a fixed window in `rate_limits` (above; it would reuse the refund code, but not keep the "per 30 minutes" promise); a separate claims table (one more table to prune, while the repo row already exists for every repo we queue); counting check-on-view's runs in this claim (#11 scoped it to `/api/analyze`, and check-on-view's own limit already bounds it; together a repo gets at most 6 + 2 new analyses an hour); refusing the same commit's retry within the period (a transient failure would make everyone wait 30 minutes; the per-address limit and M5.18d's monthly budget bound repeated retries).
- **Consequences:** a repo pasted from many addresses costs one job per 30 minutes, plus one GitHub lookup and three small queries per refused ask. The repo row is now written before the hourly limit is checked, so a refused caller can leave a repo row with no analyses (harmless: pages and images read published analyses). Retries of one failing commit from many addresses are bounded by those addresses' hourly limits, not by this claim.

## ADR-0156: A monthly compute budget, counted by the worker and the website; new analyses pause at 80% of Google's free allowance
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.18d); follows ADR-0096, ADR-0111, ADR-0114, ADR-0155, issue #11
- **Context:** after its trial, Cloud Run bills whatever goes past the monthly free tier (SPEC §5 bounds the worst case at about $1 with a budget kill switch, M5.17e). The per-address, per-repo, and size limits (M5.18a–c) bound each caller and each repo, not the total: enough different repos and addresses could still use up the month. #11 asked for a global budget that stops new analyses before the allowance runs out, while diagrams keep working. Google's own billing data lags by hours and needs a Billing API reader the worker doesn't have, so GitInside counts its own use, as it does for the bucket's requests (ADR-0096).
- **The allowance** (Google's free-tier page, read 2026-11-01): Cloud Run with request-based billing, 180,000 vCPU-seconds, 360,000 GB-seconds, and 2 million requests a month; Cloud Tasks, 1 million operations (ADR-0114's figure; its pricing page didn't render to check again). With 1 vCPU and 4 GiB, memory runs out first: 4 GiB is 4.295 GB, so 83,819 instance-seconds (23.3 h) a month, and the pause comes at 18.6 h. The page says "GB-seconds"; counting 4 GiB as 4.295 GB pauses earlier if Google means GiB.
- **What's counted** (`compute_usage`, one row per UTC month, migration `0007`; `countComputeUsage` adds in one upsert, so concurrent processes never lose an increment):
  - **The worker** (`apps/worker/src/compute.ts`): Google's billing settings page says a request-billed instance is charged "when they process requests, when they start, and when they shut down." So once listening, each instance counts its process's age plus 10 s for its shutdown (SIGKILL comes 10 s after SIGTERM; counted up front because a count at shutdown may never land). Every answer except `/health`'s is preceded by a count of the request's time so far, one Cloud Run request, and one Cloud Tasks operation (the delivery), while the instance still has CPU (`run.ts`'s `meter`, after `afterJob`, so maintenance's time is in it). Each time is rounded up to 100 ms, at least one step.
  - **The website** (`enqueueAnalysis`): one Cloud Tasks operation per create call, answered or not (`ALREADY_EXISTS` is still a call).
  - A count that fails is logged (`compute_count_failed`) and never fails a job or an enqueue.
- **The pause** (`findComputePause`, `packages/db/src/computeUsage.ts`): when any of the four meters is at or past 80% (`COMPUTE_PAUSE_RATIO`), new analyses stop until the next UTC month. `/api/analyze` answers `503 ANALYSIS_PAUSED` with `retryAt`, `Retry-After`, and "Analysis is paused until Dec 1, 2026, 12:00 AM UTC: GitInside has used most of this month's free compute. Diagrams already made still work." No row, task, slot, or repo claim. Check-on-view shows the last diagram with `behind`, `paused`. It's checked after the size checks (a repo that's too big hears that, not a date when it still wouldn't run) and only for new analyses: cached diagrams, commits already in flight, and their retries go on.
- **The quota command and maintenance** (`measureQuotas`): the four meters join the bucket, database, and request readings, so `pnpm --filter @gitinside/worker quota` prints them and exits 2 when analyses are paused, and maintenance logs `quota_alarm`.
- **Alternatives rejected:** reading Google's billing (lags by hours, needs a Billing API role and BigQuery export, which isn't free to query at scale); counting only job durations from `analyses.stats` (misses startup, shutdown, refused and duplicate pushes, and maintenance after a job); a per-day budget (an idle month's allowance would go unused, and a busy day would stop analyses that the month could afford); pausing at 100% (the counts are estimates, and Google's billing month may not start at UTC midnight (Pacific time is believed, not verified), so ours can end hours earlier: the 20% margin covers both).
- **Consequences:** one more small query per new analysis. Time Cloud Run bills before the process starts (pulling and starting the container) isn't seen; the margin covers it, and M5.13's smoke test should compare a day's count with Google's billing report. The budget's kill switch (M5.17e) stays the hard stop; this pause is what keeps it from ever firing.

## ADR-0157: Cloudflare Turnstile on Analyze, asked for only when an address or the site is busier than people usually are
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.18e); follows ADR-0066, ADR-0155, ADR-0156, issues #11 and #12
- **Context:** #11 asked for a free bot check on Analyze for signed-out visitors (everyone, until M8's sign-in), "ideally only when traffic looks automated so normal visitors don't see it". Abhinav created a Turnstile widget in Managed mode (#12; keys in Vercel and `apps/web/.env.local`). The per-address, per-repo, size, and budget limits (M5.18a–d) already bound the damage; the check makes a bot that rotates addresses pay per request.
- **When it's asked for** (`passBotCheck`, `apps/web/lib/botCheck.ts`): only for a new analysis (never a finished diagram or a commit in flight), after the size checks and the pause, before the repo's claim and the caller's slot. It's needed once the caller's address has started 2 new analyses this hour (`CHALLENGE_AFTER_PER_ADDRESS`, of its 5), or the site has let through 20 requests for new analyses this hour (`CHALLENGE_AFTER_SITE`). Both are read from `rate_limits` windows with the new `peekRateLimit`: the address's is the hourly limit's own count, the site's a `site:analyze` counter that every request let past the check adds to. At 20 an hour the site is far above its expected traffic and would spend the month's compute (ADR-0156: ≈ 18.6 h of instance time) within days.
- **The exchange:** without a token the answer is `403 CHALLENGE_REQUIRED` with the widget's public site key and a reason ("You've started several analyses this hour…" or "GitInside is busier than usual…"). The form loads Turnstile's script then, never on page load, renders the widget (`appearance: "interaction-only"`, the site's theme, action `analyze`) and sends the same URL again with `turnstileToken`. The server checks it with `siteverify` (secret key, 5 s timeout) and requires `success` and the `analyze` action; Cloudflare refuses tokens used twice or older than 5 minutes.
- **Failing visibly:** a token that doesn't pass is `403 CHALLENGE_FAILED` ("…didn't pass. Press Analyze to try again."); Cloudflare unreachable or refusing our secret is `503 CHALLENGE_UNAVAILABLE`. In the browser, a widget error, timeout, or a script that won't load (blocked by an extension) is said in the form with what to do. Nothing is let through unchecked once a check is needed: it's only needed under suspicious traffic, and every other limit still applies.
- **Configuration:** `NEXT_PUBLIC_TURNSTILE_SITE_KEY` and `TURNSTILE_SECRET_KEY`, both or neither. Neither (local development, tests) asks nobody; only one makes `/api/analyze` "temporarily unavailable", like a half-configured queue, rather than running without the check it was meant to have. The site key reaches the browser in the `403` body, not inlined at build time.
- **CSP:** `frame-src https://challenges.cloudflare.com` (the widget's frame). The script tag is added by our own nonced code, so `'strict-dynamic'` covers it without a host source. Checked in a real browser on the production build with Cloudflare's test keys: the widget renders under the CSP with no violations, the always-pass key resubmits with its token, the always-block key shows the failure with focus back in the field (`docs/milestones/M5/bot-check/`).
- **Not checked:** the token's hostname (Cloudflare only issues the widget on its configured hostnames) and the visitor's IP (not sent to Cloudflare, which sees it anyway when the widget loads).
- **Alternatives rejected:** the check on every Analyze (#11 wanted normal visitors not to see it, and Managed mode still costs a script load and a round trip); failing open when Cloudflare is down (a bot could then pass whenever siteverify is slow; the check is only asked for under suspicion, when refusing is the safer side); counting refused requests toward the site's threshold (a visitor bouncing off the check would keep it on for everyone).
- **Consequences:** two small queries per new analysis (one for each count), plus one counter upsert. A busy hour's 21st visitor sees a sentence and, at most, a checkbox. The counts are fixed UTC-hour windows, so a burst straddling the hour gets up to twice the thresholds before the check turns on; the hourly limit and the repo's claim still bound it.

## ADR-0158: An env var's in-code fallback says what follows the env base and whether it points here
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5A.3a); changes ADR-0031's "the env var is the deployed value"; issue #13
- **Context:** the recall diagnosis (`eval/results/2026-11-recall-diagnosis.md`, R1) found 87 labels blocked, in part, by `env ?? fallback` being read as the env var alone. WealthWise's `process.env.NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL ?? "http://localhost:4000/api/v1"` lost `/api/v1`, so `/auth/login` matched nothing; foodie's `process.env.REACT_APP_FOODIE_API_VERSION || 'v1'` in the middle of the URL became a hole that never matches the literal `v1`; and foodie's base `REACT_APP_FOODIE_URL || 'http://localhost:9000'` isn't named like an API base, so it matched nothing either. EVALUATION.md §1 already labels such calls by the fallback. The fallback is the only value the repo itself states; the deployed value lives in an env file or a host's settings, which we never read (values may be secrets).
- **Decision** (`packages/analyzer/src/url.ts`, `match.ts`):
  - **The env part carries its fallback.** `env ?? x` and `env || x` read the right side too, as parts (`fallback`). Parameters read only in the fallback don't make the function a wrapper (`used` is restored), so wrapper detection is unchanged.
  - **At the start of a URL** the env var stays the base, and a fallback that is a literal URL or root-relative path adds its path in front of the rest (`{env:NEXT_PUBLIC_API_URL}/api/v1/auth/login`), a trailing slash kept so `"…/api/"` + `"users"` reads `/api/users`. Its host (lowercased, credentials dropped, a dynamic port as `{}`) is kept on the base as `fallbackHost`. A fallback whose path has a hole, a page-relative one, or one that isn't readable adds nothing: the old reading.
  - **Later in a URL** a fallback of text and holes takes the env var's place (`/api/${V || 'v1'}` ⇒ `/api/v1`); anything else is still a hole.
  - **`new URL("/x", env ?? fallback)`** keeps only the fallback's origin, as a root-relative URL drops a literal base's path.
  - **Matching:** an env base reaches this repo's routes (at `heuristic`, as before) when its name looks like an API base *or* its `fallbackHost` is a loopback host. A remote fallback with a non-API name still matches nothing. The pattern text is unchanged in form (`{env:X}/…`), so the diff's `parseHttpLabel` reads it as before.
- **Why the fallback, not the env var alone:** the code's default is evidence written in the repo; ignoring it threw away a path that every request through that base carries. It stays `heuristic`, the level that already says "assumes this env var points at this backend".
- **Risk, said plainly:** a fallback path that differs from the deployed one (dev `/api/v1`, production `/v2`) now gives a confident-looking `heuristic` edge to the dev route where before it gave `NO_MATCHING_ROUTE`. We accept it: the route exists in the repo, the confidence says it's an assumption, and the label rule makes the eval measure the same thing.
- **Alternatives rejected:** reading `.env.example` values (not "never read env values", and examples drift from the code just as much); showing the fallback in the pattern text (`{env:X ?? http://localhost:4000}`): longer node labels, and the diff's label parser and every golden would change for no matching difference.
- **Measured:** original five TP 28 ⇒ 32, FP 1 ⇒ 1, FN 110 ⇒ 106 (WealthWise's 4 direct `fetch` calls); held-out repos unchanged. The other R1 labels also need R2 (WealthWise's `apiClient` methods) or R3–R5 (foodie), as the diagnosis said. Fixture `env-fallback`.

## ADR-0159: The methods of an exported `const` object literal are symbols, so they can be wrappers
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5A.3b); extends ADR-0023 and ADR-0032; issue #13
- **Context:** the recall diagnosis (R2, 44 labels, all WealthWise) found `export const apiClient = { async get(endpoint, params) {…}, async post(endpoint, body) {…}, … }`, each method calling `fetch(\`${BASE_URL}${endpoint}\`)`. Wrappers are symbol nodes (ADR-0032), and ADR-0023 made symbols of class methods and `module.exports = { … }` members but not of an ES-module object's methods. So each method body was one `DYNAMIC_URL` request and the 44 hook calls (`apiClient.get("/accounts")`) got nothing. An object of request helpers is as common a client shape as a class.
- **Decision** (`packages/analyzer/src/symbols.ts`): a top-level `const` that is exported (modifier or local `export { … }` list) and holds an object literal (through `as`/`satisfies`/parentheses) yields a `method` symbol for each method declaration with a body and each property whose value is a function or arrow: ID `apiClient.get`, parent the `apiClient` variable symbol, exported, `requiredParams` as for class methods. The resolver then maps `apiClient.get(…)` to it through the checker's property declaration, and `wrappers.ts` inlines it with no change of its own. The GET method's `const url = new URL(…)` + `url.searchParams.set(…)` + `fetch(url.toString())` already normalized (the query is dropped); the fixture checks it.
- **Not followed:** `let`/`var` objects (a property could be reassigned), unexported objects (a module-private helper object stays as before, its sites `DYNAMIC_URL`), getters and setters, shorthand (`{ get }`: the function is already a symbol), spreads, computed names, nested objects, and `export default { … }`.
- **Consequences:** graphs gain symbol nodes and `calls` edges for such objects, and calls made inside a method now belong to the method rather than to the object's variable. No existing fixture golden changed. Fixture `object-client`.
- **Measured:** original five TP 32 ⇒ 76, FP 1 ⇒ 1, FN 106 ⇒ 62 (precision 97.0% ⇒ 98.7%, recall 23.2% ⇒ 55.1%); WealthWise 48/48. Held-out repos unchanged (75.4% / 55.1%).

## ADR-0160: A wrapper whose parameter is the whole axios config reads it from the caller
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5A.3c); extends ADR-0032 and ADR-0159; issue #13
- **Context:** the recall diagnosis (R3, 39 labels, all foodie) found `const httpRequest = (req: AxiosRequestConfig) => new Promise(async (resolve, reject) => { … await axios(req) … })`, called as `httpRequest({ method: "POST", url: "/authenticate", data })`. Two gaps stopped it: a config the collector can't see (`axios(req)`) had no URL at all, so the site was one `DYNAMIC_URL` request inside the helper; and the request sits in a callback the wrapper declares (the Promise executor), so the helper wasn't treated as its wrapper. A request helper taking the whole config is a common axios shape.
- **Decision** (`http.ts`, `wrappers.ts`):
  - An `axios(config)` / `x.request(config)` site whose config isn't an object literal or const object keeps the config expression and the instance's `baseURL` on the site (`config`).
  - When such a config is a parameter of the wrapper, each caller's argument gives `url`, `baseURL` (none: the instance's), and `method` (none: `GET`), read from an object literal or a const object as any config is. A caller passing its own parameter on is `WRAPPER_DEPTH` (one level, as before); a spread, or no `url`, gives no URL (`DYNAMIC_URL`, never guessed).
  - A wrapper is the innermost symbol around the site whose value is a function containing it, through callbacks it declares; only that function's parameters are bound.
  - An unresolved request shows only the caller's `url` and `baseURL` text; the rest of the config (`data`, headers) is masked as before.
- **Not followed:** a config parameter read through properties (`axios({ url: config.path })`), a `baseURL` in a shorthand call's config (`api.get(path, config)`), destructured config parameters, and axios instances passed as parameters.
- **Consequences:** no existing fixture golden changed. Fixture `config-wrapper`.
- **Measured:** original five unchanged at TP 76, FP 1, FN 62 (precision 98.7%, recall 55.1%): foodie's 39 sites are now found (missing call sites 59 ⇒ 20), but 38 are `NO_MATCHING_ROUTE` and one (`/${username}`) `DYNAMIC_URL`, because its base comes from `axios.defaults.baseURL` (R4) and its routers are mounted from a folder listing (R5), as the diagnosis said. Held-out repos unchanged.

## ADR-0161: `defaults.baseURL` counts when it is written once, at a module's top level, on one object
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5A.3d); extends M2.1's axios instance tracing (`http.ts`); issue #13
- **Context:** the recall diagnosis (R4, 39 labels, all foodie) found `axios.defaults.baseURL = \`${foodieUrl}/api/${foodieApiVersion}\`` at the top of `services/fetcher.ts`, and wanderlust does the same on an instance (`const axiosInstance = axios.create(); axiosInstance.defaults.baseURL = BASE_URL;`). `http.ts` read a base only from `create` configs, so these requests were matched as if they had no base: `/authenticate` instead of `/api/v1/authenticate`. A `defaults` write is a runtime mutation, so the rule must say exactly when its value is certain.
- **Decision** (`http.ts`):
  - Every write to `x.defaults.baseURL` (also `x.defaults["baseURL"]`) or to `x.defaults` itself, where `x` is axios or an instance, is collected from every program once, keyed by the object written: the default export per service program (each service is its own runtime), or the `create` call.
  - The base is the written value only when there is exactly one write, it is a plain `=` expression statement at a module's top level, and the object is one object: the default export, or an instance created outside any function or class. It replaces the `create` config's `baseURL`; a per-call `baseURL` still wins.
  - Any other write makes the base unknown (`null` ⇒ `DYNAMIC_URL`, never guessed): a second write, one inside a function (it may run any number of times, or never), `+=`, `x.defaults = …`, and an instance a factory or a class makes per call.
  - `create()` without its own `baseURL`, on axios or an instance whose defaults are written, is unknown too: `create` copies the defaults when it runs, and load order isn't modeled.
- **Why not more:** following load order (import graph + statement order) would decide the `create()` case and a top-level request before the write, but it's a module-execution model for a rare shape; unknown is honest and visible. A write inside a function (a `setBaseUrl(url)` helper) can't be known statically at all.
- **Not followed:** `Object.assign(x.defaults, …)`, a `defaults` object held in a variable, interceptors rewriting URLs; these leave the `create` config's base (as before). Writes in test files count like any other (a second write makes the base unknown).
- **Consequences:** a base that was assumed absent while a write existed inside a function is now unknown, so such requests move from a (possibly wrong) match to `DYNAMIC_URL`. No existing fixture golden changed. Fixture `axios-defaults`.
- **Measured:** original five unchanged at TP 76, FP 1, FN 62 (precision 98.7%, recall 55.1%). foodie's requests now carry `/api/v1` from its `axios.defaults.baseURL`, so its one `DYNAMIC_URL` (`/${username}`, a leading hole without the base) is now `NO_MATCHING_ROUTE` like the other 38: its server's routes are still found as `/v1/…` because they're mounted from a folder listing (R5, M5A.3e). Held-out repos unchanged.
- **Amended (2026-11-02, M5A.5d, M5A review):** a write applied to every request in its service, so a test or e2e setup file's `axios.defaults.baseURL = "http://localhost:…"` rebased the whole app's requests, or (beside the app's own write) made them all unknown. Now a request sees only the writes in files its own file reaches through imports, transitively (every specifier `extractImports` finds, resolved like import edges, across workspace packages; `SymbolResolver.importedFiles`). The rules above then apply to the writes it sees: none ⇒ the `create` config's base, exactly one trusted ⇒ its value, anything else ⇒ unknown; `create()` without a `baseURL` is unknown when it sees a write to the parent's defaults. **Not modeled:** an entry point that imports both the write and the code making requests (`main.ts` importing `./setup` and `./App`) without `App` importing `setup`: those requests keep the `create` config's base. The write isn't guessed into them, but its absence is assumed; this is the price of not rebasing an app from its tests, and it stays documented in `http.ts`. Regression test in `http.test.ts` (an app write reached through a re-importing module, a test setup's writes to the default export and to an instance, a test file reaching both). No fixture golden changed.

## ADR-0162: Routers loaded from a folder listing are mounted from the repo's own files, at `heuristic`
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5A.3e); extends ADR-0093 (Express facts) and DESIGN.md §7.4 (matching); issue #13
- **Context:** the recall diagnosis (R5, 39 labels, all foodie) found `routes/createRouter.ts` building one router from `glob.sync(\`**/*.${env === 'dev' ? 'ts' : 'js'}\`, { cwd: \`${__dirname}/\` }).map((f) => require(\`./${f}\`)).filter((r) => r.default && Object.getPrototypeOf(r.default) === Router).reduce((root, r) => root.use(r.default), Router())`, mounted at `/api`. The Express detector never linked the listed routers to that mount, so every route kept its router-relative path (`/v1/authenticate`) with an `EXPRESS_UNMOUNTED_ROUTER` warning. The same idea appears as `fs.readdirSync(__dirname).forEach((f) => app.use(require(path.join(__dirname, f))))`. The question was whether this can be composed without executing anything.
- **Decision:** yes, because a folder listing of the repo is a read, not a run. In `express.ts` (collection) and `listings.ts`:
  - **Listings:** `readdirSync(dir)` (`fs`, `node:fs`, `fs-extra`; names only) and `sync`/`globSync(pattern, { cwd })` (`glob`, `fast-glob`; `*`, `**`, `?`, `{a,b}`, dot files skipped) are computed from the service's own root files, so they're already among its observed inputs (ADR-0090). The folder must be a repo path: `__dirname`, `path.join`/`resolve`, templates, `+`, consts, and `a ? b : c`, whose alternatives are all listed (both `ts` and `js` above; only files that exist match).
  - **Chains and loops:** `.filter` and `.sort` pass through unread; `.map(require)` and `.map((f) => require(…))` load each listed file; `.forEach`, `.reduce((acc, x) => acc.use(x), initial)` (which also makes the reduction stand for `initial` wherever it's mounted), and `for (const f of list)` mount what each element names. A file never lists itself in.
  - **What's mounted:** a module itself mounts its `module.exports` / `export =`, `require(f).default` (or another name) mounts that export, each only when it is a router. Prefixes may use the file name (`path.basename`, string `replace` and `slice`, templates); a prefix built any other way makes those routes `DYNAMIC_ROUTE`.
  - **Confidence:** routes are still deterministic, but filters aren't evaluated (foodie's prototype check, `file !== "index.js"`), so a listing can mount a router the code skips. A route reached only through a listed mount is in `ExpressRoutes.listedRoutes`, and the route matcher matches it at `heuristic` at best. A route also mounted in code keeps its normal level.
- **Why not more:** evaluating filter callbacks would mean interpreting arbitrary code (and regexes, a ReDoS risk on untrusted patterns); string `replace` with a regex is refused for the same reason. Async listings, `withFileTypes`, folders from env or `process.cwd()`, a glob without `cwd` (the process's start folder), extglobs and classes, and files in other services stay unhandled: their routers stay unmounted with the warning, visibly.
- **Consequences:** no existing fixture golden changed. Fixture `express-folder-routers` (glob + reduce, readdirSync + forEach with a file-name prefix, a non-router file listed, `exact` beside `heuristic` from one client).
- **Measured:** original five unchanged at TP 76, FP 1, FN 62 (precision 98.7%, recall 55.1%); held-out unchanged. foodie's 10 listed routers are now mounted on `createRouter`'s root router (10 `EXPRESS_UNMOUNTED_ROUTER` warnings ⇒ 1, the root router), but its 45 routes still read `/v1/…`: `app.ts` mounts the root router on `this.app`, a class property declared without an initializer and assigned in the constructor (`this.app = express()`), which the detector doesn't follow (listed as unhandled since M1.10). The diagnosis missed this cause; it's M5A.3e1.

## ADR-0163: Without workspace config, top-level folders with their own `package.json` are services
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5A.3f); extends M1.4's single-package fallback (`workspace.ts`); issue #13
- **Context:** the recall diagnosis (R6, 15 labels, all wanderlust) found `frontend/` and `backend/` side by side, each with its own `package.json` and tsconfig, under a root `package.json` with no `workspaces`. The whole repo became one service, `app`, analyzed with default compiler options (`TSCONFIG_MISSING`), so `frontend/tsconfig.json`'s `@/*` alias never resolved, the imported axios instance was unknown, and its 15 calls weren't collected. foodie, conversa, online-ide, and mern-ecommerce have the same layout. It's the common "client + server in one repo" shape that needs no workspace tool.
- **Decision:** when the repo declares no workspace config at all (no npm/yarn `workspaces` patterns, no `pnpm-workspace.yaml` or `lerna.json` packages, no Nx), every **top-level** folder with its own `package.json` is a service, keyed and named like a workspace package (folder name; `package.json` `name` for the label and imports). Each gets its own program, so its own tsconfig, `paths`, and `baseUrl`. The root stays a service beside them when it holds source files outside them (a server at the root with a `client/` package, or wanderlust's `scripts/postInstall.mjs`); otherwise it isn't one.
- **Why only top-level folders:** a deeper `package.json` without a workspace tool is usually a vendored example, a test fixture, or a tool's sub-package that belongs to the app around it; top-level siblings are the shape that's meant as separate apps. Deeper folders stay part of the service that contains them (documented as unhandled). A workspace config that matches nothing still falls back to one root service, as before: the repo said what its packages are.
- **Consequences:** service keys change for repos of this shape (`app` ⇒ `frontend`, `backend`, …), so their node IDs do too; diffs across this change show those services as replaced, not renamed. A frontend calling its backend with a root-relative URL now crosses services, so those matches are `heuristic` (a proxy is assumed, `match.ts`) where they were `exact` inside one `app`. No existing fixture golden changed. Fixture `sibling-apps` (two siblings, a `paths` alias in one, a root script that keeps the root a service).
- **Measured:** original five TP 115 ⇒ 106, FP 1 ⇒ 14 + 1, FN 23 ⇒ 32 (precision 99.1% ⇒ 87.6%, recall 83.3% ⇒ 76.8%); held-out unchanged (43 / 14 / 35). The drop isn't this change being wrong, it's two later causes it uncovers:
  - **conversa 24/24 ⇒ 15/24, 14 FP (R10, M5A.3j):** its `lib/api.ts` methods (`get: (id) => fetch(\`${API_BASE}/conversation/${id}\`)`) were imported through `@/lib/api`, which never resolved, so each request stayed at its site in `api.ts`, as labeled. With `frontend/tsconfig.json` loaded, the callers resolve, and any parameter reaching the URL makes a wrapper, so the 9 requests whose parameter fills a *later* hole move to their 14 callers. R10 (only a parameter in the URL's leading part makes a wrapper) fixes exactly this; it moves up to be the next task.
  - **wanderlust still 0/22, but seen (R7, M5A.3g):** the 15 `axiosInstance` calls are now collected (missing sites 18 ⇒ 3), with the base `{env:VITE_API_PATH}` from `defaults.baseURL` (ADR-0161), and are `unresolved NO_MATCHING_ROUTE` because `VITE_API_PATH` isn't an API-base env name, as the diagnosis predicted.

## ADR-0164: Only a parameter in the URL's leading part makes a function a wrapper
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5A.3j); narrows M2.3's wrapper rule (`wrappers.ts`, `leadingHoles` in `url.ts`); issue #13
- **Context:** since M2.3, a function was a wrapper when its `fetch`/axios call's URL read *any* of its parameters, and each call to it became the request. The recall diagnosis (R10) and M5A.3f showed what that does to the most common API-client shape, a helper that fixes its route and takes an ID (`get: (id) => fetch(\`${API_BASE}/conversation/${id}\`)`, taxonomy's `deletePost(postId)`, a query-only `?key=${apiKey}`): the request moved from the helper to every caller. EVALUATION.md §1 labels such a request at the helper's own call, and only "a helper that takes the path as a parameter" at its callers; the diagram reads the same way (the client module talks to the route, its callers call the client). conversa lost 9 labels to 14 caller sites, taxonomy 1, mern-ecommerce (held-out) 14.
- **Decision:** a parameter makes the function a wrapper only when its hole reaches the leading part of the URL or the axios `baseURL`, each read on its own (axios's join adds a `/`): before any literal `?` or `#`, and at the start, in the host or port, right after the base (env var, page origin, literal origin), glued to the path's text (`/api${path}`, `https://h/v1${endpoint}`: the argument brings its slash, unless its segment already has a hole), or as the first path segment (`${API}/${resource}`). A parameter after a literal segment and a `/` (`/posts/${id}`) or in the query stays a hole at the helper's site. A parameter whose position the normalizer can't see (absorbed by `new URL(path, base)` or an unequal ternary) counts as leading, as before. A config parameter (`axios(req)`, ADR-0160) and a method parameter still make a wrapper.
- **Why positions, not intent:** nothing in the code says "path" versus "ID"; where the hole sits is the only static signal, and it matches how the two shapes are written. Two shapes read wrongly and are documented: a parameter glued inside a segment (`/posts/post-${id}`) reads as a path, and the first segment after a literal base's own path (`"https://h/api/" + resource`) as an ID. Either way the URL still resolves, only the site the request is placed at differs.
- **Consequences:** no existing fixture golden changed. Fixture `fixed-url-helpers` (a fixed-route function, an object client with an ID and a query-only parameter, and a path-taking helper). Callers of a fixed-route helper keep their `calls` edge to it; the `http` edge leaves from the helper.
- **Measured:** original five TP 106 ⇒ 116, FP 15 ⇒ 0, FN 32 ⇒ 22 (precision 87.6% ⇒ 100.0%, recall 76.8% ⇒ 84.1%): conversa 15 ⇒ 24/24, taxonomy 4 ⇒ 5/5, and WealthWise's query-only Gemini call is now a correct abstention at its own site. Held-out (never tuned against; the rule came from the original repos' diagnosis) TP 43 ⇒ 57, FP 14 ⇒ 0, FN 35 ⇒ 21 (75.4% ⇒ 100.0%, 55.1% ⇒ 73.1%): mern-ecommerce 25 ⇒ 39/39, and online-ide's GitHub call is a correct abstention.

## ADR-0165: API-base env names come from a documented list of endings
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5A.3g); widens M2.4's env-base rule (`API_BASE_ENV_ENDINGS` in `match.ts`, DESIGN.md §7.2); issue #13
- **Context:** an env-var URL base is never read (its value may be a secret, and it isn't in the repo), so whether `{env:X}/api/posts` may point at this repo's routes rests on the name alone, or on a loopback fallback in the code (ADR-0158). Since M2.4 only `*_API_URL` and `*_BASE_URL` counted. The recall diagnosis (R7) found wanderlust's whole frontend on `VITE_API_PATH`: 18 requests that normalize fine were `NO_MATCHING_ROUTE`. Other common spellings of the same idea (`VITE_API`, `NEXT_PUBLIC_API_HOST`, `REACT_APP_BACKEND_URL`, `SERVER_URL`) had the same fate.
- **Decision:** a name counts when it is one of these endings, alone or after a prefix ending in `_`: `API`, `API_URL`, `API_URI`, `API_PATH`, `API_HOST`, `API_ORIGIN`, `API_ROOT`, `API_BASE`, `API_ENDPOINT`, `BASE_URL`, `SERVER_URL`, `BACKEND_URL`. Case-sensitive (env names are upper case by convention). The match stays `heuristic`, and the path must still align with a route. Not on the list, on purpose: a bare `*_HOST` or `*_URL` (`ANALYTICS_HOST`, `CMS_URL`: most hosts a frontend reads are someone else's), `*_APP_URL` (often the site's own origin, for links), `*_BASE_PATH` (Next's page prefix), and API settings that aren't a base (`*_API_KEY`, `*_API_VERSION`). `RAPIDAPI` or `MAPI_URL` don't match: the ending must start the name or follow `_`. The unresolved detail now reads "isn't named like an API base (\*_API_URL, \*_API_PATH, …)", short enough for a box's subtitle.
- **Why a list, not "contains API":** the endings say "where the API is"; a name merely containing `API` (`STRIPE_API_KEY`, `API_TIMEOUT`) doesn't. A wrong name still needs a route whose path aligns, so the cost of a too-wide list is a `heuristic` edge to a real route, never an invented node; the cost of a too-narrow one is an unresolved node with this detail. The list came from the diagnosis (`*_API_PATH`, `*_API_HOST`, `*_API`, `*_SERVER_URL`, written in M5A.1, before any held-out repo was picked) plus the spellings in the same family (`URI`, `ORIGIN`, `ROOT`, `BASE`, `ENDPOINT`, `BACKEND_URL`).
