- **Consequences:** five fixture goldens change only in that detail text (`shop-demo`, `env-base-heuristic`, `env-fallback`, `diff-rename-base`/`-head`); no edge changes in any fixture. Across all eight eval repos, the only env bases the list newly accepts are wanderlust's `VITE_API_PATH` (18 sites) and online-ide's `SERVER_URL` (held-out, 19 sites).
- **Measured:** original five TP 116 ⇒ 133, FP 0 ⇒ 1, FN 22 ⇒ 5 (precision 100.0% ⇒ 99.3%, recall 84.1% ⇒ 96.4%): wanderlust 0 ⇒ 17/22. Held-out TP 57 ⇒ 58, FP 0 ⇒ 17, FN 21 ⇒ 20 (precision 100.0% ⇒ 77.3%, recall 73.1% ⇒ 74.4%). 17 of the 18 new FPs (wanderlust's 1, 16 of online-ide's) are the labeled route reported at the line of `.get(` in an `axios⏎ .get(…)` call instead of the call's first line, so each scores as an FP plus an FN; that's R9 (M5A.3i), not a wrong link. The 18th is a real wrong candidate: online-ide's `` `${SERVER_URL}/user/submissions${page ? "?page=" + page : ""}` `` reads as a last segment `submissions{}` (a ternary with unequal branches is a hole), which aligns with `/user/submissions` and `/user/:id` alike, so the request links to both as `AMBIGUOUS`; reading the ternary's alternatives (R8, M5A.3h) would see that both are `/user/submissions`. online-ide's one TP is its one single-line call (`Code.jsx:364`).

## ADR-0166: A URL the code picks from fixed values is one request per value
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5A.3h); extends M2.2's normalizer (`alternatives` in `url.ts`, new `lets.ts`, `wrappers.ts`); implements ADR-0041's "several labels at one call site" on the analyzer side; issue #13
- **Context:** since M2.2 a ternary whose branches differ was one hole, and a `let` was never read, so `axios.get(API + (featured ? "/api/posts/featured" : \`/api/posts/categories/${c}\`))` and `let url = \`/api/posts/${id}\`; if (admin) url = \`/api/posts/admin/${id}\`;` were `DYNAMIC_URL` (the hole leads the path), though the code names every URL it can send. The recall diagnosis (R8) found 4 such labels in wanderlust; ADR-0041 already labels each route such a call reaches. A hole also misled the matcher when the branches differ only in the query (`/user/submissions${page ? "?page=" + page : ""}` read as a segment `submissions{}`, `AMBIGUOUS` with `/user/:id`).
- **Decision:**
  - **Paths, not a new part kind.** `alternatives(site, bindings?)` re-runs the same normalization once per combination of choices: each ternary, and each local `let` read where several values may reach it, is a choice point; the first run takes option 0 everywhere and records how many options each point had, then the next run advances the last point with an option left (depth-first, so a choice reached only inside one branch is enumerated only there). Results that read the same are kept once, in path order. Each result becomes its own request at the same site (own sites and inlined wrapper calls alike), and `buildHttpEdges` already merges those into one edge per target with that site, sharing one unresolved node if several are unresolved.
  - **At most 8 paths** (`MAX_PATHS`) per site; past that, `normalize`'s single reading (unequal options are one hole), so a URL built from many independent ternaries can't multiply requests.
  - **`let` values (`lets.ts`)** are the reaching definitions in the declaring function's statements, read structurally: a plain `x = v;` statement replaces the values before it; an `if` adds its branch's values (both branches writing replace them; a branch ending in `return`/`throw`/`break`/`continue` adds nothing after it); a write inside a loop, `switch`, `try`, or label that comes before the read adds its values. Anything this can't place makes the variable unknown, as before: `var`, exported `let`s, `+=`/`++`, destructuring and `for…of` targets, an assignment used as a value, a write or read in a nested function, and a read inside a loop (or `switch`/`try`/label) that also writes it. A `let` without an initializer starts with no value, and a path that never writes it adds none (it would send `undefined`).
  - **`normalize` keeps one reading** (wrapper detection and parameter lookup use it): options that read the same are that value, otherwise one hole. So a `let` with one reaching value now reads like a `const` everywhere.
- **Why every option is an edge:** each is a request the code really makes from that call under some condition, and the condition isn't known statically; picking one would hide a real edge, and a hole hides both. An option that can't be read (`flag ? "/a" : build()`) stays visible as its own unresolved node beside the readable one (fail visibly).
- **Alternatives rejected:** an `alt` part in `UrlPart` (every function over parts, `mergeParts`, `toUrlPattern`, `joinBaseURL`, `leadingHoles`, would need to branch); returning the cartesian product eagerly (exponential before any cap); a full control-flow graph for `let`s (TypeScript's isn't public API, and the structured rules cover the shapes seen).
- **Consequences:** no existing fixture golden changed. Fixture `url-alternatives` (a ternary in a `const`, a `let` set in an `if`, a query-only ternary, a wrapper called with a ternary).
- **Measured:** original five TP 133 ⇒ 135, FP 1 ⇒ 3, FN 5 ⇒ 3 (precision 99.3% ⇒ 97.8%, recall 96.4% ⇒ 97.8%): wanderlust 17 ⇒ 19/22, its `details-page.tsx:44` `let` giving both DELETE routes. Its `blog-feed.tsx:23` ternary now predicts both labeled routes, but at line 24 (`axios⏎ .get(`), so they score as 2 FP + 2 FN until R9 (M5A.3i). Held-out TP 58, FP 17 ⇒ 20, FN 20 (precision 77.3% ⇒ 74.4%, recall unchanged 74.4%): online-ide's query-only ternary now links only `/user/submissions` (the wrong `AMBIGUOUS` `/user/:id` candidate is gone), and its two route-choosing ternaries (`Code.jsx:193`, `:248`) predict both labeled routes each, one line off like the rest of that repo (R9). Every new prediction is a labeled route; every new FP is that anchoring.

## ADR-0167: An `http` call site is the start of the call expression
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5A.3i); changes where M2.1's collector (`http.ts`) and M2.3's wrapper inlining (`wrappers.ts`) put a request's `site`; issue #13
- **Context:** since M2.1 an HTTP request's site was its callee name: `fetch`, the `get` of `api.get(…)`. EVALUATION.md §1 has defined the labeled call site as the start of the call expression since M3, and the two differ whenever the callee is a member access: the column always (`api.get` at `api`, `window.fetch` at `window`), and the line when the call is split (`axios⏎ .get(…)`). Columns only matter to scoring when a label has one, but lines always do, so the recall diagnosis (R9) found wanderlust's `blog-feed.tsx` calls scored as FP + FN pairs, and after M5A.3g–h the held-out online-ide showed the same on every multi-line `axios⏎ .get(`. The labels follow the documented rule, so the analyzer moves, not the labels.
- **Decision:** a request's site is `locationOf(call)`, the call expression's first token (`node.getStart()`), both for a `fetch`/axios call and for a call of a wrapper the request is inlined into. Parentheses and `await` around the callee are part of the call's start or outside it as TypeScript parses them (`(axios).post(…)` starts at `(`; `await` is outside). This also moves unresolved `http` node IDs (`unresolved:<service>/<file>:<line>:<col>`), which are built from the site.
- **Not changed:** call edges (ADR-0025), Prisma, SDK, and queue sites keep their method-name anchor. They aren't scored as HTTP predictions against EVALUATION.md's rule, except SDK `external_call` sites, which predict "no route" and are only compared when a label sits on that line; they can move separately if a label ever needs it.
- **Alternatives rejected:** amending EVALUATION.md to anchor at the method name (the start is what a reader and GitHub's line link point at for a multi-line call, and the rule predates the eval); comparing lines with a tolerance in scoring (it would hide real off-by-one bugs).
- **Consequences:** 5 fixture goldens moved in columns (and `axios-defaults`' unresolved node ID); no README image bytes changed; the collector's tests now expect the start column, and a new test pins `axios⏎ .get(`, `window.fetch`, and `(axios).post`.
- **Measured:** original five TP 135 ⇒ 138, FP 3 ⇒ 0, FN 3 ⇒ 0 (precision 97.8% ⇒ 100.0%, recall 97.8% ⇒ 100.0%; wanderlust 22/22). Held-out TP 58 ⇒ 78, FP 20 ⇒ 0, FN 20 ⇒ 0 (74.4% ⇒ 100.0% both): every one of those FP/FN pairs was this anchoring.

## ADR-0168: M5A's outcome, and the held-out set counts as seen from now on
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5A.4); sums up ADR-0158 to ADR-0167 and amends EVALUATION.md §1's held-out rules; issue #13
- **Context:** M5A began at precision 96.6%, recall 20.3% on the original five (`eval/results/2026-10-30-0.1.0.md`); the held-out three, first scored before any fix, were 75.4% / 55.1%. The diagnosis (`eval/results/2026-11-recall-diagnosis.md`, written from the original five before any held-out repo was picked) found ten root causes, R1–R10, each fixed as a general pattern with its own fixture and ADR. M5A.4's re-run: `pnpm eval --check` at `ca04e28` reproduced `eval/results/2026-11-01-0.1.0.md` byte for byte (analyzer commit `6096989`). Later runs overwrote that file, and the review fixes (M5A.5b–f) moved the report to `eval/results/2026-11-02-0.1.0.md` with the same counts; at M5A.5g, `pnpm eval --check` at `7c1c3e1` reproduces it byte for byte (recorded at `ecd6e4d`). The pre-fix held-out numbers live in `2026-11-01-0.1.0.md` at `1cdf2ec`, batch 2's blind run in the same file at `eee9e37`.
- **Result, as measured:** original five precision 100.0% (138/138), recall 100.0% (138/138), all 6 `null` labels correct abstentions; held-out precision 100.0% (78/78), recall 100.0% (78/78), all 3 `null` labels abstentions. The M5A targets (≥ 95%, ≥ 80%) are met. Most links are `heuristic`, not `exact`: 133 of 138 on the original five and 60 of 78 held-out, because they cross services through an env-named base, a proxy-less root-relative path, or a folder listing, which is what the diagram should say.
- **The approach changed in four ways** (details in the ADRs named):
  - **Names stand in for values the repo doesn't hold**, at `heuristic` and only with a route that aligns: env bases by a documented list of endings (ADR-0165), a loopback in-code fallback (ADR-0158).
  - **"Exactly one write in a known place" reads mutable state:** `defaults.baseURL` (ADR-0161), a class property set once in the constructor (M5A.3e1), a `let`'s reaching values (ADR-0166). Anything else stays unknown.
  - **Wrapper inlining follows data, not shape:** object-literal methods (ADR-0159), a whole axios config parameter (ADR-0160), and only a parameter reaching the URL's leading part makes a wrapper (ADR-0164).
  - **The analyzer moved to the documented rules, never the reverse:** call sites at the call's start (ADR-0167), service boundaries from sibling `package.json` folders (ADR-0163), routers from folder listings without running filters (ADR-0162).
- **The held-out set is no longer unseen.** No fix was chosen from it: R7–R10 are all in the diagnosis, and `SERVER_URL` was on R7's list before the pick. But its FP and FN lists were read after M5A.3g and M5A.3h, M5A.3h's scope named online-ide's query-only ternary (the general form is in fixture `url-alternatives`), and online-ide's 16 split `axios⏎ .get(` calls made R9's size visible. So its 100% says the fixes carried over to three repos already looked at, not to repos nobody has looked at. From now on: the three stay scored apart for continuity, EVALUATION.md calls them seen, and a generalization claim needs a fresh held-out batch, picked and labeled (labels committed) before the analyzer first runs on it, and reported as first measured, whatever the numbers (M5A.4a).
- **What's still weak:** the sample (8 hobby-size repos, 225 labels) says nothing about large production codebases; the original five's labels wait on the owner review (#1, the eval re-runs when it closes); known misreads are documented, not fixed: `/posts/post-${id}` reads as a path and `"https://h/api/" + resource` as an ID (ADR-0164), more than 8 URL paths fall back to one hole (ADR-0166), `var`, loops, and `+=` stay unknown (ADR-0166), env names off the list stay `NO_MATCHING_ROUTE` (ADR-0165), and only Express and Next.js routes exist (other frameworks are after v1, ROADMAP M5A). URLs built at runtime stay unresolved with a reason by design.
- **Alternatives rejected:** keep calling the three "held out" (the README's "none chosen from these repos" is true for the fixes but not for what was read, so the claim would overstate); drop them from the report (hides a measurement); add a fresh batch only after M6 (M5A's goal is "any repo written in the common styles", and the one number that tests it should come before the milestone is tagged).

## ADR-0169: The billing kill switch is a second Cloud Run service from the worker's image, fed by a $1 budget over Pub/Sub
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.17e1, planning #14); follows ADR-0111, ADR-0151, ADR-0156
- **Context:** ADR-0111 requires "a budget alert that turns billing off (Google's documented kill switch)" before the trial ends, as the backstop behind the app's own compute pause (ADR-0156) and the 2-instance cap. Google's recipe: a budget publishes to a Pub/Sub topic, and a function subscribed to it calls Cloud Billing's `projects.updateBillingInfo` with an empty `billingAccountName`. Abhinav asked (#14) for everything that doesn't need his Google account to be written now. Nothing is deployed yet (#3).
- **Decision:**
  - **The switch is code in the worker's package** (`apps/worker/src/killswitch.ts`, entry `killswitch-bin.ts`), deployed as its own Cloud Run service `gitinside-killswitch` from the worker's image with another command (`infra/gcp/killswitch.json`). One image to build, scan, and keep in Artifact Registry's free storage; no Cloud Functions source upload or Cloud Build. It imports only `node:http` and the worker's HTTP and log helpers, never the analyzer or the database.
  - **When:** a Pub/Sub push whose budget notification has `costAmount ≥ budgetAmount` (and a positive budget). It reads billing info first and does nothing if billing is already off (the budget keeps notifying; unlinking twice is an error), then unlinks. The token is the instance's own account's, from the metadata server: no key, no secret.
  - **Answers for Pub/Sub:** `204` for done, under budget, already off, or not a budget notification (redelivery wouldn't change those); `503` when a Google call fails or takes over 10 s, so Pub/Sub retries for up to 7 days (the subscription's retention), 10 s to 10 min apart.
  - **The budget:** $1 a month on this project, `INCLUDE_ALL_CREDITS` (what the card would pay; during the trial credits cover it all, so it never fires then), thresholds at 50/90/100% of actual spend for the emails. Forecast thresholds aren't used: a forecast must never turn billing off.
  - **IAM:** a fourth account, `gitinside-killswitch`, runs the service and signs Pub/Sub's pushes to it (the only invoker), and holds `roles/billing.projectManager` **on the project**, the one project-level binding (ADR-0151 had none). Unlinking needs only `resourcemanager.projects.deleteBillingAssignment` on the project, and that is the narrowest predefined role holding it; nothing on the billing account. Cloud Billing's own agent may publish to `billing-alerts` and nothing else. Ingress is internal and IAM is required, as for the worker.
  - **Checked:** `killswitch.test.ts` against a fake Google (metadata header, bearer token, unlink only of a linked project), `gcpConfig.test.ts` for the files (same image, the entry exists, scale 1, its timeout fits three 10 s calls and Pub/Sub's ack deadline, the budget's amount, credits, topic, and push path), and CI's image check starts the switch from the image as `killswitch.json` says and sends it an under-budget push.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a Cloud Run function from source (Cloud Build and a second artifact, for 200 lines); the route inside the worker service (the worker's requests are HMAC-gated jobs with a 4 GiB instance each, and a busy worker answers `429`, which would delay the stop); a budget only with emails (nobody may read them in time); Cloud Run's spend cap alone (in preview; kept as an extra if the console offers it, ADR-0111); disabling the Cloud Run API instead of billing (stops the worker but not other billable services; Google's recipe is the billing unlink).
- **Consequences:** a fired switch takes the whole project offline until Abhinav re-links billing by hand, and Google warns that resources in a project without billing may be deleted; `infra/gcp` can recreate them. Budget data lags usage by hours, so the bill can pass $1 by what accrues meanwhile; the 2-instance cap keeps that to cents. Not verified until deployed: that Pub/Sub pushes count as internal ingress, and that the role suffices to unlink (M5.17e3's `verify-guardrails`, M5.13's smoke test).

## ADR-0170: The deploy is CI's last job, keyless through Workload Identity Federation, and only replaces what was set up by hand
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.17e2, planning #14); follows ADR-0111, ADR-0151, ADR-0169
- **Context:** ADR-0111 says the worker is "deployed from GitHub Actions with keyless Workload Identity Federation", with image cleanup inside Artifact Registry's free 0.5 GB. `infra/gcp` holds the reviewed services, queue, and IAM, with `{{…}}` placeholders for the image and the snapshot store's identifiers. Nothing is deployed yet (#3); the workflow must be ready and idle until then.
- **Decision:**
  - **A job in `ci.yml`, not its own workflow.** `deploy` `needs` the other four jobs, so only a fully green commit ships, with no `workflow_run` (whose `branches` filter matches a fork's branch named `main`). It runs on a push to `main` or a manual run there, and is skipped while the repo variable `GCP_PROJECT_NUMBER` is unset. It alone has `id-token: write`.
  - **Who may deploy:** pool `github`, provider condition on the repository's and owner's numeric IDs, `ref == refs/heads/main`, `workflow_ref` = `ci.yml` on main, event `push` or `workflow_dispatch` (`infra/gcp/github.json`); the binding on `gitinside-deploy` names the repository ID. IDs rather than names, so a deleted and re-created repo of the same name gets nothing. The two Google actions are pinned by commit.
  - **What the deploy account may do** (`iam.json`, each on one resource): push to the `gitinside` repository (`artifactregistry.writer`); replace each service (`run.developer`, which holds no `setIamPolicy`, so it can't add an invoker or `allUsers`); act as each runtime account (`iam.serviceAccountUser`, required to deploy a service that runs as it); update the `analyses` queue (`cloudtasks.queueAdmin`). No project-level role: it never creates a service, so the first versions of both are created by hand from Google's sample image (M5.17e4).
  - **The work is a tested script** (`infra/gcp/deploy.mjs`, plain Node like `check.mjs`): `changed` diffs the pushed range against the image's inputs (the worker plus its workspace dependencies read from the `package.json` files, root manifests, `.dockerignore`, `infra/gcp`) and deploys when it can't tell; `apply` builds for `linux/amd64`, pushes `worker:<sha>`, reads the digest, renders both services (on the parsed JSON, `IMAGE` must be this registry's worker by digest, other values identifiers), replaces the kill switch then the worker, and sets the queue from `queue.json`. `apps/web/lib/gcpDeploy.test.ts` runs it against a fake `gcloud`/`docker` and checks the job, the condition, and the IAM.
  - **Registry size:** keep the 3 most recent versions, delete the rest once an hour old; `check.mjs` fails CI if the image passes 150 MB gzipped, so 3 versions fit 0.5 GB even with no shared layers.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a service-account key in GitHub secrets (a long-lived credential; ADR-0111 says keyless); a separate `deploy.yml` on `workflow_run` (the fork-branch pitfall, and a second place to restate "only green commits"); deploying on every push to `main` (most commits don't touch the image, and each build is a new registry version); `run.admin` or a project-level `run.developer` so the deploy can create services (lets CI change invokers, or touch any service); Cloud Build (another API and service agent for one `docker build` that the runner can do).
- **Consequences:** a deploy is about a build plus two service updates after CI. A deploy that fails half way leaves the new image unused (kept by the 3-version rule) and is retried by a manual run. Not verified until #3's setup exists: that queue-level `cloudtasks.queueAdmin` and service-level `run.developer` suffice for `gcloud` (M5.17e3's `verify-guardrails`, M5.13).

## ADR-0171: `verify-guardrails` reads the live project back against `infra/gcp`, read-only, and a check it can't run fails
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-01, M5.17e3, planning #3, #14); follows ADR-0111, ADR-0151, ADR-0169, ADR-0170
- **Context:** ADR-0111 accepts a card on file only with the guardrails verified. The reviewed files say what the project should be, and CI checks the files against the code, but the project itself is set up by hand once (M5.17e4) and can drift afterwards: a console click that sets `maxScale` to 100, a binding added to debug, a cleanup policy left in dry-run, an unused static IP. Abhinav must hear before trial day 90 that the live project matches.
- **Decision:**
  - **A plain Node script, `infra/gcp/verify.mjs`** (`pnpm verify-guardrails`), run by the project's owner with `gcloud auth login`: a token from `gcloud auth print-access-token`, then Google's REST APIs directly (no SDK, no `gcloud` output parsing), every request with `x-goog-user-project` (the Budget API refuses a user token without one). It sends GETs and `getIamPolicy` only, so running it can't change anything.
  - **What it compares:** both Cloud Run services through the Knative API (the same document as `service.json`): scale, concurrency, timeout, CPU and memory (as quantities, `1000m` = `1`), ingress, CPU throttling and boost, execution environment, account, the image (this registry's worker by digest), command, variables and secret references, no VPC egress annotations, Ready. The queue. Every IAM policy `iam.json` names plus each account's and secret's, **exactly**: missing and extra bindings both fail. On the project, besides `iam.json`'s one binding, only what Google and the project's creation put there: `roles/owner` held by a person, Editor for the Google APIs Service Agent (`<number>@cloudservices`), and `*.serviceAgent` roles for `service-<number>@…` agents. Anything else (the default compute account's Editor, `allUsers`) fails. No user-managed service account keys. The GitHub provider's condition and mapping. Billing on, the budget, the topic, and the subscription pushing to one of the kill switch's live URLs. Nothing billed by the hour: Cloud NAT, forwarding rules (load balancers), reserved IPs, Memorystore, Serverless VPC Access connectors; an API that's off (`SERVICE_DISABLED`) counts as none, for those three APIs only. Artifact Registry: format, cleanup policies (gcloud's file format and the API's compared through one normalized form), not a dry run, no other repository in the region, total under 0.5 GB.
  - **Fail visibly:** a resource it can't read is a problem line, never a pass; the other parts still run. Exit 0 only when every check matched.
  - **Defaults:** where Google leaves out a value (a zero duration, `minScale` 0, CPU throttling on, a false boolean), the documented default is compared.
  - **Tested** in `apps/web/lib/gcpVerify.test.ts` against a fake Google built from `infra/gcp`'s own files (checking the token, the quota project, and each API's method for `getIamPolicy`): the healthy project passes reading only; 48 single changes each give exactly one line.
- **Alternatives rejected:** `gcloud … describe` per resource and parsing its output (formats shift between versions, and a Windows shell per call); running it in CI's deploy job (the deploy account would need read access to the project's IAM, billing, and budgets, which it shouldn't have); comparing only the cost settings (a public invoker or an extra key is how a guardrail is bypassed); Policy Troubleshooter to prove each account's permissions are enough (another API; the first deploy and the kill switch's first notification show it, M5.13).
- **Consequences:** M5.17e4's steps end with `pnpm verify-guardrails`, and it's worth re-running after any console change and before trial day 90. Until CI's first deploy, both services run Google's sample image, which it reports. Still unverified by it: whether `run.developer` and `cloudtasks.queueAdmin` suffice for `gcloud` (the first deploy) and whether `billing.projectManager` suffices to unlink (only a real over-budget message shows it); the Vercel pool, which has no file here yet.

## ADR-0172: SDK calls and queue events are per-service facts; worker factories are found globally over recorded factory calls
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.7d3c); follows ADR-0090, ADR-0094, ADR-0098, ADR-0099
- **Context:** M6.7d3c. The SDK and queue detectors each walked every program in one pass, with value caches shared across services. Queues also had a global step inside the walk: a worker factory (a function whose `new Worker` reads its queue name from a parameter, `startWorker(name, fn)`) was found from every service's events, then every service was walked again for calls naming it, in rounds, and a called factory's own consumer was dropped. So a service's queue result depended on who called its factories, which its cached facts can't see.
- **Decision:**
  - **SDK:** `collectSdkFacts(sp, resolver, workspace)` records each request as plain data (owners, API host, confidence, site) in walk order, with its own value caches. `composeSdkEdges` binds owners and merges in program then walk order, as before.
  - **Queues:** `collectQueueFacts(sp, …, repoUsesQueues)` records the first site naming each known queue, each producer and consumer (the queue's name, or the `unresolved` node a dynamic name gets, computed while walking), and **every call of a function that returns a worker** with the factory's key, the callee's name as written, and the consumer it would make with the call's arguments bound. A consumer inside a function whose parameter its name reads is `within` that function (key and names). `composeQueueEdges` runs the old rounds over these records: fresh factories from `within`, then the recorded calls whose name is one of theirs and whose key is one of them, in program then walk order; then the old event loop.
  - **Factory keys are scoped to a program:** `<service key>|nodeKey`. The old rounds compared nodes by identity, so a factory a service reached through a relative import TypeScript resolved itself (its own copy of the other service's file) never matched the other service's walk; keying by file position alone would have matched it and changed output. A test pins this, and pre-change output was compared on such a repo.
  - **`usesQueueLibrary` gates the scan** like `usesPrisma` (ADR-0099): facts record `scanned`, and unscanned facts compose to nothing.
- **Alternatives rejected:** resolving only calls spelled like a known factory (the set of factory names is global, so every service's facts would go stale when any service adds one, as wrapper names do, ADR-0092); deciding factory-ness per service by reading the callee's body (a second definition of "factory" to keep equal to the rounds, including a factory that passes its parameter on through another service); keeping a shared cache across services (a service's facts would depend on which services were walked first).
- **Consequences:** no output change (goldens, eval). In a repo using a queue library, the collector resolves every named call to a function with a parameter and reads what it returns, where the old walk resolved only calls spelled like a factory found in an earlier round; those answers never reach the output unless the call is a factory's. Composing JSON round-tripped facts equals `analyze`'s queue edges on `queues-bullmq` and its SDK edges on three fixtures; a factory package's facts don't change when another service starts calling it, and an SDK package's don't change when another service mounts its router.

## ADR-0173: A TypeScript failure belongs to the service whose analysis asked, not to the program whose checker threw
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.7d3d1); follows ADR-0090, ADR-0091
- **Context:** M6.7d3d (split: d1 this, d2 the `ServiceFacts` bundle and `analyze` as facts → compose). The resolver kept one list of `TYPESCRIPT_INTERNAL_ERROR` warnings (M5.8c) for the whole run, keyed by file and position. A service's cached facts (M6.7e) must carry the warnings its analysis produced, but a failure often happens in another program's checker: web's analysis follows a workspace import into `shared`'s program and asks `shared`'s checker. Attributing it to `shared` would tie the warning to the wrong facts: a warm run reusing `shared`'s entry would keep it after `web` stopped asking, and one reusing `web`'s would lose it.
- **Decision:**
  - **`resolver.asking(sp, ask)`** runs `ask` as part of `sp`'s analysis; a failure meanwhile, in any checker, is recorded under `sp` and listed by `resolver.warnings(sp)`, deduplicated per service by file and position. Nested calls restore the outer service, also when `ask` throws. A failure outside any `asking` belongs to the program whose checker threw (the old meaning, for detectors called directly in tests).
  - **Who asks:** `analyze` wraps every per-service collector that uses the resolver (Express, Next.js, Prisma, calls, SDKs, queues) in `asking`. The HTTP stage walks all programs inside itself, so `collectHttpCalls` and `collectHttpFacts` ask as each service they walk (site collection, wrapper finding, and each service's facts). Its lazily built `defaults.baseURL` scan of every program isn't scoped: it asks as the service whose request started it, because that is the computation it feeds, so a cached triggering service carries its warnings and a recomputed one triggers the scan again.
  - **Assembly** lists every program's warnings in program order; it already deduplicates and sorts warnings, so output is unchanged.
- **Alternatives rejected:** threading an "asking" program through every detector function and `checkerOf` call (dozens of call sites in seven files, and a missed one fails silently just the same); a checker wrapper per (asker, program) pair (the same threading problem: detectors pick the checker by the program they're in, not by who asked); one global dedupe with a per-service owner (a second service hitting the same failure wouldn't record it, so its facts would depend on which service ran first).
- **Consequences:** no output change (goldens, eval). Two services failing at the same position each record it; assembly drops the duplicate when the messages match, and would keep both if TypeScript threw different errors at one position (it hasn't been seen). Whether TypeScript throws at all can still depend on what the checker was asked before, which no cache can fix; the union over services is what a warm run reproduces.

## ADR-0174: One `ServiceFacts` bundle per service; `analyze` collects each stage into the bundles, then composes it from them
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.7d3d2); follows ADR-0090, ADR-0173
- **Context:** M6.7d3a–d1 turned every detector into per-service facts plus a global composition, but `analyze` still held them in per-stage locals, so nothing a cache could store or hand back existed. M6.7e needs one entry per service holding everything its analysis found, and a way to compose the graph from entries it didn't collect.
- **Decision:**
  - **`ServiceFacts`** (`facts.ts`): the service's key, its program's root files and warnings, its symbol nodes, each detector stage's facts (imports, Express, Next.js, Prisma, calls, HTTP, SDKs, queues), the stages whose collection failed (`failed`: stage + its `DETECTOR_FAILED` warning), and its `TYPESCRIPT_INTERNAL_ERROR` warnings from any program's checker (`resolver.warnings(sp)`, ADR-0173). Plain data; JSON round-trips unchanged.
  - **Stage-major, not service-major:** each stage first collects every service's facts into its bundle (asked as that service), then composes from all bundles (`stageFacts`). Collecting one service through every stage would move each stage's composition, and so `onFound`'s routes and stores, to the end of the run; stage timings and progress events stay as they were.
  - **Failures per service:** a throw while collecting one service is recorded in its bundle, and the other services still collect, so a bundle doesn't depend on how another service's analysis went. A global step a collection needs (the repo-wide router and queue-library checks, the Prisma schemas, the HTTP stage's whole-repo passes) that throws is recorded in every bundle. Either way the stage contributes nothing and the graph gets the first recorded warning, as when a stage throws as a whole; a throw while composing is still the stage's own failure.
  - **`analyzeServices(dir, { facts, onFacts })`**: `analyze` is this without options of its own. `onFacts` gets the bundles before assembly; `facts` composes from given bundles (one per workspace service, in workspace order, else it throws) without building programs, while the global steps (inventory, workspace, Prisma schemas, infra, file nodes, matching, assembly) still run. Express's root-file owners come from the bundles' `rootFiles`, so composition needs no program.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a separate `composeFacts(bundles, context)` beside `analyze` (two orderings of the same stages to keep in step; the `facts` option is the path M6.7e takes anyway); service-major collection (above); keeping a whole-stage failure on the first throw (later services' bundles would depend on an earlier service's bug, and a warm run would differ from a cold one).
- **Consequences:** no output change on healthy repos (goldens, eval); composing every fixture's JSON round-tripped bundles gives the same bytes. When one service's collection throws, the services after it now still collect, so their TypeScript warnings appear where the old run stopped early. A bundle's stage facts can still depend on global inputs (`scanned` flags for routers, Prisma, queues), and a bundle with a recorded failure may be caused by a global step; M6.7e keys the former and shouldn't reuse the latter.

## ADR-0175: `analyze({ cache })` reuses facts only when every service is clean (first step); keys hold the global inputs and each service's own files
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.7e1); follows ADR-0090, ADR-0091, ADR-0174
- **Context:** M6.7e (split: e1 this, e2 reusing clean services beside dirty ones, e3 building clean programs lazily). ADR-0174 made `analyze` compose from given bundles without programs. What a bundle depends on beyond its service's observed inputs: the global inputs ADR-0091 left out of the logs (TypeScript's version and so its `lib.*.d.ts`, the workspace layout, the inventory limits), whether the repo uses Prisma (`repoUsesPrisma`: a `.prisma` file or an `@prisma/client` dependency, which decides whether client calls are looked for and isn't a program root), and repo-wide facts computed over every program (wrapper names, ADR-0092; the router and queue-library checks; the HTTP stage's whole-repo passes).
- **Decision:**
  - **One entry per service** (`incremental.ts`): `{ facts, inputs }` (its `ServiceFacts` and `serviceInputs`), under `service-facts:<sha256>` of the global inputs above, the service key, and its own root files with their content hashes. The own files don't decide validity (the inputs do); they keep one entry per version of a service, so a diff's base and head, or a branch switch, don't overwrite each other's entries for the services that differ.
  - **Whole-run reuse:** in the `programs` stage, every service's entry is read and its inputs verified (`createInputVerifier`, one file hasher shared with the keys). If all hold, the graph is composed from them with no program built; every program would then be as before, so the repo-wide facts are too. Otherwise the run is cold and stores every bundle again after the last resolver stage. Bundles with a failed collection are never stored (the cause may be global, or TypeScript's query order).
  - **Interfaces:** `AnalyzeOptions.cache` (any `FactCache`), `openSide(spec, repo, { cache })`, and `--cache <dir>` on `analyze` and `diff`, which opens one `openDiskCache` for both sides. No new pipeline stage, so stage timings and progress events keep their shape.
- **Alternatives rejected:** partial reuse in this step (a clean bundle's call and HTTP facts depend on every program's wrappers and on the `baseURL` scan, which needs its own check; e2); one entry per repo (a one-file change would drop every service's facts, which e2 needs); keys without the own files (base and head would overwrite each other's entries on every diff); keying on the whole inventory's hashes (every edit misses; the inputs already say exactly what was read).
- **Consequences:** a warm run of an unchanged repo, or one where only global-step files changed (compose, `.env`, Prisma schema contents, non-source files), parses no TypeScript; any change a service read makes the whole run cold until e2. Tests: every fixture analyzed warm from a moved copy gives the cold bytes with no writes; ten edits on a three-service monorepo (edit, add, delete, a file another service imports, a cross-service import, an added, a broken, and an extended tsconfig outside every service, a dependency, a Prisma schema) each give the cold bytes, and the next run is warm; switching between two versions of one service stays warm; a CLI diff with `--cache` matches one without. Mutation checks: skipping verification fails the tsconfig cases, dropping Prisma use from the key fails the schema case.

## ADR-0176: Partial reuse: clean services' facts beside dirty ones, checked against the repo-wide facts, else collected again
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.7e2); follows ADR-0175
- **Context:** ADR-0175 reused facts only when every service was clean. A clean service's observed inputs vouch for everything its analysis read, including other programs it reached, but not for three facts computed over every program: whether any service creates an Express router (else routes aren't collected), whether any root file names a queue library (else queues aren't), and the set of HTTP wrappers (a call spelled like one is resolved and inlined, ADR-0092). The `defaults.baseURL` scan needs no check: only writes in files the request's file reaches count, and reaching them records their programs. Two more things a clean bundle can't vouch for: a collection that failed and a TypeScript internal error, which may depend on what the checker was asked before (a run that skips the clean services asks less).
- **Decision:**
  - **Entries record the repo-wide facts** they were collected under (`RepoFacts`: `routers`, `queues`, sorted wrapper IDs) and the service's own part of `queues` (whether its root files name a library; its router creations and wrappers are already in its facts). `KEY_FORMAT` 2.
  - **Every service clean:** the repo-wide facts are predicted from the entries' own parts (`repoFactsOf`), each vouched for by its inputs; entries collected under other ones are dropped, so the run is partial. Otherwise the graph is composed with no program built, as before.
  - **Partial run:** every program is built (lazy building is M6.7e3); the services without a reused entry collect; the router and queue-library checks and the HTTP whole-repo passes (sites and wrappers) still run over every program, but `collectHttpFacts(…, only)` inlines calls only for the collected services. After the last stage, the run's repo-wide facts are compared with each reused entry's. A mismatch reruns the analysis with those services forced to collect (a second mismatch, which can't happen with deterministic facts, goes cold). A collected service's failure, or a TypeScript internal error in any program's checker, reruns it cold.
  - **Stored:** only the collected services' bundles, with the run's repo-wide facts; never one with a failed collection or a TypeScript internal error (now also skipped on cold runs).
  - **Events of a rerun:** the first attempt already reported every stage before assembly, so the rerun reports only `assemble`, and its last findings once. Stage timings then omit the rerun's time; reruns happen only when an edit changes a repo-wide fact or TypeScript fails.
- **Alternatives rejected:** comparing wrapper names rather than IDs (IDs are as cheap and also catch a wrapper moving between files); recollecting only a stale service's affected stage (its other stages' inputs would need merging with the new run's; a repo-wide fact changes rarely); computing the wrappers before any stage collects (moves the HTTP passes ahead of Express and changes the query order the warnings follow); keeping TypeScript-internal-error bundles (their warnings depend on query order, which mixing entries from several runs doesn't preserve).
- **Consequences:** an edit now recollects only the services whose inputs it changed: on the three-service monorepo, editing api's server stores one entry, changing shared's file stores shared's and web's (web reached shared's program), and global inputs (a dependency, a Prisma schema) still recollect all three. Adding a wrapper, the first router, or the first queue library recollects the services collected under the old answer. Tests: the ten ADR-0175 edits each give the cold bytes and store exactly the dirty services; a wrapper named like a clean service's call, the first router (and switching back, where every entry is clean but one was collected under a router), and the first queue library each give the cold bytes and recollect the clean service; a rerun reports each stage once; a TypeScript internal error or a failed collection in a dirty service runs cold. Mutation checks: skipping the comparison fails the wrapper and router cases with wrong bytes; skipping the all-clean prediction, or either half of the trouble check, fails its test.

## ADR-0177: Programs parse on first use; a reused service's program only when a collected service's analysis needs it
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.7e3); follows ADR-0176
- **Context:** ADR-0176's partial run built every program, and the repo-wide passes (the router check, the queue-library check, the HTTP stage's site collection, wrapper finding, and `defaults` scan) walked all of them, so a run that reused most services' facts took as long as a cold one (`bench/results/2026-11-02-lazy-programs.md`). What a collected service's analysis can need from a reused service's program: an import into it (`importTarget`, `programOf`), the symbol index of one of its files (`symbolOfDeclaration`, `callers`), a wrapper whose body is in it, and an axios `defaults` write in one of its files that a request reaches.
- **Decision:**
  - **`ServiceProgram` parses on first read** of `program`, `host`, `configFile`, or `warnings`; `service`, `rootFiles`, `inputs`, and the path helpers are known without parsing. The `programs` stage parses the collected services' programs (all of them on a cold run), so its timing and progress keep their meaning.
  - **The resolver indexes a program's symbols on first need:** the owner of every root file is known from `rootFiles`; looking up a file (`programOf`, an import's target, a declaration's symbol, `callers.get`) parses and indexes its owner. The answers are the ones the eager index gave.
  - **Repo-wide facts take a reused service's part from its entry,** which its inputs vouch for: its router creations (`facts.express.creations`), whether it names a queue library (`queueLibrary`), and its wrappers' IDs (`facts.http.wrappers`). The stale-entry comparison (ADR-0176) runs on these combined facts unchanged.
  - **HTTP:** sites are collected per program on first need (`createHttpSiteCollector`, shared so axios values stay the same objects). `collectHttpFacts` takes `{ only, known }`: a reused program's wrappers are known by ID, so its names still make calls candidates. Its sites are collected, and its wrappers found, only when a collected service's call resolves to one of them. The call then records that program as reached, as before.
  - **`defaults` writes are scanned per file, in the files a request reaches** (each in its owner's program), instead of every root file up front. A request now always walks its reachable files, also when no write exists anywhere (before, it skipped the walk then), so the programs it reaches are always recorded as reached: possibly more inputs than before, never fewer.
  - **Stored inputs:** `serviceInputs` runs only for the collected services.
- **Alternatives rejected:** indexing a file's symbols from whichever program loaded it first (avoids parsing the owner, but the eager index read the owner's parse, and the bytes must not depend on that); keeping the eager `defaults` scan over every program (it would parse every program on any axios request); a separate "lazy" option (a cold run collects every service, so laziness changes only when programs parse, not what is asked).
- **Consequences:** on the three-service monorepo, editing api's server parses only api's program; changing shared's file parses shared's and web's; a run where every service is clean parses none. A collected service calling a reused package's wrapper parses that package and not the unrelated service. Measured (median, dev machine): shop-demo's partial runs 34.8–40.4 ms against 45.7–50.2 cold (M6.7e2: no faster than cold); this repo's tree, a worker edit 2,588 ms against 3,999 cold (M6.7e2: 4,141), an edit to `packages/diff`, which other services import, 4,075 against 4,717 (M6.7e2: 4,923). Cold runs unchanged (3,667 vs 3,643 ms). Tests: per run, which programs were parsed (a proxy over `buildServicePrograms` in `incremental.test.ts`), with warm == cold bytes; every earlier fixture, edit, and repo-wide-fact test unchanged. Mutation checks: parsing every program, or taking the router or queue part from the program instead of the entry, fails the parse assertions; skipping the on-demand wrapper lookup gives wrong bytes.

## ADR-0178: On Cloud Run the fact cache is 64 MiB of the in-memory `/tmp`, one folder per tenant, and makes room before each write
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.7f1); adapts ADR-0090's step f (written for the Oracle VM's 2 GB disk volume) to Cloud Run (ADR-0111)
- **Context:** ADR-0090 planned a 2 GB cache volume on the worker's VM, with private entries in per-installation folders. On Cloud Run there is no free persistent volume: the only writable place is `/tmp`, an in-memory filesystem that counts against the instance's 4 GiB, and ADR-0146's budget had already spent 4,090 MiB of it (256 MiB of that spare). Instances scale to zero, so a cache lives only as long as its instance. Measured (`bench/results/2026-11-02-cache-sizes.md`): one analysis's cached facts take 25.2 MiB for cal.com (113 services), 2.0 MiB for VS Code's `src/vs/platform`, and at most 28 KiB for the eval repos. `openDiskCache` also only bounded its folder after a write (it evicted once the total had passed the cap, so the folder briefly held cap + one entry, plus a temporary file during the write), and it counted bytes, not the 4 KiB pages an in-memory filesystem allocates per file.
- **Decision:**
  - **64 MiB, out of the budget's spare** (256 → 192 MiB; `memoryBudget` gains `factCache`, still 4,090 of 4,096 MiB): two cal.com-sized analyses, e.g. a PR's base and head, which share their unchanged services' entries anyway. `budget.test.ts` fails if the cache grows past it.
  - **`openDiskCache` makes room before writing** and counts each entry in whole blocks (`blockBytes`, 4,096 on the worker): a write that would pass the cap first lists the folder and evicts down to 90% including the new entry, and is skipped if room can't be made; writes are serialized, so concurrent puts can't each see room that only one has; opening with a smaller cap than the folder holds evicts the same way. The folder's entries plus the file being written stay within the cap.
  - **Folders** under `CACHE_ROOT` (default `/tmp/cache`, refused if it contains or is inside `JOBS_ROOT`): `public/` for public repos and `installations/<id>/` for each GitHub App installation's jobs (the request's `installationId`, M8.3). A repo the recorder found private without an installation gets no cache. `deleteInstallationCache` is ready for M8.5's uninstall.
  - **One cap across the folders:** before a job, the worker deletes other folders whole, least recently used first (each job touches its folder's time), until they hold at most half the cap; the job's folder gets the rest as its own cap, which the child's `openDiskCache` keeps.
  - **The child gets its folder through the job** (`ChildJob.cache`), and the runner adds `--allow-fs-read` and `--allow-fs-write` for that folder only. A cache that can't be prepared or opened makes the analysis run cold. The child reports the cache's hits, misses, writes, evictions, and errors in its stats (into `analyses.stats`, so M5.13 sees real hit rates).
- **Alternatives rejected:** a 2 GB cache in `/tmp` (half the instance; the heap cap would have to drop below what cal.com needs); the bucket on Oracle Object Storage as a shared cache (persists across instances, but the free tier's 50,000 requests a month would go to cache reads, and every miss adds a round trip; reconsider if M5.13 shows instances rarely stay warm); Cloud Storage FUSE volumes (us-east4 isn't in the free tier's regions); a separate cap per folder (with many installations their sum has no bound); taking the cache's room from the heap or the extraction limits (both were sized from measurements, ADR-0146).
- **Consequences:** reuse happens only between jobs on one warm instance (and between a diff's two sides, M6/M8.6); with at most 2 instances and scale-to-zero, the hit rate is likely low, and M5.13's recorded counters will show it. The spare is 192 MiB, about 3.6× the largest graph. The CLI keeps its 2 GB default (`DEFAULT_CACHE_BYTES`). The container check doesn't yet run two analyses through the image's cache; that's M6.7f2. There is no compose file to change any more (ADR-0150 replaced the Oracle stack).

## ADR-0179: Shared inputs are merged, digested, and verified once per run; the disk cache compresses off the main thread
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.8b); refines ADR-0175/0176's entries and ADR-0178's writes
- **Context:** on 10 real cal.com commits a warm run was 0.84× of cold (M6.8): it collected 88 of 113 services and then spent 4.6–6.2 s outside every stage, plus ~1.6 s more in `programs`. A profile of one such run: 2.1 s in `serviceInputs` (each program's log was snapshotted and sorted again for every service that reached it, ~88 × 75 snapshots), 2.3 s storing (88 entries serialized and gzipped one after another on the main thread), 0.9 s reading and verifying 112 entries one at a time, 0.6 s computing keys. Most of those services reach the same 75 programs, so 80-odd entries carry the same 1.3 MB of inputs (119 of the cache's 138 MB before compression).
- **Decision:**
  - **`serviceInputs` snapshots each program once** and merges once per distinct set of reached programs; services with the same set share one inputs object.
  - **An entry records `inputsDigest`**, the SHA-256 of its inputs' JSON (`inputsDigest` in `inputs.ts`; every list is sorted, so equal inputs give equal digests). `reusableFacts` verifies each digest once per run; `storeFacts` computes each shared object's digest once. `KEY_FORMAT` 3: older entries are misses.
  - **`openDiskCache` serializes at `put` time and gzips on libuv's thread pool**, so puts compress side by side; making room and renaming stay one at a time, so ADR-0178's bound holds. `get` gunzips on the thread pool too. `storeFacts` keeps 4 puts in flight (the pool's size), so only a few serialized entries are held at once; `reusableFacts` reads all entries at once and checks them in workspace order.
  - Still one entry per service, so the cache's counters, the worker's stats, and the container check mean what they did.
- **Measured** (`bench/results/2026-11-02-incremental-calcom-m6.8b.md`, `…-commits-m6.8b.md`): one commit pair's time after `queues` fell from 4.5 s to 1.4 s; the `packages/lib` edit went from 0.85× to 0.92×; an unchanged warm run's peak RSS rose from 417 to 576 MiB (every entry read at once), still far below a cold run's 2.3 GiB, which ADR-0146's budget is sized for.
- **Alternatives rejected for now:** storing each distinct inputs set once as its own entry, referenced from the service entries (cuts serializing, compressing, and reading the duplicates, ~85% of the bytes, but makes a service two cache entries, so every hit/write count in the worker, the container check, and the benchmark's columns changes; that's M6.8c); skipping or deferring stores when most services were collected (the entries a re-collected run writes are what make the next unchanged or leaf-edit run 2–4× faster); returning the graph before the stores finish (a job still pays for them before its process exits, and the benchmark would stop measuring them); a faster gzip level (bigger entries in the worker's 64 MiB, ADR-0178).
- **Consequences:** a mostly re-collected warm run is still slower than cold on cal.com (see the reports); M6.8c removes the duplicated inputs. Equal inputs are trusted by digest within a run: an entry whose digest doesn't match its inputs can only come from editing the cache folder, which could already inject facts.

## ADR-0180: Observed inputs are their own content-addressed cache entry, counted as shared
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.8c1); refines ADR-0179's entries
- **Context:** after M6.8b, about 80 of cal.com's 113 service entries still each carried the same 1.3 MB of observed inputs (119 of 138 MB before compression), so a run that stored or read them serialized, gzipped, and parsed one set ~80 times. Storing each set once makes a service two cache entries, and the worker's stats, the container check (`infra/gcp/check.mjs`), and the benchmark's "entries found" and "services collected" columns all read the cache's hits, misses, and writes as one per service.
- **Decision:**
  - **A service entry holds its facts, repo-wide facts, queue-library part, and `inputsDigest`; no inputs.** Each distinct inputs set is stored under `service-inputs:<KEY_FORMAT>:<digest>` (`inputsKey`). `KEY_FORMAT` 4: older entries are misses.
  - **`reusableFacts` reads each digest's inputs entry once**, as soon as a service entry names it, and verifies it once. An entry whose inputs entry is missing or malformed is a miss (the service is collected and stored again, with its inputs).
  - **`storeFacts` writes each distinct set once, before the entries naming it, and only if the run hasn't read or stored it already** (`FactKeys.present`), so a run that re-collects 88 services writes the few new sets, not 88 copies.
  - **The cache counts entries by use:** `get`/`put` take an optional `{ shared: true }` (`EntryUse`), and `CacheStats.shared` counts those reads and writes, so `hits`, `misses`, and `writes` stay one per service and every consumer keeps its meaning. Sizes, entry counts, and eviction cover both kinds.
- **Measured** (`bench/results/2026-11-02-incremental-calcom-m6.8c.md`, `…-commits-m6.8c.md`): the cache folder after filling is 7 MiB (was 25 MiB); an unchanged warm run peaks at 285 MiB (was 576 MiB); storing after a re-collected run takes 0.9–1.6 s (was 1.8–2.4 s); real commits median 0.94× (was 0.91×), `packages/lib` edit 0.92× (unchanged). Not yet parity: the `programs` stage is still ~0.9–1.1 s over cold, and storing the 88 service entries is ~1 s (M6.8c2).
- **Alternatives rejected:** counting per service in `analyze` and leaving the cache's counts raw (every consumer would need a second source for the same numbers); one inputs entry per program rather than per merged set (more entries, and a service's check would join many reads; the merged sets are already few); re-hashing an inputs entry on read to check its digest (costs a pass over 1.3 MB per set, and a mismatch can only come from editing the cache folder, as in ADR-0179).
- **Consequences:** evicting an inputs entry makes every service naming it a miss at once; LRU touches it whenever one of them is read, so it outlives them in practice. Two processes sharing a folder may both write a set; both write the same bytes (ADR-0090's atomic renames).

## ADR-0181: One read of each repo file per run; observed inputs stored in parts by owning service
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.8c2); refines ADR-0180's inputs entries
- **Context:** after M6.8c1 a re-collected warm cal.com run was still ~6% slower than cold. Profiling `6bfe2df` (cache filled at `1dbaf8e`) with timers in the built files: keys 0.6 s, reading and checking entries 0.33 s, `serviceInputs` 0.21 s, storing 0.62–0.92 s (215 ms `JSON.stringify`, ~450 ms gzip, 125 ms writes). Two thirds of the stored bytes (31 of 45 MB of JSON) were 23 new merged inputs sets: ~80 services reach most programs in 23 slightly different sets, each a 1.3 MB union, and one `packages/lib` edit makes every one new. Separately, every compiler host read each file it loads with `readFileSync`, so a `packages/lib` file was read once per program that loads it, and the `files` stage read and hashed every source file again (2.2–2.8 s, cold and warm alike). Reading the 5,039 program root files async took 0.48 s; one serial `readFileSync` pass took 2.3 s.
- **Decision:**
  - **`createRepoFiles` is a run's one view of file contents.** `preload` reads every program root file side by side (64 in flight) before the keys, in cold and warm runs alike; the hosts take text and hash from it (the first read keeps the text, which every later program shares); the keys, input checks, and file nodes take hashes from it. A file only hashed keeps no bytes, and a preloaded file's bytes are dropped once its text is decoded. Same bytes, so the same graph.
  - **`serviceInputs` returns each service's inputs in parts by owner:** a listing goes to its service, a file or folder to the service holding what's inside it (`serviceForFile` of `path + "/"`, so `apps/web` is web's), `""` outside every service. Each program's snapshot is split once; an owner's part is one object for every reach whose programs observed that owner's paths in the same programs. A service entry names its parts' digests (`inputParts`, `KEY_FORMAT` 5); each distinct part is a shared entry under `inputsKey`, read and verified once a run, and written only if the run didn't read or store it. A part that's gone makes the entries naming it misses.
- **Measured** (dev laptop, every warm graph equal to its cold pair): one cold run at `6bfe2df` 29.0 → 22.1 s (`programs` 9.8 → 5.8 s, `files` 2.2 → 0.05 s). The filled cache is 1.9 MiB (ADR-0180: 7 MiB). `bench/results/2026-11-02-incremental-calcom-commits-m6.8c2.md`: real commits median **0.99×** (M6.8c1: 0.94×), nine source-changing commits 0.96–1.01×, each warm run writing 1–8 parts; `…-calcom-m6.8c2.md`: `packages/lib` edit 0.99× by pairs (0.92×), unchanged 5.7× (4.0×), leaf edit 2.8× (2.5×), unchanged warm peak 190 MiB (285 MiB).
- **What's left, accepted:** 0.72–0.85 s per re-collected warm run outside every stage, mostly serializing and compressing the 88 service entries' facts (14 MB of JSON), which a re-collected run must rewrite because each entry names new parts. Removing it would mean storing facts content-addressed too (an unchanged service writing only a small head), or reuse finer than a service (SPEC D3.4's per-file cache), both for the M6 review rather than this task.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a faster gzip level for inputs (2× faster on a 1.3 MB set, 9% bigger; the parts made the bytes small instead); a small head entry per service read before its facts (parsing entries costs ~120 ms a run, so little to save); overlapping the store with assembly (both are main-thread work, and on Cloud Run's 1 vCPU it saves nothing); one inputs entry per program (ADR-0180: programs overlap heavily, so more bytes than the merged sets); preloading only in cache runs (the reads are the same work either way, so cold gets them too).
- **Consequences:** a cold run holds the program root files' bytes until each is first parsed (cal.com: 18.6 MB; at most `MAX_SOURCE_MB`), and one text per file instead of one per program. A part read by many entries is touched on every read, so LRU keeps it at least as long as them.

## ADR-0182: Diff mode draws one overlay graph holding both versions
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.9a)
- **Context:** UX.md's diff mode toggles Base, Head, and Overlay, and nodes present in both versions must keep their positions (SPEC §3, ROADMAP M6.9 "pinned base positions"). The explorer's view model, ELK layout, and layout cache all take one `ArchitectureGraph`. Laying out base and head separately and then pinning positions would need ELK's interactive mode and a matching step, and the two layouts would still disagree wherever an added or removed box sits.
- **Decision:** `buildDiffOverlay(base, head, diff)` (`packages/viewer/src/model/diff.ts`) builds one graph: head's nodes and edges plus every base-only (removed) node and edge, sorted by ID like a canonical graph, valid under `parseGraph`. A renamed item appears once, under its head ID, with its base record in `renamedFrom`; a removed node or edge whose parent or endpoint was renamed points at the head ID. `nodesIn` / `edgesIn` say which IDs each version draws. Base, Head, and Overlay are then three ways of drawing the same laid-out boxes, so stability needs no pinning: the layout is the overlay's.
  - Each version's node set is a tree: a base node whose parent changed to an added one (a move the diff didn't pair as a rename, e.g. without file contents) brings that frame into base, drawn plain.
  - States: added, changed (modified or renamed; `updated` records are no change, as in the Mermaid summary), removed; broken connections by the overlay edge the canvas draws (the removed base edge for `TARGET_REMOVED`/`ROUTE_CHANGED`, the head edge otherwise), with the diff's own words. An edge renamed only because an endpoint was isn't marked changed.
  - `inside` counts the changes below every ancestor, so a closed frame can say it holds changes. The changes list groups Added / Changed / Removed / Broken, shallow first, then by label; an added or removed node whose parent has the same state is folded into its nearest listed ancestor's count.
  - A diff whose repos, analyzer version, or items don't match the graphs throws `DiffMismatchError`, so the explorer fails visibly instead of drawing a wrong picture.
- **Alternatives rejected:** two layouts with head pinned to base positions (ELK interactive constraints, still moves boxes around added ones); laying out base and drawing head's additions in free space (added boxes land far from their siblings); deriving the diff in the viewer (the stored diff is the PR bot's, and one source of truth keeps the canvas and the comment in step).
- **Consequences:** the overlay's layout has room for removed boxes even in Head, and for added ones in Base: holes rather than motion when switching versions. M6.9c decides how a version hides the other's boxes (fade out in place). A graph's warnings in the overlay are head's.

## ADR-0183: The explorer's diff mode sizes diff marks into the layout
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.9b)
- **Context:** UX.md's diff mode draws states on boxes and lines, broken connections with the diff's words, and (ROADMAP M6.9) closed frames saying they hold changes. The layout measures every box and pill from what it draws (ADR-0101); a pill that leads with "Added" or says "DELETE /api/orders/:id was removed" is wider than its plain label, and a frame's change badges widen its count row. Base, Head, and Overlay must keep every box in place (ADR-0182).
- **Decision:**
  - `Explorer` takes `diff: { base, diff, baseName?, headName? }`; `graph` is the head. It builds the overlay (`buildDiffOverlay`) and draws the overlay graph; a `DiffMismatchError` shows an error-toned card instead of a canvas.
  - `diffMarks(overlay)` gives each node's state, the changes inside it, and each drawn line's marks: a line standing for several edges takes their common state, or `changed` when they differ; it's broken when any of them is, with that connection's words, or "N connections broken".
  - The layout cache takes the marks (`createLayoutCache(index, engine, limit, marks)` → `layoutInput` → `viewNodeSize`/`edgeLabelSize`): a closed box makes room for its change badges ("5 added", "6 broken", each with its state's dot; broken dashed), a pill for its state's word or what broke. The same sizes serve all three versions, so switching never re-lays out.
  - The canvas takes the look per box and line (`BoxLook.diff/changes/absent`, `LineLook.diff/broken/absent`). Overlay (the default) draws states; Base and Head draw no marks and leave out what the version lacks in place (`data-absent`: hidden, inert, out of the tree and of its siblings' count). The top bar's segmented control switches, announced; a "Comparing base → head" chip (short SHAs unless named) sits top-left.
  - A box holding changes says so in its accessible name too ("Changes inside: 5 added, 6 broken").
- **Alternatives rejected:** one "N changes" badge (shorter, but hides whether anything broke); sizing per version (moves boxes when switching); leaving pills at their plain size (the diff word would overflow, which the real-browser fit check forbids).
- **Consequences:** a diff-mode layout differs from the plain one for the same view (a separate cache per overlay). M6.9c fades the other version out in place and makes counts per version: frame badges and the inspector's summary still count both versions' items (Head says "5 routes" where 3 were removed), and an aggregate pill's count includes the other version's edges.

## ADR-0184: Base and Head fade in place and trace their own graph
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.9c1)
- **Context:** M6.9b hid the other version's boxes and lines at once, and the selection, a trace, the trace picker, and the palette all read the overlay's graph, so in Head a trace could step along a removed connection, and a removed node could stay selected while hidden. UX.md asks that switching moves nothing and keeps your place.
- **Decision:**
  - What a version lacks fades out where it is (`opacity` over `--duration-standard`, `visibility` changing at the far end, so it stays drawn while fading and fades back in from the same place); reduced motion makes it instant through the tokens. It's inert and out of the tree from the first frame.
  - `versionGraph(overlay, version)` gives one version's graph on the overlay's IDs; the explorer builds a hierarchy of it for Base and Head (`shownIndex`), and traces (`traceFrom`), `canTraceFrom`, the trace picker, and the palette read it. The canvas, its view, and its layout keep the overlay's, so positions don't change.
  - Switching keeps the selection and a trace when the version has their node (a trace's steps then follow that version's connections); otherwise they end, and the announcement says so ("Showing the head version. DELETE /api/orders/:id isn't in it, so it's no longer selected"). They aren't restored on switching back.
  - A real-browser check (`packages/viewer/visual/diff.visual.ts`, no screenshots, so every OS) samples every frame of each switch: shared boxes never move, left-out boxes are caught partway (visible, 0 < opacity < 1), then hidden; under reduced motion they're hidden in the first frame. The visual config runs `*.visual.ts`.
- **Alternatives rejected:** restoring a dropped selection on switching back (state that changes without the reader acting); a trace on the overlay that only follows head (the overlay shows both, so its trace does too); checking the fade in jsdom (no CSS transitions there; a mutation with an instant `visibility` passed a first, opacity-only browser check, which now also requires "visible").
- **Consequences:** M6.9c2 makes counts per version (frame badges, the inspector's summary, pill counts) and shows a renamed node's base label in Base, which `versionGraph` is the natural place for.

## ADR-0185: Base and Head draw their own records and counts on the overlay's sizes
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.9c2)
- **Context:** after M6.9c1, Base and Head left out what a version lacks, but what they drew still came from the overlay: a closed service said "5 routes" in Head where three of them were removed, the inspector's summary and connection lists counted both versions, a line standing for two connections said "2" where Head keeps one, and a renamed file showed its head name in Base. Switching must still move nothing (ADR-0182).
- **Decision:**
  - `versionGraph(overlay, "base")` gives a renamed node its base record (`baseRecord`: base name, path, and source) at its head ID and overlay parent, so the version's hierarchy stays a tree on the overlay's IDs.
  - `DrillCanvas` takes that hierarchy as `versionIndex` in Base and Head. Boxes draw its records and frame counts; lines and pills draw only the connections it holds (`edgeWithin`: the kept edges, their weakest confidence; one left draws its own label). A box or line the version lacks fades out as the overlay drew it, so nothing changes mid-fade.
  - The inspector (`NodePanel`, `TracePanel`) and the breadcrumbs read the version's hierarchy, so the summary, facts, and connection lists are that version's.
  - Sizes stay one per box and pill, now the widest of the three drawings: `viewNodeSize` fits a renamed node's base record too, and `viewEdgeLabelSize` fits each version's label for the connections it keeps (`DiffMarks.baseNode`, `DiffMarks.edgesIn`). A version's frame counts never outgrow the overlay's, which hold both versions' items, so they need nothing extra.
  - Stories `Diff, head` and `Diff, renamed, base` (on the diff-rename pair) switch the version in their `play`, so Linux baselines cover both.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a layout per version (boxes would move on switching); keeping "N calls" in a version where one connection is left (says less than the overlay's own single-edge label would); renaming in the canvas only (the inspector and breadcrumbs would disagree with the box).
- **Consequences:** a renamed node's base source still links and loads at the head commit, and so does every node Base draws; the website's diff mode (M6.9e) must load and link Base's code at the base commit. Edge records are still head's in Base (a renamed edge's base label isn't shown). Export (SVG, Mermaid) draws the overlay's view in every version.

## ADR-0186: The changes list lives in the inspector's overview and in each selection
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.9d)
- **Context:** UX.md asks for a changes list in the inspector (Added / Changed / Removed / Broken, a click flies to the item). The model (ADR-0182) already folds and orders the rows. Open questions: where in the inspector it goes, what a group's count counts, how a rename or move reads, and what a row does in Base or Head when that version lacks its node.
- **Decision:**
  - The repo overview (nothing selected) lists every row under "Changes", after the summary and before "Start here". A selection lists the rows for itself and what's inside it under "Changes here" (none: no section), so after a pick the inspector still says what changed there, and a selected frame lists its own changes.
  - A group's count is its rows plus what's folded into them, the same number the frames' badges show ("Removed 9" over four rows, one saying "1 more inside").
  - Each row is a button named "<state>: <name>, <kind>. <detail>". The detail is from the diff alone: a moved file or folder says its old path ("Moved from src/routes/orders.ts"), one renamed in its folder its old name; a symbol its old name and the file it moved from; then any other changed field ("code changed"); a changed node its fields in words ("Method and path changed"; labels and source positions never count, as in `packages/diff`'s fingerprints); a broken connection the diff's own words; an added or removed node its kind, where it lives, and how much more came with it. A renamed row now carries the rename's `changes` as its `fields`.
  - Picking a row reveals its node like a palette pick: the filters hiding it come off, its ancestors open, it's selected, the camera zooms to read it, and focus moves to its box (the row is gone once the inspector shows the selection). In Base or Head, a node that version lacks (a removed route in Head) switches to the Overlay first, and the announcement says why; the list itself is the diff's, the same in every version.
- **Alternatives rejected:** the list only in Overlay (Base and Head would hide what the review is about); disabled rows for what a version lacks (unreachable by keyboard and unexplained); a separate changes panel or tab (a second inspector to keep in step); group counts of rows only (would disagree with the frames' badges); focus kept in the list after a pick (its rows are replaced by the selection's inspector).
- **Consequences:** the list isn't virtualized; a PR with thousands of changed nodes draws that many rows (folding keeps added and removed trees to one row each). A changed model doesn't yet say which fields it gained or lost (the overlay holds no base record for a same-ID change). The website's diff mode (M6.9e) gets the list with no extra wiring.

## ADR-0187: Base reads every node and hop as base recorded it, at the base commit
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.9e1)
- **Context:** after M6.9c2 (ADR-0185), Base swapped in base's record only for renamed nodes. Everything else Base drew kept head's record: a route that kept its ID but moved with its file (`diff-rename`'s `GET /api/orders`, base `src/routes/orders.ts:19`, head `src/orders/routes.ts:20`) showed head's path and line, and every edge's call sites were head's. The inspector also had one repo and one file loader, head's, so Base's "Open on GitHub" and code preview pointed at the head commit, and so did a removed node or a removed trace hop in the Overlay, whose files may not exist at head at all.
- **Decision:**
  - `buildDiffOverlay` keeps base's own record of every node and edge base has whose record differs from the overlay's (`baseNodes`, `baseEdges`, by overlay ID; compared by value, whatever the key order). `versionGraph(overlay, "base")` gives each its base record at its overlay ID and place (`baseRecord`, `baseEdgeRecord`), so Base's graph is base's own graph on the overlay's IDs (a test maps it back and compares it with base, on both fixture pairs). `DiffMarks.baseNode` now covers every differing node, so boxes are still sized for all three drawings.
  - `ExplorerDiff` takes Base's `sources` and `loadSource`, as `sources`/`loadSource` are head's. The inspector reads code at base's commit (`base.repo`) with base's files for everything in Base, and in the Overlay for a selected node or a trace hop that only base has (not in `nodesIn.head` / `edgesIn.head`); head's otherwise. Head is unchanged.
  - Without a base loader, what Base reads shows no code section (and its GitHub link at the base commit), never head's file under base's line.
- **Alternatives rejected:** linking at the base commit but keeping head's records (right commit, wrong path and line); one loader taking a commit argument (changes `SourceLoader` for every host, while the two loaders are what a host has anyway: `createGitHubSourceLoader` per commit); base edge records on the canvas too (pills are sized from the overlay's edges, ADR-0185; Base's pills still draw head's labels, the inspector and traces base's).
- **Consequences:** a trace in Base now orders its steps by base's call sites. The overlay holds a base record for same-ID changes, which a later task can use to say which fields a changed model gained or lost (ADR-0186). M6.9e2 passes a loader at the base commit from the website.

## ADR-0188: The service files set no namespace, so the deploy needs no project-level permission (amends ADR-0151)
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M5.17e5)
- **Context:** CI's first deploy (#16, run 41547020540) was refused at `gcloud run services replace killswitch.json`: "[gitinside-deploy@…] does not have permission to access projects instance [gitinside-513223]". The deploy account had exactly ADR-0151's per-resource bindings (`run.developer` on each service, `serviceAccountUser` on both runtime accounts, `queueAdmin` on the queue, `artifactregistry.writer` on the repository). Read in gcloud 591.0.0's own source (the CLI tarball, `lib/surface/run/services/replace.py`), not guessed: when the file sets `metadata.namespace`, on Cloud Run (fully managed, global endpoint) `replace` calls `projects_util.GetProjectNumber(project)`, a Cloud Resource Manager `projects.get`, only to check that the namespace is the project's ID or number. That needs `resourcemanager.projects.get` on the project, which no per-resource role gives. The error's wording ("access projects instance") is gcloud's for that Resource Manager call, and it comes before any Cloud Run request. Without a namespace in the file, the namespace falls back to `--project` (`resource_args.NamespaceAttributeConfig`), with no API call. Nothing else on the replace path calls Resource Manager: `ReleaseService` gets and replaces the service, then polls it, its configuration, route, and revisions, all Cloud Run calls in the namespace. `gcloud tasks queues update` with `--location` (as `deploy.mjs` passes) is one `queues.patch`; only without it does gcloud list locations and look for an App Engine app.
- **Decision:**
  - `service.json` and `killswitch.json` drop `metadata.namespace`. The deploy's `--project=gitinside-513223` names the project, as it already did. `gcpConfig.test.ts` asserts that neither file sets one; `gcpDeploy.test.ts` already pins `--project`.
  - No new binding: `iam.json`, the README's step 10, and `verify.mjs`'s IAM checks stay as they are, so the deploy account still has nothing at the project level.
  - Step 8 drops `--notifications-rule-schema-version=1.0`: GA gcloud has no such flag (Cloud Shell refused it). Its `CreateNotificationsRule` hook (`command_lib/billingbudgets/hooks.py`) sets `schemaVersion` to `1.0` whenever `--notifications-rule-pubsub-topic` is given. So the budget as created has 1.0, which `budget.json` and `verify.mjs` already expect. `gcpSetup.test.ts` asserts the flag is absent.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a custom role with only `resourcemanager.projects.get`, bound on the project (works, but adds a project-level binding to the deploy account for a check gcloud makes only because of a field we don't need); `roles/browser` or `run.developer` on the project (broader still); the project number as the namespace (gcloud still calls `projects.get` to compare); the regional-endpoint mode, which skips the call (a gcloud property on the runner that CI would have to set and keep, for the same result as dropping one field).
- **Consequences:** Abhinav has no extra command to run for the deploy: re-running CI on `main` after this commit replaces both services.
