If a later Cloud Run call is refused, its error names a `run.*` permission on the service. That is something the per-service `run.developer` role is meant to cover, and the logs would show it. `verify.mjs` reads services by project ID as before; the live services' namespace is Google's business.

## ADR-0189: Diff mode on the website: `?base=<sha>`, both graphs loaded, the diff computed in the browser
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.9e2a)
- **Context:** the explorer draws a comparison from a base graph and a `GraphDiff` (ADR-0182, M6.9b–e1), but the website had no way to ask for one. Nothing stores a diff yet: PR diffs come with the GitHub App (M8). Each analyzed commit's graph is already published and CDN-cached at `/api/graph/<owner>/<repo>/<sha>` (ADR-0069, ADR-0097), and `packages/diff` is pure TypeScript with only the schema as a dependency (it's in the viewer's bundle already, for Mermaid labels).
- **Decision:**
  - **The link:** `?base=<full SHA>` on `/<owner>/<repo>` and `/<owner>/<repo>/<sha>` compares that commit (base) with the page's (head). The viewer's codec names it (`BASE_PARAM`) and writes it first (`withBase`); `decodeState` skips it like any parameter it doesn't know, since it picks which graphs load rather than what the explorer shows. A repeated `base` takes the last value, as the codec does for its own parameters.
  - **The server** looks it up only once the page's diagram is ready (`viewBase`): a full lowercase SHA with a published analysis of the same repo gives its graph URL; a full SHA without one is `missing`; anything else is `invalid`. Like a permalink, it never asks GitHub and never enqueues: viewing a comparison doesn't analyze the base commit.
  - **The browser** loads both graphs side by side, then diffs them (`diffGraphs`, `compareGraphs` in `apps/web/lib/compare.ts`). The link is decoded against the overlay's hierarchy, so a link to a removed node opens it rather than being "left out". Base's code loads from GitHub at the base commit (ADR-0187).
  - **Fail visibly:** a `missing` or `invalid` base, or graphs from two analyzer versions (`IncompatibleGraphsError`: every difference the analyzer made would read as a change), are said in the page's note, and the diagram is the page's commit alone. A failed load of either graph goes on the error card, with Try again reloading both. A missing base stays in the link (it compares once that commit is analyzed); an invalid one is dropped at the next state change and from Copy link.
- **Alternatives rejected:** computing the diff on the server per view (a function invocation and two bucket or CDN reads per page view, for a result the browser computes from the graphs it already fetches); storing a diff per commit pair (no pair is known before someone asks; M8 stores PR diffs); fetching both commits' files from GitHub so renames pair by content (dozens of requests per view against GitHub's unauthenticated limit; without contents a moved file still pairs when its content is unchanged, ADR-0086, and a moved, edited one shows as removed plus added); accepting short SHAs or branch names (needs a GitHub lookup, and a branch name would make a link's meaning drift).
- **Consequences:** the comparison costs the browser one diff of two graphs on the main thread, not yet timed on a pair at the supported size (ADR-0138); if it shows, it can move into the layout worker. Without the top bar the website can't yet switch Base/Head/Overlay or leave the comparison (M6.9e2b, ADR-0190). The repo bar's status still describes the page's commit only; the explorer's chip names both.

## ADR-0190: Diff mode's controls without the top bar: the switch on the canvas, `view` in the link, Stop comparing to the permalink
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-02, M6.9e2b)
- **Context:** the website hides the explorer's top bar (its repo bar has the share menu and Add to README), and the top bar was the only home of Base / Head / Overlay (ADR-0183). With `?base=` (ADR-0189) a reader could see the comparison but not switch versions, keep a version in a link, or leave the comparison except by editing the URL.
- **Decision:**
  - **The switch:** with `topBar` off, the explorer draws the same `SegmentedControl` ("Diff view") on the canvas, in the top-left pin beside the "Comparing" chip, as one row of two 32px overlays (the switch's own frame dropped inside the overlay's). It calls the same `showVersion`, so selection, traces, and announcements behave as from the top bar. With the top bar it stays there only.
  - **The link:** `ExplorerState.version` (`DiffView`, `overlay` by default) is written as `view=base` or `view=head`, first among the state's parameters; the Overlay writes nothing. `decodeState` reads it whatever the graph (an unknown value is noted like any). The explorer takes it as `initialVersion`, only in diff mode, and starts on the Overlay instead when that version lacks the linked selection or trace (the node wins, as a changes-list pick does, ADR-0186). Outside diff mode it reports `overlay`, so the website keeps a `view` only beside a base: while comparing, or with a base not analyzed yet (kept like the base itself, so the link opens on it later); never without one.
  - **On the repo bar:** once compared, "compared with" and the base commit's chip (linked to GitHub at that commit, like the head's); with any `base` (also missing or invalid), **Stop comparing**, a link to this commit's permalink with the same state minus `view`. A link, not a button: it opens in a new tab and works before the explorer loads, and the permalink is where the state's node IDs belong (ADR-0122). What only base had is then left out, and the note says so (fail visibly).
- **Alternatives rejected:** a second top bar or a toolbar row just for diff mode (more chrome above a canvas that's the hero, DESIGN_SYSTEM §2); Stop comparing back to `/<owner>/<repo>` (that page follows the latest commit, so the state could name nodes of another commit); `view` as a page parameter beside `base` read only by the website (the explorer would still need to report it, and the codec is where a link's state is checked); an × on the "Comparing" chip as the exit (the chip is the explorer's, which doesn't know the page's URL; the host would need a callback for something a plain link does).
- **Consequences:** a hand-made link naming Base or Head with a node only the other version has opens on the Overlay without saying why (the explorer can't add to the page's note); rare, since the explorer never writes such a link. At narrow widths the repo bar wraps the compare group onto its own line; the inspector still squeezes the canvas there (not new in M6.9e2b).

## ADR-0191: next-connect routers in `pages/api`: one route per registered method, `ALL` kept when methods may be unseen
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-03, M5B.1)
- **Context:** batch 2's B1 (`eval/results/2026-11-batch2-diagnosis.md`): a `pages/api` file whose default export is a next-connect router has no function to read `req.method` checks from, so it became one `ALL` route, and scoring (method + path) counted every request to it as a wrong route plus a miss. The fix must follow next-connect's own contract, not that repo: v0 (`nc(options)`, the package's default export) returns a router whose `.get/.head/.post/.put/.patch/.delete/.options/.all(…)` and `.use(…)` return the router; v1 (`createRouter()`) has the same methods, and a pages route default-exports `router.handler(options)`. In both, a method call may take a route pattern first, matched against the request URL.
- **Decision:**
  - **Which exports:** the default export (an `export default` value or `export { x as default }`) when it's a router: a call of next-connect's factory found through the checker's declaration of the callee (default import, `createRouter` named import, `require("next-connect")`, so a shadowing local doesn't count), a chain of router methods on one, v1's `.handler(…)` on one, a variable whose initializer is one, or a call of a repo function every return of which is one (`apiHandler = () => nc(opts).use(mw)`), within 8 hops.
  - **Which methods:** registrations in the chain itself and in every call chain on the router's variables in their own file, each method one route at its first registration (the method name's location). `.all` is `ALL`; `.use` registers nothing (middleware). Each handler argument naming a symbol node gets a `handles` edge (middleware included, as Express's do), and inline handlers are recorded as `NextFacts.inlineHandlers` (node key ⇒ route IDs), merged with Express's for the Prisma, HTTP, SDK, and queue owners, so a request inside `.get(async (req, res) => …)` is drawn from `GET`.
  - **Fail visibly:** a pattern argument (`.post("/api/items/:id", h)`), a `.trace`/`.connect` registration, or the router reaching other code (`register(handler)`) means methods may exist that the graph doesn't show; the file keeps its `ALL` route beside the seen ones, with a `NEXT_CONNECT_UNSEEN_METHODS` warning naming why. A router with no method registered is one `ALL` route, as before. Patterns are not resolved against the file's path: where a pattern applies depends on how the catch-all file and the URL line up, which is a guess.
  - **Cache:** `NextFacts` gained a field, so `KEY_FORMAT` 5 → 6 (old entries would be reused without it).
- **Alternatives rejected:** reading methods by name on any default-exported object with `.get`/`.post` calls (would turn any object with those methods into routes; the factory check is what makes it next-connect's contract); resolving patterns into their own routes (`POST /api/items/:id` from a `[...slug].ts` file) by joining them with the file's path (next-connect matches the full URL, so joining is wrong, and taking the pattern as the full path ignores Next's `basePath`); dropping the `ALL` route when a pattern is seen (an endpoint the code serves would vanish from the graph).
- **Consequences:** methods registered through `.use(subRouter)`, on an exported router variable from other files, in a wrapped default export, or by v1's `router.run(req, res)` inside a function default export are not seen; the first two can leave a route out without a warning (listed in `next.ts` "Patterns not handled"). Batch 2's B1 repo is now seen (M5B.4 reports it as such).
- **Amended (2026-11-04, M5B.7c, M5B review findings 2–4 and 6):** those two now warn. A router mounted with `.use(x)`, where `x` is itself a next-connect router (checked the same way, without recording its methods; middleware isn't one), an exported router variable (`export const`, or `export { r as name }` under any name but `default`), and a module-level router variable declared in another file (one instance shared by several pages) each add an unseen reason, so the file keeps `ALL` with `NEXT_CONNECT_UNSEEN_METHODS`. A function-local router in a repo factory is a fresh one per call and doesn't count. `export { r as default }` in the page file is its default export, not an escape. The kept `ALL` is added only when `.all(…)` didn't register one, so the file no longer duplicates itself (`NEXT_DUPLICATE_ROUTE`). A router whose only registrations were unseen (`nc().post("/x", h)`) keeps its warnings instead of falling back to a silent `ALL`. `KEY_FORMAT` 11 → 12.

## ADR-0192: The worker applies migrations at startup, under an advisory lock, through Neon's direct endpoint
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-03, M5.17e6, planning #17)
- **Context:** the worker's first real revision (`gitinside-worker-00003-9l9`) exited ~1 s after starting: its startup sweep's `update "analyses"` failed because production's Neon database had no tables. On the Oracle plan, `provision.sh` ran the migrations; the Cloud Run setup (ADR-0111, ADR-0151) has no such step, and `infra/gcp/check.mjs` migrated its throwaway database by hand before starting the worker, so the image check couldn't see it. Abhinav asked for migrations on every deploy, before the new revision takes traffic, keyless, least-privilege, free; over an unpooled connection if needed; and for the startup failure to log the database's own error, not only Drizzle's "Failed query".
- **Decision:**
  - **Where:** the worker migrates as its first startup step (`startWorkerProcess`, before the pool opens, the sweep, and `listen`) with `@gitinside/db`'s `runMigrations`. Cloud Run sends a revision traffic only after its startup probe (`GET /health`) passes, which needs `listen`, so no revision serves on a schema older than its code, and a failed migration fails the start (logged, exit 1, retried by Cloud Run) while the previous revision keeps serving.
  - **Two at once:** `runMigrations` takes a session advisory lock (`MIGRATION_LOCK_KEY`) on a one-connection client before Drizzle's migrator and releases it after, so two instances (or the worker and the `migrate` CLI) take turns and the second finds nothing pending. `lock_timeout` is 15 s for the whole session: the lock wait, and each DDL statement's wait for a table that queries hold (a waiting `ALTER TABLE` queues every later query on that table, so the wait stays short). Failing then is `55P03`; Cloud Run retries the start.
  - **Connection:** session advisory locks and DDL need a real session, which Neon's PgBouncer (transaction pooling) doesn't give. `DATABASE_URL_UNPOOLED` when set, else `directUrlOf(DATABASE_URL)`: Neon names the pooled endpoint `<endpoint ID>-pooler.<region>.neon.tech`, and the same role and password reach the direct one without `-pooler`; any other host (local Postgres) is used as is.
  - **Logs:** `startup_failed` carries `causes`, each wrapped error's message and SQLSTATE `code` down the `cause` chain (at most 5), so Drizzle's "Failed query" comes with `relation "analyses" does not exist` (42P01). Messages only, no stacks; neither postgres.js nor Postgres puts a password in one. A migration failure is `message: "migrations failed"` with the database's reason below it. Each start logs `migrations_applied` with its time.
  - **Checked:** `check.mjs` now starts the worker on an empty database and requires `migrations_applied` in its log. `runMigrations` is tested on real Postgres (CI): two runs on an empty database apply each migration once, and a held lock makes a run wait and then fail with `55P03`.
- **Alternatives rejected:** a step in CI's deploy job (the deploy account would need the database password, a new secret binding, and a GitHub runner reaching Neon; and a manual redeploy of an unchanged image would skip nothing it needs); a Cloud Run Job running `migrate` from the image before `services replace` (a new resource, `run.jobs.run` for the deploy account, more for `verify.mjs`, and executions billed apart, for what one startup step does); a new `worker-database-url-unpooled` secret (a fifth secret leaves no free Secret Manager version for the HMAC rotation in the README, which needs two, and adds a read per cold start, while the direct URL is the pooled one's host without `-pooler`); migrating through the pooled endpoint (no session for the lock).
- **Consequences:** a migration must keep the previous revision working while the new one starts (add a column or table; rename or drop in a later commit once nothing reads it), since the old revision serves until the new one is ready. The website (Vercel) deploys on push, minutes before CI's deploy migrates, so a website change that needs a new column must land after the migration's deploy, or tolerate its absence. A start pays the migrator's check (one connection, a lock, a read of `drizzle.__drizzle_migrations`) when nothing is pending, tens of ms. A Neon URL with a custom host (not `*.neon.tech`) needs `DATABASE_URL_UNPOOLED`. The `migrate` CLI stays for local development.

## ADR-0193: SWR hooks call the repo's fetcher: inlined at the hook with each key it may pass
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-03, M5B.2)
- **Context:** batch 2's B2 (`eval/results/2026-11-batch2-diagnosis.md`): a library calls the repo's fetcher, so no repo code does. With `fetcher = (url) => axios.get(url)` handed to `useSWR(key, fetcher)`, the fetcher is a wrapper (M2.3) with no visible caller, so its request stayed `DYNAMIC_URL` inside it and the hooks, where the URL is written, got nothing. The fix must follow SWR's own contract, not that repo. SWR 2's documented contract: `useSWR(key, fetcher?, options?)` (also `useSWR(key, options)`) calls `fetcher(key)` when the key is truthy; a falsy key, or a function key that returns or throws a falsy one, makes no request; a function key is called with no arguments; `useSWRImmutable` (`swr/immutable`) is the same hook without revalidation; `useSWRInfinite(getKey, fetcher)` (`swr/infinite`) calls `getKey(pageIndex, previousPageData)` and then `fetcher(key)`; `useSWRMutation(key, fetcher)` (`swr/mutation`) calls `fetcher(key, { arg })` and takes no global fetcher; `preload(key, fetcher)` calls it once; a hook with no fetcher uses the nearest `<SWRConfig value={{ fetcher }}>` above it. An array key is passed whole in SWR 2 and spread into the arguments in SWR 1.
- **Decision:**
  - **Which hooks:** the five exports above, found through the checker's declaration of the callee (an import from `swr`, `swr/immutable`, `swr/infinite`, or `swr/mutation`), so a repo function named `useSWR` doesn't count. Only files that import from one of those modules are looked at.
  - **The call:** a hook whose fetcher (its second argument, or the `fetcher` of a config object there, for the hooks that take one) names a wrapper becomes a call to that wrapper at the hook (`Caller.hook` in `wrappers.ts`), placed and owned like a direct call, so the request is drawn from the hook's caller and `via` the fetcher. The key is bound to the fetcher's first parameter; the rest are absent (defaults apply), or unknown for `useSWRMutation`'s `{ arg }`.
  - **Keys:** `null`, `undefined`, `false`, and the falsy side of a ternary or `&&` make no request; a function key (inline, or a name for a function) gives each value it returns, read the same way (so `getKey`'s `return null` at the last page drops out, and its `pageIndex` is a hole, usually in the query); one request per distinct URL. An array key is `DYNAMIC_URL` with its own detail: the fetcher's first parameter is the array in SWR 2 and its first element in SWR 1, and the analyzer doesn't read the installed version. Any other key goes through the URL normalizer like a wrapper argument, so a key it can't read is unresolved at the hook, and a key that is the caller's own parameter (a custom hook `useApi(path)`) is `WRAPPER_DEPTH`.
  - **SWRConfig:** a hook given no fetcher uses `SWRConfig`'s when every `<SWRConfig value={…}>` rendered in its service sets `fetcher` to the same wrapper (an object literal or a const object), at `heuristic`: which hooks render under which config is a property of the React tree at runtime, not of the code. A config whose fetcher isn't a wrapper, spread props, or two different fetchers give no default.
  - **Cache:** what a service's HTTP facts hold changed without their shape changing, so `KEY_FORMAT` 6 → 7.
- **Alternatives rejected:** treating every call that passes a wrapper as an argument as a call to it (a callback can be called with anything; only the library's contract says the key is the URL); binding an array key's first element (right for SWR 1 only, and the version is in `node_modules`, which is never installed); `SWRConfig`'s fetcher at `exact` (two configs, or a hook rendered outside the provider, are invisible statically); reading SWR's package version from `package.json` (a range, not the installed version).
- **Consequences:** an inline fetcher (`useSWR(key, (url) => fetch(url))`) and the docs' `(...args) => fetch(...args)` aren't wrappers (the first isn't a symbol, the second's URL is a rest element), so their requests stay `DYNAMIC_URL` in place (planned as M5B.2b, since the docs' form is SWR's most common fetcher); a `fetcher` in the third (options) argument, `SWRConfig` values that are functions, `require("swr")`, and re-exports of SWR's hooks aren't read. TanStack Query and RTK Query come in M5B.3 on the same `Caller.hook` path. Batch 2's B2 repo is now seen (M5B.4 reports it as such).

## ADR-0194: SWR fetchers written at the hook, and `(...args) => fetch(...args)`, inlined like wrappers
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-03, M5B.2b)
- **Context:** ADR-0193's consequences: two of SWR's most common fetcher shapes weren't wrappers. A fetcher written at the hook (`useSWR(key, (url) => fetch(url))`, `{ fetcher: (url) => … }` in a hook's config or in `SWRConfig`'s `value`) is no symbol, so nothing could call it; SWR's documented `const fetcher = (...args) => fetch(...args).then((res) => res.json())` is a symbol, but its URL is a spread of a rest parameter, which the collector reads as "can't be read". Both left the request `DYNAMIC_URL` inside the fetcher.
- **Decision:**
  - **Fetchers written in place:** a function that is a call's second argument or an object literal's `fetcher` property, and whose own parameters reach a site's URL, config, or method, holds that site as an "inline fetcher" (`inlineFetchers` in `wrappers.ts`), not as a plain site. Only the syntax is checked there; `callersIn` makes the SWR hook whose fetcher (or `SWRConfig` default) is that function a caller of it, through the same `Caller.hook` path and key rules as ADR-0193. Its requests go into the service's own requests, without `via` (it isn't a symbol). With no hook calling it (any other library's callback, or only `null` keys), its sites keep their own requests, so no site is dropped.
  - **Rest parameters:** a `fetch` call whose only argument is a spread records it (`HttpCallSite.spread`). When that spread is the wrapper's rest parameter, the caller's argument at the rest parameter's position is the URL and the next one the init: absent ⇒ `GET`, after a spread or under `useSWRMutation` (`{ arg }`) ⇒ unknown. An array key stays `DYNAMIC_URL` (ADR-0193): with the spread it would be the URL in SWR 1 and an array passed to `fetch` in SWR 2.
  - **Cache:** a service's HTTP facts change for the same inputs, so `KEY_FORMAT` 7 → 8.
- **Alternatives rejected:** giving inline fetchers synthetic wrapper IDs (they'd enter the repo-wide wrapper name index and the cached `known` wrapper lists, which are symbol IDs by contract); checking the hook at collection time (the hook lookup already lives in `callersIn`, and an unmatched candidate falls back to exactly what it was before); binding a spread rest parameter for axios too (`axios.get(...args)` isn't SWR's documented form, and its config position depends on the method).
- **Consequences:** a fetcher written as an object method (`{ fetcher(url) {…} }`, which `objectProperty` doesn't read), a named function declared inside a component, `axios(...args)`, and `fetch` with other arguments around the spread aren't handled.

## ADR-0195: A negated `req.method` test's block either refuses or serves, read from what it calls
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-03, M5B.2c)
- **Context:** batch 2's last miss (B3, `eval/results/2026-11-batch2-diagnosis.md`): a pages-router handler whose whole body is `if (req.method !== "POST" && req.method !== "DELETE" && req.method !== "PUT") { … }` serves every method but those three. `handledMethods` treated every negated test as a guard refusing the others (`if (req.method !== "POST") return res.status(405)…`), so the file got routes for exactly the three methods it excludes, and a `GET` to it found no route. The polarity was also read only from the operator (`!(req.method === "GET")` counted as positive) or one `!` straight over `includes`.
- **Decision:**
  - **Polarity:** a method test's polarity is its operator flipped by each `!` between it and the `if` whose condition it is, through parentheses, `&&`, and `||`. A test outside an `if` condition keeps its operator's polarity, as before.
  - **Refusing or serving:** a negated test's `then` block refuses the other methods when it names 405 or `Allow`, or when every call in it is on the response parameter or `console` (`return res.status(400).json(…)`, `throw`, a bare `return`); that is the old reading. Otherwise it does work for the other methods, so the handler is open (an `ALL` route), and the methods it names become routes only when an `else` or code after the `if` (at any level up to the body) can serve them.
  - **`req.method ?? ""`** counts as the method (strict types ask for it in `.includes(…)`).
  - **Cache:** `NextFacts` change for the same inputs, so `KEY_FORMAT` 8 → 9.
- **Alternatives rejected:** judging the block by whether it returns early (a serving block returns its response too); treating every negated test as serving (each `if (req.method !== "POST") return res.status(405)` guard would add an `ALL` route); reading which methods reach the code after the block (it would need the request's path through the function, for a rare shape).
- **Consequences:** a refusal through a helper (`return methodNotAllowed(res)`, no 405 in the block) now reads as serving and adds an `ALL` route: an extra route drawn, never one dropped. The fixture is `fixtures/next-method-exclusions`, written from the general form, not from the eval repo.
- **Amendment (2026-11-03, M5B.7d, M5B review finding 5):** "every call is on the response or `console`" read two common refusals as serving, each adding an `ALL` route that a client's wrong-method call could then match, hiding a broken connection from the PR bot: a message built by a call (`res.status(400).json({ error: t("…") })`, `String(e)`) and a log line before the answer (`logger.warn(…)`, `req.log.error(…)`). Now:
  - **Logging:** a call is a log line when it's a statement (optionally awaited, its result unused) calling a method named after one of Node's `console` levels (`log`, `info`, `warn`, `error`, `debug`, `trace`) or `fatal` (pino's and bunyan's top level), on any receiver. The rule is the call's shape, not the receiver's name, so `logger`, `log`, `req.log`, and `this.logger` all count, and no eval repo's names were used.
  - **Arguments:** inside an answering or logging call's arguments, a synchronous call builds the message and isn't work; an awaited one (`res.json({ items: await db.list() })`) reads what's sent, so it still serves.
  - **Cost:** a block that serves data from a synchronous call in the answer (`res.json(listCached())`) now reads as a refusal, so its `ALL` route is missing (a client's call to it shows unresolved, not hidden). A logger called as a plain function (`debug("…")`) still reads as work (an extra route). Rejected: an error status in the chain as the only signal (`res.json({ error: t(…) })` has none) and a list of logger receiver names (it would fit the repos we've read, not the shape).
  - **Cache:** `NextFacts` change for the same inputs, so `KEY_FORMAT` 12 → 13.

## ADR-0196: RTK Query endpoints are requests at their definition; TanStack Query's key-in-context isn't followed
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-03, M5B.3)
- **Context:** M5B.2 taught the analyzer SWR's fetcher contract. TanStack Query and RTK Query were the other two data-fetching libraries named in the batch 2 diagnosis (B2's general form). Neither caused a batch 2 miss, so this is about generality. They fail differently. TanStack's `queryFn` is ordinary repo code: `queryFn: () => fetch("/api/todos")` or a call to a wrapper there already resolves, and only a URL read from the key in the function's context (`({ queryKey }) => fetch(queryKey[0])`, the documented default-`queryFn` pattern) is lost, as a visible `DYNAMIC_URL`. RTK Query's `fetchBaseQuery` makes the `fetch` inside the library, so an app whose API slice uses it showed no request at all.
- **Decision:**
  - **RTK Query, from its contract:** `createApi({ baseQuery: fetchBaseQuery({ baseUrl }), endpoints: (build) => ({ x: build.query({ query }) }) })` (also `build.mutation` and `build.infiniteQuery`, an `endpoints` method, and `api.injectEndpoints` on a `const` holding a `createApi` call) gives one HTTP site per value `query` returns, at the endpoint's `build.query(…)` call. RTK's source (`fetchBaseQuery.ts`) reads a string as `{ url }` and passes the rest to `new Request`, so `method` defaults to `GET`. Its `joinUrls` strips the same slashes as axios's `baseURL` join, so the existing normalizer joins `baseUrl`. The client is `fetch`, which is what runs. `fetchBaseQuery` counts only when it's imported from `@reduxjs/toolkit/query(/react)`: directly, through a `const`, or inside RTK's `retry`. Any other base query is left alone, since its own `fetch`/axios calls are collected as before.
  - **TanStack Query:** no contract added. The key reaches `queryFn` as a property of a destructured context object, which the binding machinery (positional identifier parameters) doesn't model. Such a request already fails visibly, so it stays `DYNAMIC_URL` and is documented in `http.ts`.
  - **Cache:** a service's HTTP facts hold new sites for the same inputs, so `KEY_FORMAT` 9 → 10.
- **Alternatives rejected:** placing RTK requests at the components that call the generated hooks (`useGetTaskQuery`, `api.endpoints.x.initiate`): their names are derived from endpoint names at runtime (`use` + capitalized name + `Query`), and linking them means modeling destructuring of the API object. It's worth doing later if eval shows the need. Treating a custom base query as an unreadable request per endpoint (it may not be HTTP at all, e.g. GraphQL, and its body's requests are already collected). Binding TanStack's `queryKey` (it needs destructured-parameter binding through array indexing, for a pattern no eval repo uses).
- **Consequences:** an RTK request is drawn from the API slice's symbol (`tasksApi`), not from the page that uses it. A `fetchBaseQuery` with a default `method` in its fetch options, a custom `baseQuery` wrapping `fetchBaseQuery` (`baseQueryWithReauth`), `buildCreateApi`, and `queryFn` endpoints aren't read. The fixture is `fixtures/rtk-query`.
- **Amendment (2026-11-03, M5B.7b, M5B review finding 1):** "leave any other base query alone" assumed its own requests are collected. RTK's documented re-auth wrapper calls a `fetchBaseQuery` result (`rawBaseQuery(args, api, extraOptions)`), which isn't a `fetch` or axios call, so batch 3's Recipen had 25 endpoints that made no site and no unresolved node: dropped, against "fail visibly". Now an endpoint with a `query` under a `createApi` whose `baseQuery` isn't readable as `fetchBaseQuery` (a custom or re-auth function, `retry` around one, or a value that can't be read) is one HTTP site at its `build.query(…)` call with no URL and no method, so an unresolved `DYNAMIC_URL` node whose detail says the base query isn't followed. The method isn't read from `query` either, since the base query decides what it sends. The rejected alternative above ("it may not be HTTP at all, e.g. GraphQL") is the cost: a non-HTTP base query now shows unresolved request nodes, an honest "not followed" rather than a silent gap; following the wrapper to `fetchBaseQuery` stays M5B.6b (#18). A builder destructured in the `endpoints` parameter (`({ query, mutation: send }) => …`) is read like `build.query`, since RTK's builder methods don't use `this`. `KEY_FORMAT` 10 → 11 (new sites for the same inputs). Batch 3's blind result stays quoted as measured.
- **Amendment (2026-11-04, M5C.2):** a base query that passes its arguments on unchanged to a `fetchBaseQuery` (the re-auth wrapper above) is now read as that `fetchBaseQuery`, and a call of a `fetchBaseQuery` result in repo code is a request: see ADR-0201.
- **Amendment (2026-11-04, M5C.3f, COVERAGE calling row 12): TanStack Query's key in context is followed.** "No contract added" is replaced, on SWR's `Caller.hook` path (ADR-0193, ADR-0194). TanStack's documented contract: a query runs `queryFn(context)`, and `context.queryKey` is the query options' `queryKey`; options with no `queryFn` use the `QueryClient`'s `defaultOptions.queries.queryFn` (the docs' "Default Query Function" example, `({ queryKey }) => axios.get(\`…${queryKey[0]}\`)`).
  - **Which calls:** `useQuery`, `useSuspenseQuery`, `useInfiniteQuery`, `useSuspenseInfiniteQuery`, `usePrefetchQuery`, `usePrefetchInfiniteQuery`, `queryOptions`, and `infiniteQueryOptions`, imported from `@tanstack/react-query` or `react-query` (v3 has the same context), found through the import like SWR's hooks. Their first argument must be an object literal or a `const` object; a spread that may set `queryFn` leaves the call alone. `queryOptions` only returns its options, so its request is drawn where the key is written, and a `useQuery(todoOptions)` given such a result isn't read again (it's a call, not an object), so nothing is counted twice.
  - **The binding:** the call is a call of its `queryFn` (a wrapper, or a function written as an object's `queryFn`, now a fetcher position like `fetcher`), with the first parameter bound to the options object. Destructured parameters are now bound too, but the URL normalizer reads only a context's key through them: `({ queryKey })`, `({ queryKey: [path, id] })`, `ctx.queryKey`, and an element at a literal index (`queryKey[0]`). It follows the options' `queryKey` property to its array literal (or a `const` holding one) and reads the element in the caller's scope, so `` [`/posts/${postId}`] `` gives `/posts/{}`. A key that is the caller's own parameter (a custom `useApi(key)`) is `WRAPPER_DEPTH` when it starts the URL, as for SWR.
  - **Default `queryFn`:** like `SWRConfig`'s fetcher, used at `heuristic` when every `new QueryClient({ defaultOptions: { queries: { queryFn } } })` in the service that sets one sets the same; a client that sets none (`new QueryClient()`, usual in tests) is left out, and one whose options can't be read, or two different defaults, give none.
  - **Unchanged:** a `queryFn` that doesn't read its context (`() => fetch("/api/todos")`) resolves in place as before. A parameter in a later segment (`` fetch(`/api/${kind}`) ``) doesn't make the function a fetcher (ADR-0164's leading-part rule), so it stays a request at its own site with a hole.
  - **Not handled:** v3/v4's positional `useQuery(key, fn, options)` (v4 and v5 document the object form), `useQueries`, `QueryClient` methods (`fetchQuery`, `prefetchQuery`, `ensureQueryData`, `setQueryDefaults`, `setDefaultOptions`: the instance's type needs the package, which is never installed), a key read with `.join()` or at a computed index, a key built by a call (a key factory), and the Vue, Solid, Svelte, and Angular adapters (outside v1). Each leaves the `queryFn`'s own site as it was, `DYNAMIC_URL` when its URL reads the key.
  - **Alternatives rejected:** binding a destructured parameter's every property (`({ path }) => fetch(path)` would start inlining wrappers no contract describes; only TanStack's says what the context holds); reading `queryKey[0]` as the URL whatever the key (a wrong guess for `["todos", id]` keys with a URL-building `queryFn`, which instead read the element they use); the positional form (a different argument shape for a major that's two versions old).
  - **Cache:** a service's HTTP facts change for the same inputs, so `KEY_FORMAT` 18 → 19. The fixture is `fixtures/tanstack-query-key`; `fixtures/rtk-query`'s `useStats` trend query now reads `/api/stats/trend`, which no route serves, so it's an unmatched request rather than `DYNAMIC_URL`.

## ADR-0197: Service programs share a parsed file when TypeScript would parse and bind it alike
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-04, M5.13a4a)
- **Context:** each service gets its own TypeScript program (ADR-0177's lazy programs), and each program parsed every repo file it reached, so a file that five services import was parsed and bound five times and kept in memory five times. On cal.diy at `2eb48ec` (113 services, 5,148 repo files loaded) that was 7,636 parses, 1.48 per file (`bench/results/2026-11-04-caldiy-stages.md`). Only TypeScript's own `lib.*.d.ts` files were shared. Building programs was the largest stage on one vCPU (12.0 s of 44.3 s), and the heap ended at 81% of its cap.
- **Decision:**
  - **One `SourceFile` per parse, per run.** `buildServicePrograms` keeps a map of the repo files it has parsed, and the host hands a program the same object when the key matches: the program's settings key, the parse options TypeScript passes the host (language version, implied module format, JSDoc parsing mode), the file's content hash, and its name. The map lives for one `buildServicePrograms` call, so nothing crosses runs or repos.
  - **The settings key is TypeScript's:** `DocumentRegistry.getKeyForCompilationSettings`, the key its language service uses to share source files between projects. It covers every option that changes a parse or a bind (`target`, `module`, `jsx`, `jsxImportSource`, `importHelpers`, `moduleResolution`, `moduleDetection`, computed `alwaysStrict`, and the unreachable-code/unused-label/fallthrough flags). We don't keep our own list. Its last field, `pathsBasePath`, is left out (set to `undefined` before asking): TypeScript adds it because the same key also keys module resolution, and it only changes how `paths` resolve. Left in, every service with its own `paths` (cal.diy's web, features, atoms, app-store) parsed alone.
  - **Inputs unchanged:** the host still reads the file (through the run's shared `RepoFiles`) and records its hash in the asking service's input log before looking up the parse, so each service's observed inputs are what they were (ADR-0090).
- **Why it's safe:** TypeScript's checkers keep their per-program state (node and symbol links, merged globals) in the checker, and bind a file once whatever program asks first, which is what lets tsserver share files between projects. The key holds every option the binder reads. Our detectors already pair each node with the program they ask about (`checkerOf(sp)`), and their node- and symbol-keyed caches are either per service or read only syntax and local scope (`lets.ts`).
- **Measured:** cal.diy's parses 7,636 → 5,577 (1.08 per file; 313 files still parsed more than once, all in services whose target, module, JSX, or resolution mode differs). Goldens, determinism, warm == cold, and `pnpm eval` unchanged. On one vCPU (`bench/results/2026-11-04-cloudrun-caldiy-m5.13a4a.md`, 18 runs each side) the analysis child's final heap went from 1,975–1,985 to 1,802–1,808 MiB (81% → 74% of its cap) and the container's peak down by about 180 MiB; time moved by less than runners of one CPU model differ (−1.9 s and −1.1 s on two models, +8 s on a third measured an hour apart), so no time gain is claimed.
- **Alternatives rejected:** TypeScript's `DocumentRegistry` itself (`acquireDocumentWithKey`): it wants script snapshots and versions and is built for a language service's edits; our host already has the text and hash. One program for the whole repo: services have different options, and the cache and lazy building (ADR-0090, ADR-0177) are per service. Our own list of parse-affecting options: it would drift from TypeScript's.
- **Consequences:** a file's parse now depends on which program asked first only through options in the key, so it's the same either way. A shared module-resolution cache (each resolution's probes replayed into the asking service's inputs) is M5.13a4b.
- **Amendment (2026-11-04, M5.13a4b): module resolutions stay per program.** A resolution cache shared by programs with the same TypeScript resolution key (`pathsBasePath` included, since it changes resolutions), each entry replaying its reads and probes into every asking service, was built and measured on cal.diy, then not merged (branch `ci/m5.13a4b-shared-resolutions`, `871750a`; `bench/results/2026-11-04-caldiy-m5.13a4b-resolutions.md`). Only 6.5% of resolutions repeat across programs with the same key (24,119 → 22,555; 113 services have 20 keys), and a shared entry needs a complete probe list, so misses ran without TypeScript's cache, which skips probes on its hits. Building every program then took 23.3–24.8 s against 18.3–19.2 s, and the heap grew by about 40–50 MiB. Each program keeps TypeScript's own per-program resolution cache. A test now guards that a shared parse still resolves with each service's own `paths`.

## ADR-0198: The analysis child runs with a 32 MiB semi-space
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-04, M5.13a5); amends ADR-0146's budget
- **Context:** M5.13a2's laptop profile put 12% of cal.diy's CPU in the garbage collector. On one vCPU, V8's helper threads share the same core as the analysis, so collector work can't overlap it. V8's default semi-space with our 2,560 MB heap cap is 16 MiB on Node 22 (a 32 MiB new space). To measure the collector, the child now reports its main-thread pauses by kind (`gcMs`, from `gc` performance entries), which also go into `analyses.stats`. The 1-vCPU workflow can now cycle through semi-space caps run by run on one runner. That matters because two runners can differ by 13% (M5.13a4a).
- **Decision:** `DEFAULT_RUNNER_LIMITS.semiSpaceMb = 32` (`--max-semi-space-size=32` next to the heap cap).
- **Measured** (`bench/results/2026-11-04-cloudrun-caldiy-m5.13a5.md`, four CI CPU models, 38 runs): main-thread pauses are 14–17% of the analysis, 93–97% of them minor.
  - **32 MiB:** cut the pauses by 15–23% (0.9–1.9 s), and the analysis medians by 0.2–1.0 s (0.6–1.8%). It was faster in 13 of 16 paired runs. Final heap +23–28 MiB, container peak +26–44 MiB.
  - **64 MiB:** no faster than 32 MiB on the two models that tried it, for a 120–132 MiB larger heap.
  - **Graphs:** identical across all runs.
- **Why keep a small gain:** it was measured on every CPU model, in the same direction, within each runner. It costs one flag and memory the budget already has: `budget.ts`'s 256 MiB allowance for the child's memory outside its heap (`childOutsideHeap`) covers the 91 MiB measured over the cap plus 32 MiB more young generation.
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - 64 MiB: more memory, no measured gain over 32.
  - Leaving V8's default: slower on every model.
  - `--single-threaded-gc` (no helper threads competing for the one vCPU): not tried. M5.13a5 asked only about the young generation's size, and all pauses together are now 5.0–6.4 s.
- **Consequences:** collector pauses are 5.0–6.4 s of a 34–59 s analysis. The rest is TypeScript and our detectors (M5.13a2), so the collector isn't where the remaining time is. The 60 s budget is met by the analysis alone on all four CI models (33.7–58.5 s). Production's end-to-end 96.3 s is mostly outside the analysis (gitinside-planning#20).

## ADR-0199: A level too big for the 40% floor is fitted on the window holding the most boxes
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-04, M5.13b2); amends ADR-0134's fit
- **Context:** the fit (`fitCamera`) centers a level on an axis where it fits, else puts the level's top-left corner at the inset, so "its first column is in view". cal.diy's system level (113 services, 7 datastores, 30 outside APIs; 369 lines) lays out 11,977 × 16,205 px. At the 40% floor a 1080 × 795 canvas shows 2,580 × 1,668 of it, and that top-left window holds **0** whole boxes: ELK's first layer starts 2,713 px down, and the corner is crossed only by routed lines (M5.13's production screenshot). The densest window of that size holds 15.
- **Decision:** `fitLevel(layout, canvas)` (`explorer/framing.ts`) is the fit at the same zoom, but on an axis that overflows the window is the one holding the most boxes wholly in view. The candidates are windows centered on each box's center, moved back inside the level. Ties go to the leftmost window (where a RIGHT-flowing layout starts), then the topmost. An axis that fits stays where `fitCamera` centers it, and a level that fits is unchanged. The first view, the fallback after a close, and 0 / the fit button all use it.
- **Why the most boxes:** it needs nothing but the layout, so it's deterministic and the same for every repo, and it guarantees the first screen shows boxes whenever any window can. Weighting by "importance" (files, routes, layer) was rejected: it's a guess about what the reader wants, and the minimap already shows where the rest is.
- **Cost:** quadratic in the level's boxes (each candidate counts every box). It runs only when the level overflows at 40%, and the explorer memoizes the camera per layout, canvas, overview, and focus, so hovering doesn't recompute it. cal.diy's 150 boxes are about 22,500 comparisons.
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - Anchoring at the first box in reading order: it can still be a lone box with its neighbors off-screen.
  - Compacting the layout (ELK options for long edges): a layout change for every repo, beyond this task. The sparse layout stays as it is.
- **Consequences:** a big level's first view no longer starts at its top-left corner, so a reader who wants the start of the flow pans there or uses the minimap. Levels that fit at 40% or more look the same as before.

## ADR-0200: A repo page with no analysis asks GitHub for the repo's current name, and follows a rename
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-04, M5.13b3); amends ADR-0068 step 1
- **Context:** `cal.com` was renamed `cal.diy` on GitHub. Its analysis is stored under the name GitHub answered with (`calcom/cal.diy`), so `/calcom/cal.com` found no row by name and said "hasn't been analyzed yet", though GitHub still redirects the old name. The README badge and image at an old URL went link-only the same way. The opposite case fails too: a repo analyzed before a rename is stored under its old name until the next analysis's `upsertRepo`, so its new URL found nothing.
- **Decision:** when the name finds no published analysis (repo pages and images, not permalinks), `viewRepo` asks GitHub through `resolveRepo`, which already follows the API's same-origin `301`:
  - GitHub answers with another owner or name (ignoring case) ⇒ `moved`. The page redirects there (Next's `redirect`, a 307, since a rename can be undone), keeping the query so a deep link survives. The image route draws the new name's view.
  - GitHub answers with the same name ⇒ the published analysis by GitHub's repo ID (`findPublishedAnalysis({ providerRepoId })`), checked on view as usual with that answer (no second lookup). None ⇒ `not-analyzed` in GitHub's spelling.
  - Not found, unavailable, or the cap below used up ⇒ `not-analyzed` as written, as before.
- **Quota:** answers are remembered like check-on-view's (a minute, successes only). Made-up names would each cost a GitHub call, so lookups for unknown names are capped site-wide at 500 an hour (`site:unknown-repo` in `rate_limits`, about a tenth of a token's 5,000), leaving the rest for `/api/analyze` and check-on-view. Past the cap, the page offers "Analyze" unchecked, which asks GitHub itself.
- **Permalinks unchanged:** `/<owner>/<repo>/<sha>` still never asks GitHub (ADR-0068), so a permalink under a name that no longer matches the stored one stays a 404 until the repo is re-analyzed under it. Asking GitHub on every unknown permalink would let any SHA spend quota.
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - Rewriting the stored name on view: a write on a GET, and it would move every graph URL (`/api/graph/<stored name>/…`) the CDN has cached.
  - Remembering former names in the database: GitHub already keeps them, and asking it is the only way to know a name is still a redirect and not taken by another repo.
- **Consequences:** a never-analyzed repo's page now costs one GitHub lookup a minute (it used to cost none), and shows GitHub's spelling.

## ADR-0201: An RTK Query base query that passes its arguments on to `fetchBaseQuery` is that `fetchBaseQuery`
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-04, M5C.2, #18)
- **Context:** batch 3's only cause of misses (C1, `eval/results/2026-11-batch3-diagnosis.md`, 25 of 70 labels in Recipen) is RTK Query's documented re-authorization pattern ("Automatic re-authorization by extending fetchBaseQuery"): `const baseQuery = fetchBaseQuery({ baseUrl })`, then `baseQueryWithReauth = async (args, api, extraOptions) => { let result = await baseQuery(args, api, extraOptions); if (401) { await baseQuery("/refreshToken", api, extraOptions); result = await baseQuery(args, api, extraOptions); } return result; }` as `createApi`'s `baseQuery`. ADR-0196 followed only `fetchBaseQuery` itself, and since M5B.7b every endpoint under such a wrapper is an unresolved `DYNAMIC_URL`. The wrapper's refresh call made no site at all. `docs/COVERAGE.md` counts this form at about 9.2k files.
- **Decision:**
  - **The forwarding wrapper.** A `baseQuery` that is a repo function (arrow, function expression, or declaration; through a `const`, an import, or RTK's `retry`) counts as a `fetchBaseQuery(config)` when its first parameter is a plain identifier that is the first argument of at least one call of that `fetchBaseQuery`'s result (nested callbacks included, so the docs' `async-mutex` variant too), and every other use of the parameter only reads it: a property read never written, `typeof`, or an equality or `instanceof` comparison. RTK calls the base query with the endpoint's `query` value as `args`, and a wrapper that hands `args` on unchanged sends exactly what `fetchBaseQuery` alone would, so the endpoint's URL, method, and `baseUrl` are read as in ADR-0196. Headers, retries, and refreshes around it don't change the URL.
  - **Not followed** (the endpoints stay unresolved, with a detail now saying "isn't fetchBaseQuery or a function passing its arguments on to one"): a parameter that is assigned or has a property written, built into another value that reaches a `fetchBaseQuery` result (`{ ...args, url: "/v2" + args.url }`, a conditional), passed to any other call (another base query, a logger), destructured, or forwarded to two different `fetchBaseQuery` calls. Each of those could change where the request goes, and reading it anyway would be a guess.
  - **A call of a `fetchBaseQuery` result in repo code is a request** at the call, read from its first argument like a `query` value (a string is the URL, an object its `url` and `method`, default `GET`) and joined to that call's `baseUrl`, owned by the enclosing function (`baseQueryWithReauth`). A first argument that is a parameter of the enclosing function is the forwarding itself and makes no site: its requests are the endpoints'. Only services whose program imports RTK Query are searched, so the check costs nothing elsewhere.
  - **Confidence:** the weaker of the hops reached through (imports), as for `fetchBaseQuery` itself; the forwarding rule is structural, not a name match, so it adds no weakening.
  - **Cache:** a service's HTTP facts hold new and different sites for the same inputs, so `KEY_FORMAT` 14 → 15.
  - **Batch 3 is now seen** (`heldOutBatches`, ADR-0168): this fix was chosen with its misses in view. Its blind result (100.0% precision, 64.3% recall) stays quoted as measured; later scores are a seen set.
- **Alternatives rejected:**
  - Following every custom base query into its body: one that isn't HTTP (GraphQL, a local store) would then be read as requests, and one that rewrites `args` would be drawn with the endpoint's unrewritten URL.
  - Matching the wrapper by name (`baseQueryWithReauth`) or by RTK's types (`BaseQueryFn`): names prove nothing, and JavaScript repos (Recipen) have no types.
  - Treating a logged `args` (`console.log(args)`) as a read: a helper the analyzer doesn't follow could just as well rewrite it. That wrapper stays unresolved, visibly.
- **Consequences:** the `rtk-query` fixture's two session endpoints now reach `GET` and `DELETE /api/session`, and its wrapper's refresh call reaches `/api/session/refresh` (new routes in the fixture). A repo helper that takes a URL parameter and hands it to a `fetchBaseQuery` result (`get(path) { return raw(path, api, {}) }`) reads as forwarding, so it makes no site; and a `fetchBaseQuery` result imported from another workspace package into a service whose own program has no RTK import isn't searched. Both are documented in `http.ts`.

## ADR-0202: An API-named env base takes the URL path from a committed example env file, for matching only
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-04, M5C.2b, #18)
- **Context:** batch 3's second cause (C2, `eval/results/2026-11-batch3-diagnosis.md`): Recipen's RTK base is `import.meta.env.VITE_SERVER_BASE_URL` with no fallback in code, and its only value is in `client/.env.example`: `http://localhost:5000/api`. The server mounts every router under `/api`, so all 25 requests (read since ADR-0201) look for `/auth/login` instead of `/api/auth/login` and are `NO_MATCHING_ROUTE`. The same shape (`VITE_API_URL=http://localhost:5000/api`) is how many React apps document their backend. SECURITY.md T5 kept only an env value's scheme, host, and port, discarded the rest at parse time, and never built a graph string from a value, because env files hold secrets, and a URL's path can be one (a webhook's token).
- **Options:**
  1. Leave it unresolved and documented. Safe, but the pattern stays a guaranteed miss.
  2. Match a request's path as a suffix of a route's under an env base (`/auth/login` ⇒ `/api/auth/login`). Needs no value, but it's a guess: `/users` would match `/api/users` and `/admin/users` alike, against "never guessed".
  3. **Read the path of an example file's URL value, and use it only while matching.** Chosen.
- **Decision:**
  - **Which files:** only example env files, by name: `.env.example`, `.env.sample`, `.env.template`, and `.env.<name>.example` (and `.sample`, `.template`). They exist to be published with placeholder values. `.env`, `.env.local`, `.env.production`, and every other env file are never read for paths (their T5 handling is unchanged).
  - **What is read:** for a value that is an `http(s)://` URL or a root-relative path, only its path: no scheme, host, port, credentials, query, or fragment. A path is kept only when it has at most 4 segments, each 1–64 characters of `A–Z a–z 0–9 . _ ~ -` and not `.` or `..` (so no interpolation, percent-encoding, or a long token). Anything else is no path.
  - **Which env var it applies to:** the env base of a request, only when the base already reaches the repo (an API-like name or a loopback fallback, ADR-0165, ADR-0158). The nearest folder above the calling file whose example files define the key decides; several different paths there (two example files, or one key twice) mean no path.
  - **How it's used:** the matcher first matches the request with the path in front (`/api` + `/auth/login`); only if that matches nothing does it match as before. So a code fallback that already added the same prefix (`|| "http://localhost:5000/api"`, ADR-0158) still matches through the second try, and a stale example costs nothing that today's matching had. Confidence stays `heuristic`, as for every env base.
  - **Never in the graph:** the path lives in the matcher only. A matched request's edge carries the route's own label (from the server's code), and an unmatched one keeps today's detail (`{env:VITE_SERVER_BASE_URL}/auth/login`), so no graph string is built from an env value, and a path that matches no route (a token) never shows. SECURITY.md T5 now says so; the `fake-secrets` fixture plants an example file with credentials, a path, a query, and a fragment, all `gi_fake`, behind a request that matches nothing, and the test still finds none of them.
- **Alternatives rejected:** reading paths from `.env` or `.env.development` too (often real values; committed or not, they aren't meant to be published); writing the prefix into the request's URL text (it would then reach unresolved details and the diff's re-resolution, which T5 forbids); trying the path only when no code fallback exists (the URL pattern doesn't record that, and the second try handles it without a cache format change).
- **Consequences:** facts and their cache are unchanged (the match is a global step, ADR-0172), so no `KEY_FORMAT` bump. The diff's re-resolution of an unresolved request (ADR-0087) doesn't know the path, so a request resolved only through it in the head stays as the analysis drew it; only the analysis draws such edges. Fixture `env-example-prefix`.

## ADR-0203: Next.js server actions stay `calls` edges, labeled `server action` when the browser makes them
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-04, M5C.3a, #18)
- **Context:** `docs/COVERAGE.md` ranks server actions 4th among the ways React/Next apps call their backend (1,286,781 files with `"use server"`). A client component's direct call of an action was already a `calls` edge inside the Next service, but nothing said it crosses from the browser to the server, and the most common form, `<form action={createTodo}>`, made no edge at all: it passes the action rather than calling it, so the component and the model the action writes weren't connected. EVALUATION.md had no label rule for them.
- **How Next runs one** (React and Next docs, "Server Functions" / "Server Actions and Mutations"): an action is an async function in a file whose directive prologue holds `"use server"` (every export is one) or whose own body starts with `"use server"`. Calling it from client code, or submitting a form whose `action` / `formAction` is it, sends a `POST` to the current page's URL with a `Next-Action` header naming a build-time ID. A server component calling it directly runs it in-process, with no request.
- **Options:**
  1. A `route` node per action (`POST <page>`) and an `http` edge to it. Rejected: the URL is whichever page the form is on, not a property of the action, and the ID exists only in a build. Any path we drew would be invented, against "never guessed".
  2. A new edge kind (`action`). Rejected for v1: a schema enum change that every reader (viewer legend, filters, trace, diff, Mermaid, PR bot) must learn, for an edge that never leaves its service. Revisit if users need to filter by it.
  3. **Keep the `calls` edge, add the references React makes, and label the browser's.** Chosen.
- **Decision:**
  - **Which functions are actions:** exported function symbols of a file whose prologue holds `"use server"`, and top-level function symbols whose block body's prologue holds it (a `"use server"` string later in the body isn't a directive). Read from syntax only.
  - **New edges for references React calls:** a `calls` edge from the enclosing symbol when an action is the value of `<form action={…}>`, `<button formAction={…}>` or `<input formAction={…}>` (intrinsic elements only), or the first argument of `useActionState` / `useFormState` imported from `react` or `react-dom` (by name, or as a property of a default or namespace import), directly or through `.bind(…)`. The site is the reference's name and has no `args`: React supplies them (form data, previous state), so the diff's `PARAMETERS_ADDED` check doesn't apply to it. A function passed this way that isn't an action makes no edge, so React 19 client-side form handlers don't start a new class of edges by accident (`onClick` handlers make none either).
  - **The label:** a `calls` edge into an action is labeled `server action` when any of its sites leaves the browser: a reference React calls (above), or a call in a file whose prologue holds `"use client"`. A server component's direct call stays an unlabeled `calls` edge. Confidence is unchanged (`exact` for a resolved symbol).
  - **Eval:** an action call isn't an HTTP request written in the repo's code (the request expression is Next's), so it isn't labeled (EVALUATION.md §1, "Not labeled"), and `http` scores don't change.
  - **Cache:** call facts gain `browser`, `passed`, and per-service `actions`, so `KEY_FORMAT` 15 → 16. The label is a global step (an action in another service counts), so a service's facts stay its own inputs' (ADR-0091).
- **Not handled** (documented in `calls.ts`): an action defined inside a component has no symbol node, so the component owns its calls and a form naming it makes no edge; a module without `"use client"` that only client modules import runs in the browser too, but its calls get no label (deciding it means walking every importer, and a shared module can run on both sides); actions handed down through props or context, a custom component's `action` prop (`next/form`'s `<Form>` included), and `require("react")` hooks.
- **Consequences:** the graph schema doesn't change (`label` is already an optional edge field, and a fingerprint, so a call becoming or ceasing to be a browser call is a modified edge in a diff). The viewer doesn't show `calls` labels yet; showing the label on the edge is a follow-up UI task (M5C.3a2). Fixture `next-server-actions`.
- **Amendment (2026-11-04, M5C.3a2):** the viewer draws a `calls` edge labeled `server action` as "calls · server action": the label becomes a muted note, like `http`'s "HTTP", on the line's pill, in the inspector's connection list, in accessible names and tooltips, and in the SVG/Mermaid/README image exports (all read `edgeLabel`). Only that exact label is drawn; any other `calls` label stays hidden, so a future label isn't shown by accident. An aggregated line still says "3 calls" (it can mix both kinds). Story `Explorer/Explorer › Server actions` on the fixture.

## ADR-0204: A root-relative request through a Vite or CRA dev proxy is matched by the path the proxy sends
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-04, M5C.3b, #18)
- **Context:** `docs/COVERAGE.md` ranks dev proxies 3rd among the ways backends receive calls (549,376 Vite configs mention `proxy`). A SPA calls `fetch("/api/users")`, and in development its dev server forwards `/api/*` to the backend, often with `rewrite: (path) => path.replace(/^\/api/, "")`, so the backend's route is `GET /users`. The matcher assumed every proxy keeps the path: such a request matched nothing, or worse, matched an unrelated `/api/users` route, and a proxy to an outside API (`/weather` ⇒ `https://api.weather.example.com`) looked for a route in the repo. CRA's `package.json` `"proxy"` (foodie's, the only one in the eval set) can't rewrite, so only its target matters.
- **Options:**
  1. Leave it documented as not handled. The rewrite form is Vite's documented example, so it stays a common miss and a source of wrong edges.
  2. Strip any leading `/api` when nothing matches. A guess: `/api` may be part of the route as often as not.
  3. **Read the proxy table from the config's syntax and match the path it sends.** Chosen.
- **Decision:**
  - **What is read** (`devProxy.ts`, as syntax with the TypeScript parser, never loaded or run, SECURITY.md T1): Vite's `server.proxy` from the first `vite.config.{js,mjs,ts,cjs,mts,cts}` in Vite's order, exported as an object, `defineConfig(…)`, a function returning one, or a top-level `const`; each entry's key (a string prefix, as Vite matches it), a literal `target` (credentials dropped), and a `rewrite` of the form `(path) => path.replace(<pattern>, "<text>")`, the pattern a regex literal of plain characters (read character by character, never compiled: a repo's regex can't cost us time) or a string. CRA's `"proxy"` from a `package.json` that depends on `react-scripts`.
  - **Which app's proxy:** the nearest folder above the calling file (within its service) holding a Vite config or a `package.json` decides. Only root-relative and `location.origin` requests go through it; a literal or env-var origin goes straight to that server.
  - **How it's matched:** entries are tried in order. The first whose key the request's path starts with takes it: the rewrite is applied, the target's path put in front, and the result matched against every service's routes at `heuristic` (the target's port doesn't say which service listens there, so the caller's own routes lose their `exact`). No fallback to the unrewritten path. A literal target outside the repo (a dotted host that isn't loopback) is an `external_call`, also `heuristic` (a dev proxy says where requests go in development). A single-label host (`http://api:5000`) may be a compose service, so it's matched like loopback.
  - **Fail visibly:** a rewrite of any other shape leaves the request `unresolved` (`NO_MATCHING_ROUTE`, "the dev proxy for /api in client/vite.config.ts rewrites the path in a way that isn't read"), and a proxied path that matches nothing says what was sent ("no route matches GET to /api/orders (sent as /orders by the dev proxy in client/vite.config.ts)"). Where a hole in the path, a regex or computed key, or a spread hides which entry takes a request, it's matched as before.
  - **Not handled** (documented in `devProxy.ts`): which service a loopback port belongs to (no service records its listen port), `bypass` and `configure`, `preview.proxy`, a config's `root`/`base`, CRA's `src/setupProxy.js`, webpack-dev-server's `devServer.proxy`, and Angular's `proxy.conf.json`.
- **Alternatives rejected:** narrowing a loopback target to the service whose `app.listen(port)` matches (needs a new port reading in every server detector; no eval repo has two backends behind one proxy); applying the proxy to every request in the app regardless of its origin (an absolute URL never passes through the dev server).
- **Consequences:** the match is a global step (ADR-0172), so facts, their cache, and `KEY_FORMAT` don't change; config files are read each run, like example env files (ADR-0202). `RouteMatch`'s `external` outcome gains an optional `confidence`. The diff's re-resolution of an unresolved request (ADR-0087) doesn't know about proxies, so a request resolved only through one in the head stays as the analysis drew it. The proxy reaches only the development server, so a production setup that routes differently (nginx, a CDN rewrite) isn't seen; edges stay `heuristic` for that reason. Fixture `vite-proxy`.

## ADR-0205: ofetch requests are read like axios's, with ofetch's own base join
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-04, M5C.3c, #18)
- **Context:** `docs/COVERAGE.md` ranks ofetch 7th among the ways frontends call their backend (348,608 files name `ofetch`; many are Nuxt projects, outside v1). Its documented API is `ofetch(url, { method, baseURL, body, query })` (also exported as `$fetch`), `ofetch.raw(…)` for the raw response, `ofetch.native(…)` for the platform's `fetch`, and `ofetch.create(defaults)` for an instance whose defaults merge over its parent's (`$fetch.create` spreads the new defaults over the old). The method is upper-cased, `GET` when unset. The base joins through ufo's `withBase`: an absolute URL or an empty base leaves the URL alone, a URL that already starts with the base (its trailing `/` dropped) is left alone too, and otherwise one `/` joins them. The collector knew none of it, so every ofetch call was missed (no edge at all, not even unresolved).
- **Options:**
  1. Treat ofetch as `fetch` with a `baseURL`. Misses instances, which are how most apps use it (`export const api = ofetch.create({ baseURL })`).
  2. A second copy of the axios tracer for ofetch values. Two copies of the same tracing to keep in step.
  3. **One value tracer for both libraries** (`createTracer` in `http.ts`), each library saying only what its imports, `require`s, type annotations, and `create` calls give; ofetch's requests and join on top. Chosen.
- **Decision:**
  - **Values:** `ofetch` or `$fetch` imported from `ofetch` (named, renamed, or `of.ofetch` under `import * as of`) is the root: no base, no default method. `x.create(options)` on it or an instance takes `baseURL` and `method` from `options`, else from `x`; a spread in its arguments or options the collector can't see make both unknown. Values are traced through the program exactly as axios's are (variables, imports across workspace packages, factories, `??`, `await`).
  - **Requests:** `x(url, options?)` and `x.raw(url, options?)`: the call's `method`, else the instance's, else `GET`; the call's `baseURL`, else the instance's. `x.native(url, init?)` is a plain `fetch` (no base or default method). Options the collector can't see are assumed not to set a `baseURL`, as axios's shorthand calls already are (`fromConfig` in `http.ts`): otherwise every `api(url, opts)` wrapper would lose its base. Their `method` is left to wrapper inlining (M2.3), which reads it from the caller's arguments, except under an instance with a default `method`, where it's unknown.
  - **Join:** a site carries `baseJoin: "withBase"`, and the URL normalizer then leaves a URL alone when its leading text starts with a literal base, so `ofetch("/api/health", { baseURL: "/api" })` is `/api/health`, not axios's `/api/api/health`. Like ufo, it's a plain prefix test (`/apiary` is under `/api`). A base that isn't one literal (an env var) isn't compared, and the URL is joined as axios would.
  - **Not handled** (documented in `http.ts` and `url.ts`): Nuxt's auto-imported global `$fetch`/`useFetch` (never imported from `ofetch`; Vue is outside v1), `require("ofetch")`, `createFetch`, the old `ohmyfetch` package, a declaration typed `$Fetch` with no traced value, `onRequest` interceptors that change the request, `query`/`params` (only paths are matched), and ufo's handling of protocol-relative URLs and `./`/`../` segments.
- **Alternatives rejected:** recognizing a global `$fetch` by name (in a React app it's any function; in Nuxt it's out of scope); treating ofetch's join like axios's (wrong for URLs that repeat the base, which ofetch's docs allow).
- **Consequences:** the axios tracer moved into `createTracer` unchanged in behavior (every axios test and golden is the same). `HttpClient` gains `"ofetch"`. New request facts for unchanged files, so `KEY_FORMAT` 16 → 17. Fixture `ofetch-client`. No eval repo uses ofetch, so the eval doesn't move.

## ADR-0206: A Next app's routes are matched under its `basePath`, and its own requests through its `rewrites`
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-04, M5C.3d, #18)
- **Context:** `docs/COVERAGE.md` ranks `next.config` rewrites 5th among the ways backends receive calls (86,656 config files mention `rewrites`). Two settings move where a Next app's requests land. `basePath: "/shop"` serves every route and page under `/shop` (`app/api/items/route.ts` is `/shop/api/items`); `next/link` adds it, but `fetch` doesn't, so the app's code writes `fetch("/shop/api/items")`, which matched nothing. `rewrites()` maps a source path to a destination, often the repo's own Express server (`/backend/:path*` ⇒ `http://localhost:4000/:path*`) or an outside API, and the matcher looked for the source path in the repo instead. Next tries rewrites in phases: `beforeFiles` before any file, `afterFiles` (also the plain array form) after static routes and pages but before dynamic ones, `fallback` after both. `basePath` goes in front of each `source` and internal destination unless the entry says `basePath: false`; entries with `has`/`missing` apply only to some requests.
- **Options:**
  1. Leave both documented as not handled. Every request in a `basePath` app stays unmatched, and every rewrite to a backend is a miss.
  2. Rewrite the route nodes' paths with `basePath`. The graph would show `/shop/api/items` where the file says `api/items`, and the diff's re-resolution and every reader would see changed IDs.
  3. **Read both from the config's syntax and apply them in the matcher only.** Chosen.
- **Decision:**
  - **What is read** (`nextConfig.ts`, as syntax with the TypeScript parser and the `ConfigReader` shared with `devProxy.ts`, never loaded or run, SECURITY.md T1): the first `next.config.{js,mjs,ts}` in the folder of a service that depends on `next`, exported as an object, Next's `(phase) => config` function, a top-level `const`, or passed through plugin wrappers (`withBundleAnalyzer(config)`, a call of an identifier named `with…`, its first argument that is a config object). A literal `basePath`; `rewrites` as a function or method returning an array or a `{ beforeFiles, afterFiles, fallback }` object of entries with a literal `source` and a destination that is a literal or one value followed by a literal path (`` `${process.env.API_URL}/:path*` ``, only the env var's name kept). Path patterns are read segment by segment (literals, `:name`, and `:name?`/`*`/`+` last), never compiled.
  - **basePath:** the Next detector's route and page nodes are matched under it, from every service (a custom server's Express routes in the same service aren't). The nodes keep their file-system paths. An unprefixed request from the app that matches nothing says so: "no route matches GET to /api/items (the Next app's basePath in web/next.config.mjs is /shop)".
  - **rewrites:** only the app's own root-relative requests go through them (an absolute URL or another service's request isn't sent to the app's server, as for dev proxies). Phases in Next's order, the app's own static (all-literal) routes and pages checked before `afterFiles` and all of them before `fallback`; the first entry whose source matches takes the request, its params filling the destination. An internal destination is matched like the app's own request (`exact` in its service). A loopback or single-label host, or an env var named like an API base (ADR-0165's list), is matched in every service at `heuristic` (the port doesn't say which service). Another literal host is an `external_call` at `exact`: unlike a dev proxy (ADR-0204), rewrites run in production.
  - **Fail visibly:** a destination whose host or path can't be read, or an env var that isn't API-named, leaves the request `unresolved` (`NO_MATCHING_ROUTE`) with which rewrite in which file did it; a rewritten path that matches nothing says what it became ("rewritten to /nope by web/next.config.js"). Where a hole in the path, a condition (`has`/`missing`), or an entry that can't be read (a spread, a regex param, rewrites built by code) hides whether an entry takes a request, it's matched as without rewrites.
  - **Not handled** (documented in `nextConfig.ts`): a computed `basePath`, chained rewrites, `i18n`/`locale`, `redirects`, `trailingSlash`, case-insensitive matching, `public/` files, middleware, plugins that add their own rewrites, `pageExtensions`, and other services' requests through the app's rewrites.
- **Alternatives rejected:** treating a call of any function as a config wrapper (`makeConfig(…)` may return anything; the `with…` naming is Next's plugin convention, and a plugin's own changes stay unseen); falling back to the unrewritten path when the rewritten one matches nothing (a guess, as ADR-0204 says for dev proxies).
- **Consequences:** like ADR-0204, the match is a global step, so facts, their cache, and `KEY_FORMAT` don't change; the config is read each run. `createRouteMatcher` takes the Next apps (their configs and the Next detector's nodes). The diff's re-resolution (ADR-0087) doesn't know `basePath` or rewrites, so a request resolved only through them in the head stays as the analysis drew it. Fixture `next-rewrites`.

## ADR-0207: ky requests are read with the shared client tracer, for ky 1's and ky 2's bases
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-04, M5C.3e, #18)
- **Context:** `docs/COVERAGE.md` ranks ky 10th among the ways frontends call their backend (35,520 files `import ky`). Its API: `ky(input, options)` (method from `options.method`, `get` by default, upper-cased), `ky.get|post|put|patch|delete|head(input, options)`, which set the method over anything in `options`, `ky.extend(options)` for an instance whose defaults merge over its parent's (or a function of the parent's defaults), and `ky.create(options)` for one with "complete new defaults", inheriting nothing. The base changed between majors: ky 1 has `prefixUrl`, joined with one `/` and throwing ("`input` must not begin with a slash when using `prefixUrl`") when the input starts with `/`; ky 2 (current, 2.2.0) replaced it with `prefix`, a plain string join with the slashes at the boundary trimmed, and added `baseUrl`, resolved like a URL (`users` on `/api/` is `/api/users`, `/users` on it is `/users`). The collector knew none of it, so every ky call was missed outright.
- **Options:**
  1. ky 1 only (`prefixUrl`, what the task named). Wrong for every repo on ky 2, and its `baseUrl` would silently read as no base.
  2. Treat every base as axios's `baseURL`. Wrong for `baseUrl` (a root-relative URL drops its path) and hides ky 1's throw as a working request.
  3. **All three, each joined as its major does,** on the value tracer ADR-0205 shared. Chosen.
- **Decision:**
  - **Values:** the default export of `ky` (`k.default` under `import * as k`) is the root. The tracer now lets each library name its instance makers: `create` for axios and ofetch, `create` and `extend` for ky. `extend(options)` takes `prefixUrl`, `prefix`, `baseUrl`, and `method` from `options`, else from its parent; `create(options)` from `options`, else ky's defaults. A spread, a function, or options the collector can't see make all four unknown.
  - **Requests:** `x(url, options?)` (the call's `method`, else the instance's, else `GET`, read like ofetch's) and the shorthands (their own method). Each base is the call's, else the instance's; options the collector can't see are assumed not to set one (as for ofetch and axios shorthands). An unknown base the instance has makes the base unknown. More than one of the three in effect at once is a request with no URL, `DYNAMIC_URL` saying "more than one of ky's prefixUrl, prefix, and baseUrl is set, which isn't followed": no repo needs it, and ky 1 and ky 2 read them differently.
  - **Joins** (`baseJoin` on the site, `url.ts`): `prefix` and `prefixUrl` join as axios's `baseURL`; under `prefixUrl` a URL starting with `/` is `DYNAMIC_URL` "ky refuses a URL starting with / under a prefixUrl, so this request fails" (shown, not joined anyway: ky never sends it). `baseUrl` resolves with `resolveKyBaseUrl`: a root-relative URL keeps only the base's origin (none for a root-relative base), a relative one replaces the base's last segment; a base whose last segment can't be found (an env var alone) gives a hole.
  - **Not handled** (documented in `http.ts` and `url.ts`): `require("ky")` and `ky-universal`, `ky.query` (no `QUERY` in `HttpMethod`), a `Request` object as input, `hooks.beforeRequest`, `searchParams`, an absolute URL under a `prefix`/`prefixUrl` (read as itself; ky glues it on), ky 1 not trimming a prefix's doubled slashes, and `.`/`..`/`?`/`#` at a relative URL's start under `baseUrl` (a hole). Which ky major a repo installs isn't read: each option is joined as the major that has it.
- **Alternatives rejected:** reading the installed major from `package.json` to decide one join (each option exists in only one major, so the option already says it); treating ky 1's leading-slash throw as a working request (a guess about a call that fails).
- **Consequences:** `HttpClient` gains `"ky"`; `BaseJoin` names the four joins. New request facts for unchanged files, so `KEY_FORMAT` 17 → 18. Fixture `ky-client`. No eval repo uses ky, so the eval doesn't move.

## ADR-0208: Held-out batch 4 is drawn by a committed rule: whole search pools, a seeded order, no skipping
- **Status:** accepted (2026-11-04, M5C.5a1, #18)
- **Context:** M5C ends with one fresh blind sample (Abhinav on #18), and its recall is the generalization claim for the whole coverage plan. Batches 1–3 were found by browsing `gh search repos` results and picking repos that looked like a fit, so a reader could (even without meaning to) pass over repos that look hard. A sample only says something about "React/Next + Express/Next apps" if nobody chose which ones.
- **Options:**
  1. Browse and pick, as for batches 1–3. Fast, but the picks are the reader's.
  2. Take the top N of a search sorted by stars. Repeatable, but biased toward large showcase repos, and "best match" order isn't stable.
  3. **Commit the rule first, then draw from each query's whole pool in a seeded order, and record every candidate read.** Chosen.
